#7400. AI Shopping Errors: What To Fix To Help Shoppers Choose Your Products

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Explore the future of retail with AI shopping. Understand how AI affects product visibility and decision-making for consumers.

This post was sponsored by Writesonic. The opinions expressed in this article are the sponsor’s own.

When shoppers ask ChatGPT or Google what to buy, answers often come back as product cards with prices, ratings, and “buy now” links. AI writes those cards from data spread across the web, but few brands know how those cards position their products.

That’s a risky blind spot to have heading into the holidays. Adobe forecasts AI traffic to US retail sites will grow 130% this holiday season.

AI shopping results usually go wrong in three ways. Your product doesn’t show up, it shows up with the wrong details, or a competitor or reseller gets the final click instead of you. The good news is that these errors trace back to product data you control.

This guide covers what these results look like, why they go wrong, and how to audit your own products.

AI shopping results are product recommendations that appear inside an AI answer. They’re usually triggered by a specific buying question, like “waterproof hiking boots for wide feet under $150.”

In March 2026, OpenAI expanded its Agentic Commerce Protocol (ACP) to cover product discovery. Merchants now share product feeds through ACP so their catalogs appear in ChatGPT.

Retailers like Target, Best Buy, and Wayfair are already integrated. Shopify merchants flow in through Shopify Catalog.

OpenAI dropped in-chat Instant Checkout in March 2026 and now lets merchants run checkout on their own sites. ChatGPT helps you get the shopper, but your product page still has to close the sale.

AI Overviews now show product cards for many shopping searches, drawing on Google’s Shopping Graph. Merchants add their products mainly by uploading feeds to Google Merchant Center. Google says the Shopping Graph holds over 50 billion product listings.

AI Mode and Gemini use the same Shopping Graph, and Google is rolling out agentic checkout there too. In this scenario, Google’s agent buys a tracked item with Google Pay once the shopper confirms. To qualify, merchants need a Merchant Center account and a Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) integration.

The most common AI shopping issues fall into three groups.

Most brands find these issues by accident. Someone runs a prompt, sees a wrong price, and fixes that one product. Hundreds of other prompts go unchecked.

The common thread is consistency. When your feed, product page, and retailer listings disagree on price, stock, or naming, AI has to pick one. It doesn’t always pick right.

An analysis reported by Search Engine Journal tracked ChatGPT product picks that came from integrated feeds. In one day in July 2026, their share went from 8.26% to 61.54%. Of 517 tracked stores with visibility swings of 33% or more, 450 saw drops.

Merchant variety shrank too. The top 10 merchants’ share of references grew from 22.5% to 41.8%. Stores without a connected feed now risk losing picks they used to win.

Your product also lives on Amazon, Walmart, Target, and other retailers. Each listing can show a different price, title, or stock status. AI may link to whichever one looks most complete.

Product schema, plus identifiers like Global Trade Item Number (GTIN), manufacturer part number (MPN), brand, and variant, tells AI exactly which item it’s looking at. Without them, AI can mix up models, sizes, and colors.

AI leans hard on what third-party websites say about your brand and products. Writesonic’s June 2026 study of 2,000+ brands found YouTube or Reddit mentions made a brand about three times more likely to show up in AI answers. Mentions on its own site raised that just 1.3 times.

Manual audits break down fast. Each check is only a snapshot, and AI answers change constantly.

Results vary by platform, region, and day, so rerun the same prompts over time. Daily checks give you the most accurate picture. Audit at the category level too, because a brand can dominate headphones and be invisible in speakers. How do you do this? A strong AI Visibility platform will help you collect, understand and action this data.

Writesonic’s study of 631,999 prompt runs across seven AI platforms found the top-ranked brand in ChatGPT rotated on 52% of prompts when the same prompt was rerun.

Writesonic’s AI Shopping Tracker automates those checks daily across ChatGPT and Google AI Overviews. It also shows how often your prompts trigger shopping results at all, which manual audits miss.

It maps directly to the problems covered above:

Any tracker only sees the prompts you give it, so the prompt set from your audit is the foundation. Start with your best sellers and the categories you can’t afford to lose this season.

The brands that check what AI says about their products, and fix the data behind it, will own these product cards. Everyone else is letting a model decide how their products are shown.

#7396. Weird animals win on Instagram. Mongabay’s next-best choice? Its reporters.

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“People are sick and tired of doomer content, and they also don’t like ‘climate stories.’”

Mongabay’s most successful videos are about weird animals.

But not every video from the environmental newsroom can be about strange — or strangely cute — animals. Some have to be about shrimp farm pollution. Or a deforestation crisis brought on by cacao farming in Liberia. That’s where the challenge comes in, Mongabay video managing editor Lucia Torres has found. Turning that kind of journalism into something people stop scrolling for isn’t easy.

“Generally mass audiences are less interested in environmental content on YouTube [and] Instagram,” Torres said. “We have to compete with recipes, music videos, etc.”

Mongabay publishes around 150 to 200 videos a year with a team of four full-time and one half-time producers. These include short vertical videos on YouTube Shorts and Instagram; evergreen explainers around “complex enviro-problems”; twice-monthly features; and lengthy investigations or short documentaries.

To reach new audiences with its environmental journalism, Mongabay started experimenting with behind-the-scenes reels in late 2024. At the time, Mongabay’s Instagram reels averaged 2,000 to 3,000 views. Torres said she was inspired by behind-the-scenes videos from National Geographic, The New York Times, ProPublica, and contributing creator Izzy Sasada. An early behind-the-scenes video — on pollution from those shrimp farms — drew about 14,000 views. Additional videos from California and Indonesia each got about 6,000 views. Mongabay started to dive deeper with videos that featured sources and reporters filming from the field.

A post shared by Mongabay | mongabay.com (@mongabay)

From the initial tests, it became clear to Torres that the best-performing videos were ones with behind-the-scenes footage from investigations or collaborations. Putting reporters on camera — in the field or at their desks — worked.

“The content [on platforms like Instagram] is dominated now by individual content creators,” said Torres, who is currently a Knight Science Journalism Fellow at MIT. She said Mongabay is “still figuring out how we can create personalities or creators within the organization that represent what Mongabay does — that are good journalists but also good at talking in front of the camera.”

A post shared by Mongabay | mongabay.com (@mongabay)

Another successful example came with Mongabay’s investigation into vanishing forests in Liberia. The piece ran as a long-form article, a lengthy YouTube video, and a behind-the-scenes short.

A post shared by Mongabay | mongabay.com (@mongabay)

Mongabay now plans its multimedia packages for big investigative stories from the beginning. “They require cross-team, high-level collaborations,” Torres said. “You really have to think about all the elements you are going to use before starting production so when you’re in the field you get everything you need.”

Not everything has panned out. Last year, Mongabay tried a data visualization series on climate data. It didn’t work. The lessons for the newsroom went beyond video.

“People are sick and tired of doomer content, and they also don’t like ‘climate stories,’” Torres said. “The simple mention of ‘climate’ scares them away.”

CEO and founder Rhett Butler has seen the same thing across Mongabay’s journalism.

“We’re seeing strong evidence that doomer stories are resonating less with our audiences,” he said. “Climate stories — especially those focused on disasters or the climate emergency — tend to garner less interest than other topics.” The ones that do well, he added, have “an ecosystem or wildlife angle, give readers a sense of agency, or present a solution.”

Mongabay has responded by introducing more projects and series focused on solutions journalism, like Indigenous-led conservation, agroecology, and conservation technology.

RELATED ARTICLEAudience editors offer advice for “dispiriting” times in social and searchSarah ScireJanuary 16, 2025“We’ve also been sharing data with the newsroom about which kinds of framing are reaching more people right now, so writers and editors can consider whether they might want to incorporate how people are responding to problems into their stories,” Butler said. “But we aren’t overly prescriptive, so ultimately it’s up to editors and writers how they use this information.”

Looking at the 3,422 articles published between June 1, 2025 and August 31, 2026, Butler found that the median story featuring hope or a solution got 41% more traffic than articles that only described the crisis or threat. (Articles classified as being about an “action confronting a threat” got 43% more.)

Today, 71% of the 100 most-read stories on Mongabay’s English-language site can be classified as “hope / solution / agency” or “action confronting a threat,” Butler said. Only 21% were “doom only.”

The video team has done something similar. The environmental newsroom’s short series Stranger Creatures leads with “the wonder of wild creatures first,” Torres noted. The conservation or endangered species angle follows.

“We’ve stopped framing stories as a climate problem, and started framing stories from a solutions-oriented, action-driven perspective, community-first, or fun-stuff first,” Torres said. “Climate is implicit now in the stories.”

What video won’t be, at least for now, is a revenue generator. Almost all of Mongabay’s revenue comes from contributions and grants, according to its annual report. (Events, licensing, and advertising “are insignificant at this point,” Butler said.)

“I’m not bullish on video becoming a material source of revenue for Mongabay, given how the space has evolved and our position in it,” Butler said. “Ten years ago I was a lot more optimistic on that front.”

Part of the problem, he said, is incentives. “Building a video strategy around advertising creates an incentive to prioritize views, which may not align with our broader organizational emphasis on impact,” Butler said. “The stories that attract the largest audiences aren’t necessarily those most likely to inform decisions or prompt action.”

#7394. Don’t Let A Tool Lead Your AI Search Strategy – Own Your Data Instead via @sejournal, @TaylorDanRW

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Keep your AI search strategy independent of unstable tools by owning your prompts and raw data, so vendor changes don't erase your measurement history.

One of the best things about the capabilities of AI is how easily it turns complex, multi-source data into dashboards anyone can understand.

Couple this with the opportunity of creating new tools and platforms for the AI search space, and you end up with this culmination of AI search tools and the need to fill a marketing channel void. The risk is that we reach for dashboards for comfort, especially when we are facing unknowns.

Marketing leadership loves the comfort of a software dashboard.

Search has always operated on a predictable, standardized framework when it comes to tools and platforms. You license an enterprise suite, import your target queries, track deterministic positions from one to one hundred and build quarterly reports that stakeholders can understand at a glance.

When generative search emerged, we naturally sought that same software-first playbook. Marketers looked for a platform to define what AI brand visibility should look like.

When you let software lead your AI strategy, you anchor your organization to an immature, volatile software market. And which tool do you choose with so many new offerings?

I researched Product Hunt, G2, and LinkedIn, and found 194 tools that have launched over the past two years that focus on aiding brands to improve their AI visibility.

Looking at the AI platform landscape, the number of tools and the speed at which they are being launched creates a false illusion of category maturity. Breaking down the 194 tools from this sample across the last two to three years highlights that a little over 60% of them are lightweight bootstrap experiments, side projects, and solo founder builds. The mortality rate of a startup mindset is running between 55% and 60%. This does not include the ones I have not been able to identify and track that do not have a presence on these platforms. Every month, many tools currently switch off APIs, stop renewing domain names, or pivot into unrelated generative wrapper apps.

The main driver of this is the impact of cost. In traditional search monitoring, scraping for our own tracking purposes has been relatively stable and has only become more volatile in recent years. But pinging a search engine and parsing HTML markup costs a fraction of what it takes to fire complex multi-turn prompt matrices when you have live web browsing enabled.

Then, factor in how Google is actively working to combat this practice, meaning that you have to continue reinvesting and adjusting how your stack works to provide the same service. Inference calls, headless browsers, anti-bot bypass proxies, and context tokens all add up relentlessly, especially as your business becomes more successful and scales.

For a self-serve tool charging $100 a month, running comprehensive weekly prompt variations for a large enough sample across a client’s core product catalog, margins are non-existent. The moment an underlying model provider updates its inference pricing, tightens its rate limits, or changes its interface, these projects really show how fragile they are and break down economically and operationally.

See also: AI Visibility Isn’t A Sham – Most Ways Of Measuring It Are

Another trap with allowing a tool to lead your AI strategy is approaching it the same way you would a vendor. Tools often come with multi-year or multi-term contracts. Once that contract is up, or if a better offer comes along from another provider, you will want to move away. It is often assumed that you can just export the data and migrate to the next platform.

We know from traditional SEO tooling that everything is measured slightly differently. While they have become relatively homogenized over the years, this is still a very immature marketplace. There is no standard or uniform underlying measurement of what AI visibility actually means. So when we swap one AI visibility tool for another, it does not mean that we are swapping like for like. It means we are swapping divergent philosophies of measurement and meaning.

Because there is no universally accepted standard for measuring AI and conversational output, every tool invents its own proprietary synthetic index that it also markets as being its main differentiator and USP.

See also: The AI Search Metrics I Use To Track Client Pipelines

From my experience and industry sentiment across social media, there is substantial executive pressure to track AI, meaning these tools are filling an inherent need created within organizations. They will generate an initial audit, take a screenshot of a brand mention for a board deck, and then face the daily reality of stochastic noise. Sources cited inside generative responses can suffer huge amounts of citation drift with high levels of variance.

This occurs without any changes made to the brand’s website or competitors. It is simply the model experiencing variation. Approaching this the same way as a rank tracker means you would spend hours investigating phantom drops and random gains caused by inference temperature shifts or model hallucinations.

Letting a tool dictate the metrics inevitably forces teams and management to track tool idiosyncrasies rather than business fundamentals.

See also: Your AI Visibility Tracker Is Quietly Breaking Your Analytics And Your Strategy

Software cannot be the anchor of your AI search strategy. Strategic stability comes from decoupling your execution from specific vendors. This is easier said than done, and third-party tools do offer an easier path without needing to engineer your own solutions. However, there are core elements you need to take ownership of.

You need to own the query matrix internally and not let a third-party platform decide which queries matter. You need to form your own internal stacks of prompts based on customer intent and categories, high-value comparative queries, and category-defining questions. You need to store this query set in internal documentation belonging to your organization, independent of any external subscription.

You also need to own experimental prompt stacks, brand prompt stacks, and other core elements so that you can track variation across a set and start to measure your own citation drift rates. This is not the same approach as traditional rank tracking.

Try to find external platforms that offer raw webhook exports, BigQuery connectors, or standardized CSVs. You want to extract all response text, timestamp data, source URLs, and prompt inputs directly into your own data infrastructure. If your vendor decides to change its calculation logic, your historical raw observations will remain intact.

Importantly, you need to recognize visibility as an outcome and not an input. A tracking dashboard does not generate any authority.

LLMs do not reference brands because the company bought visibility software. In my experience, they reference brands that possess dense authoritative entity definitions, unambiguous architectures and schemas and active digital footprints.

The software landscape for conversational and generative search will take years to reach maturity. Consolidations will accelerate, venture capital subsidies will dry up, and the vast majority of tools will vanish. Do not construct your executive roadmap around the features of an unstable tool category.

#7393. MedAmerica: Instructional Designer

Remote, Nationwide – Seeking Instructional Designer

Everybody Has A Role To Play In Transforming Healthcare

At Vituity you are part of a larger team that is driven by our purpose to improve lives. We are dedicated to transforming healthcare through our culture by working together to tackle healthcare’s most pressing challenges from the inside.

Join the Vituity Team. At Vituity we’ve cultivated an environment where passion thrives, and success comes through shared purpose. We were founded in a culture that values team accomplishments more than individual achievements, an approach we call “culture of brilliance.” Together, we leverage our strengths and experiences to make a positive impact in our local communities. We foster this through shared goals and helping our colleagues succeed, and we also understand the importance of recognition, taking the time to show appreciation and gratitude for a job well done.

#7391. The Onion’s new plan to market its print newspaper: sponsoring a bowl game

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

College football already has an Orange Bowl, a Peach Bowl, and a Citrus Bowl, so on its face, there’s nothing outlandish about the concept of an Onion Bowl.

But this matchup wouldn’t be titled to evoke some locale’s bumper crop, à la the Famous Idaho Potato Bowl. Instead it would market “America’s Finest News Source,” The Onion. (Today’s top headlines: Bain Capital Acquires Tibetan Buddhism For $35 Million; Abdul El-Sayed Makes History As First Physically Healthy Senator; Chuck Schumer Delivers Victory Speech To Previously Enthusiastic Crowd.)

On Thursday morning, Onion CEO (and past Nieman Lab contributor) Ben Collins announced the publication’s quest to buy naming rights for a bowl game, both on Bluesky and on the podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out:

The Onion is extremely close to buying an honest-to-goodness, Division 1 college football bowl series bowl game.

Yes, we're trying to pull off The Onion Bowl.

A few days before Christmas, potentially in Texas.

But we've already been banned once. And we need your help.

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:32 AM

For those unfamiliar, American college football seasons traditionally end with a few dozen bowl games, scattered around the country (and occasionally outside it). Nearly all of these games sell naming rights to one company or another, leading to fun combinations like the Poulan Weed Eater Independence Bowl, the TaxSlayer.com Gator Bowl, and the Union Home Mortgage Gasparilla Bowl. Collins wants to buy the rights to one of the several bowl games that are still without a sponsor for their games next month.

This absurdity — though no more absurd, I suppose, than the Snoop Dogg Arizona Bowl presented by Gin & Juice by Dre and Snoop — serves as the excellent marketing hook to get people to buy a $75/year print subscription, a major driver of The Onion’s revenue.

Indeed, only three U.S. newspapers have a daily print circulation over 100,000 — The Wall Street Journal, The New York Times, and the New York Post; The Washington Post and USA Today have already fallen below that line. (At the risk of being pedantic, though, The Onion appears in print monthly, not daily, making it a bit of an apples-to-oranges comparison. Also, The Onion gets to make up its stories.)

Collins says The Onion actually got quite close to buying the naming rights to the New Orleans Bowl, which last year lost its longtime naming sponsor, the…Ohio-based freight-shipping concern R+L Carriers? The Onion was prepared to pay “mid six figures.” But the organization that runs the bowl, the Greater New Orleans Sports Foundation, apparently said no deep into the process, a move Collins attributed to “politics.” (I reached out to the Greater New Orleans Sports Foundation for comment — a sentence I did not expect to type today — but did not hear back.)

So the guys who sell bowl games ushered us to what we thought was a pretty good fit: The New Orleans Bowl.

I love New Orleans. I love having crawfish out of the back of a pickup truck at 3 a.m. The game would've been in the Superdome two days before Christmas. Good fit! Right? Right???

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:38 AM

Then the folks who run the New Orleans Bowl, a commission that includes representatives of the city and state of Louisiana, found out our plans to highlight our mascot:

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:39 AM

Despite getting all approvals from ESPN and scraping together the cash, right as we were about to sign the paper work to put The Onion Sports Bowl in New Orleans, their state-funded commission called us and said it straight up: "No."

We asked why. They said politics. And the Sickos Guy.

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:40 AM

While the New Orleans Bowl might be out, several currently-brand-free possibilities remain, including the Frisco Bowl in newly blue Texas — the state also home to The Onion’s rival to the death, Alex Jones.

#7389. With “Bimbo University,” Nikita Redkar leans into femininity to explain the news

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, ‘OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.’”

The data is in: News creators and influencers are a major source of news for Americans, especially people under 30. This is the latest edition of Creators of Record, an occasional series of interviews with popular creators about how they do their jobs.

For Nikita Redkar, a big part of filming a video is putting together an outfit.

In a July video about youth-led protests in India, the 33-year-old is walking around New York City explaining how movements grow when people with opposing views come together on the things they do agree on. She’s wearing a white tube top and black jeans with sunglasses holding her hair back, along with a matching gold necklace and earrings that look like soda can tabs. You can’t see her shoes, but they clack enough for you to know she’s wearing a heel.

In another about comedian Shane Gillis’s Saturday Night Live monologue in which he called women “whores” for wearing shorts to the gym, she’s seated on the floor of her living room wearing a black silk velvet top with a pink faux-fur collar and sleeve cuffs. In the style of a Get Ready With Me video, she explains how 2000s comedy and pop culture entrenched misogynistic ideas of gender roles while applying her makeup.

A full-time news creator who mostly focuses on American politics and culture through a leftist and feminist lens, Redkar calls herself a “professor at Bimbo University.” The girliness is the point, she told me.

“I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice these parts of yourself to be taken seriously,” Redkar said. “You should be taken seriously no matter what.”

Redkar and I chatted in October about how she does her job, leaning into her femininity to explain the news, and what her relationship with social media is like. Our conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Hanaa’ Tameez: How did you start creating news videos? Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics. In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent. I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Nikita Redkar: I started in 2022, kind of accidentally. I did standup comedy for five years before the pandemic. I was always doing political humor. I took many political science classes in college, I wrote for the college newspaper, and I always had an interest in politics.

In 2022, I had a video that really took off — like millions of views within a few hours. I was explaining the Russian invasion of Ukraine with a silly metaphor about Russia being a jealous ex that doesn’t want to see his ex thrive and be independent.

I tried that formula again a couple weeks later…about NATO and fears of World War III. I explained exactly what would be affected. I said there’s not going to be a world war, but things are going to get really expensive, which happened. I also used a girly friendship metaphor for that video. That’s when I started doing that formula — leaning into hyper-femininity to explain these complex geopolitical topics.

Tameez: Tell me about why this work is important to you. Redkar: I feel like my work has made a lot of these daunting topics a little bit more accessible. I incorporate a lot of humor, which is important because there’s a lot of doom and gloom on social media. It’s a normal reaction to have toward everything that’s going on, and I feel it myself, too. But when I talk to my audience, I intentionally give them hope. I don’t just tell the news, but tell them, OK, if we really locked in and held these corporations and the government accountable and we were intentional in our activism, this is what the world could look like. It’s important to make information accessible because we are going through so much news-wise and not all of it is easy to understand. I enjoy bringing this information I’m naturally interested in to people in a way where everyone [can] get involved. When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

Redkar: I feel like my work has made a lot of these daunting topics a little bit more accessible. I incorporate a lot of humor, which is important because there’s a lot of doom and gloom on social media. It’s a normal reaction to have toward everything that’s going on, and I feel it myself, too. But when I talk to my audience, I intentionally give them hope. I don’t just tell the news, but tell them, OK, if we really locked in and held these corporations and the government accountable and we were intentional in our activism, this is what the world could look like.

It’s important to make information accessible because we are going through so much news-wise and not all of it is easy to understand. I enjoy bringing this information I’m naturally interested in to people in a way where everyone [can] get involved.

When I was starting, I was not [a stereotypical] history buff or a news junkie. I would like to think that someone who looks like me and talks like me should be just as interested in politics and just as interested in the outcome of this country.

Tameez: Tell me more about that. You have a little bit of an online persona. Redkar: That was the whole reason I created Bimbo University. I wanted to get more women on board with being interested in politics and history in particular, because history is seen as such a male-dominated [field]. It was really important to me to bring, colloquially speaking, the girls and the gays and the theys into the space, because it is fascinating and it provides context to what we’re going through right now. It makes the present a little less daunting when you study historical cycles and realize that people 100 years ago dealt with [similar things to what we’re going through now]. How did they come out of that?

It was really important to me to bring, colloquially speaking, the girls and the gays and the theys into the space, because it is fascinating and it provides context to what we’re going through right now. It makes the present a little less daunting when you study historical cycles and realize that people 100 years ago dealt with [similar things to what we’re going through now]. How did they come out of that?

Tameez: How do you define your niche and how did you get there? Redkar: My niche is educational. Bimbo University is a place where anyone can start no matter where they are [in their political education]. My audience [ranges from] people who are just getting into politics to people who maybe were triggered by a specific event in the news to start paying more attention to it. I feel like leftist content creators all operate on a spectrum. You have people who are getting really deep into Marxist theory, for example, and things like geopolitics. I’m at the beginning. I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, “OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.”

Redkar: My niche is educational. Bimbo University is a place where anyone can start no matter where they are [in their political education]. My audience [ranges from] people who are just getting into politics to people who maybe were triggered by a specific event in the news to start paying more attention to it.

I feel like leftist content creators all operate on a spectrum. You have people who are getting really deep into Marxist theory, for example, and things like geopolitics. I’m at the beginning. I’m the person who’s holding the door open and being like, “OK, you just got here, this is what we’re talking about right now.”

Tameez: You’re the first news creator I’ve talked to who does Get Ready With Me videos. You take all of your interests and apply them [to your videos] to be relatable. Redkar: I got into this huge argument with my best friend’s mom, who was a second-wave feminist in the 70s and 80s. She was upset that I called my page Bimbo University. She was like, “You’re dumbing yourself down. Women shouldn’t dumb themselves down. You are just as smart as a man, if not smarter than a man.” That was my lightbulb moment of, oh, your feminism has always been to measure your worth as equal to a man and by the standards of men…working toward a man’s acceptance. In some industries, you’ve got to do that. I don’t blame [people] for thinking like that. But the reason I dial so deep into this hyper-femininity — leaning into these girly things like getting ready with me or walking with my outfit showing — is because I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice those parts of yourself to be taken seriously. You should be taken seriously no matter what.

Redkar: I got into this huge argument with my best friend’s mom, who was a second-wave feminist in the 70s and 80s.

She was upset that I called my page Bimbo University. She was like, “You’re dumbing yourself down. Women shouldn’t dumb themselves down. You are just as smart as a man, if not smarter than a man.”

That was my lightbulb moment of, oh, your feminism has always been to measure your worth as equal to a man and by the standards of men…working toward a man’s acceptance. In some industries, you’ve got to do that. I don’t blame [people] for thinking like that.

But the reason I dial so deep into this hyper-femininity — leaning into these girly things like getting ready with me or walking with my outfit showing — is because I want to show that you don’t have to sacrifice those parts of yourself to be taken seriously. You should be taken seriously no matter what.

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: On Mondays, I have a researcher and we go through the topics that people are talking about on social media. We pull up some news sites and see what the big stories are, and we leave some room for breaking news throughout the week.

My week is [split] between videos [on topics] that the general public is talking about, and videos that are just interesting to me. That’s how I stay sane in this business. I’m lucky to have the flexibility of doing that. My researcher combs through news sites and research papers and compiles all that information for me, then I put together a script.

Redkar: It’s definitely my favorite color, but it’s like the chicken and the egg — I don’t know if I liked it before I was doing this.

I pick whatever outfit I feel cute in. In getting ready with me videos, I start with a completely bare face, so at least the outfit has to be giving a little bit.

Tameez: Tell me about the Get Ready with Me videos. How do you decide what’s a GRWM and what’s a walking video?

Redkar: It’s like: What am I comfortable screaming out in the streets versus what do I need to keep at home? Some topics require more seriousness; if it does, I can do the GRWM for that. But if it’s something [less serious], I can be walking my dog or have on some ridiculous outfit.

Redkar: If I get a fact wrong, I’ll do a follow-up video. It’s rare that I’ve gotten a fact wrong because between me and my researcher, that’s two levels of checks before something hits the internet.

For one video, I didn’t factually get it wrong, but I should have included more of the story. It was about the Ugandan dictator Idi Amin and how Indians were expelled from Uganda, which is kind of how we got both Zohran Mamdani and Charli XCX [living in the U.S.], because both of their parents were part of the group that was expelled. I disregarded the racism that local Ugandans faced because of the Indians. I just focused on the expulsion and I framed Indians as the victims.

A lot of people in the comments were like, “Hey, like, you really should have said this other part too.” I just deleted that video, because I didn’t want this to spread and only give half of the story.

Redkar: My favorite video in recent memory was about girls on the first date, how we’re all so vigilant these days about dating men. I had so much fun doing it because I was talking about how life is affected by politics, instead of just politics. I think that video did really well because a lot of people connected to it.

Last year I did a video that I called “How we went from Brat Summer to Hitler Winter” and talked about the hedonism of the 2024 summer and the fascism that followed. I related it to the Roaring 20s transitioning to the Great Depression to the World War. I incorporated a lot of pop culture [references].

A post shared by Nikita Redkar (@nikitadumptruck)

Redkar: Constantly. I posted a video about the Cornell professor who invented the term sexual harassment. I thought it was so interesting and timely, but it did not do well at all. The problem with content creation is sometimes you can’t tell if people didn’t like a video or if you’re being shadow banned.

Redkar: I really love Liz Plank. She’s @feministabulous on Instagram. I love her coverage and how she leans into current conversations in pop culture.

I really like Charles McBryde. He’s not talking about just news — he’s very foreign policy-focused as well.

@MissEatingGood on Instagram is a great explainer of what’s going on and the historical context.

Redkar: Just finding ways to work together. I think there is so much benefit in that. I know countless creators who take a journalist’s article and dissect it down. Maybe [the journalists] should be the ones doing that.

I would happily pair up with journalists and teach them the ins and outs of content creation and how to make a short-form video article that’s also attention-grabbing. That’s just the reality; people are on social media, everyone’s attention span is shot, which sucks. We could try to fix that, but at this moment, we’re not fixing it. It’s really helpful to go on social media and talk [about your reporting] because journalists work hard on their articles.

Creators [should] take more time. We rush way too much. It’s not our fault, because these platforms reward you for making content more frequently and punish you if you take a few days off. [Not rushing] sucks, but what can you do? It’s better than putting incorrect, or rushed, or incomplete information out there.

#7388. Senior Product Designer, Developer Platform at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

Most product designers want to design for everyone. This role is for someone who'd rather design for one very specific, very opinionated person: the developer at 2am trying to figure out why their voice agent just failed a call. You'll design the tools developers use to test, evaluate and debug AI agents — a category that's still being invented, with no established patterns to copy. If dense data, real-time systems and reading a stack trace sound more fun than intimidating, keep reading.

You'll thrive as a Senior Product Designer if you:

are obsessed with making the best product for our customers

are known as the go-to person for tackling tough problems

are focused on polish, detail and quality

are a fast learner, frequently picking up new tips, tricks, and skills

The best way to impress us is with thoughtful ways you'd implement LiveKit, and potentially tinkering with it 😊

Own the design of how developers test, evaluate and observe their agents — simulations, evals, logs and agent insights

Turn complex, real-time data into interfaces that are easy to scan, understand and act on

Design new interaction patterns for AI-native workflows, where agents generate the first draft and humans adjust it

Prototype in code and work hand-in-hand with engineers to ship polished work

Contribute patterns to our design system for dense, debugging-oriented surfaces

You've shipped design work for developer tools, observability or data-heavy products

You have strong data-visualization instincts and know when a chart beats a table (and when it doesn't)

You're comfortable in code — reading PRs, tweaking Tailwind, building prototypes

You earn the trust of deeply technical users and engineers

Experience designing for AI or agent tooling

AI tools are already a core part of how you design

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

LiveKit is an equal opportunity employer and does not discriminate on the basis of any characteristic protected by applicable law. If you require a reasonable accommodation during the application or interview process, please contact recruiting@livekit.io.

#7387. Patchstack: Senior Product Manager

Patchstack protects WordPress sites for hosts, agencies and developers, and we're now launching a security product for people building JavaScript apps with AI tools like Claude Code. Learn how to submit your website to ChatGPT through Business Hub, verify your business, and understand what claiming changes for SEO and brand visibility.

OpenAI introduced the long-awaited Businesses in ChatGPT feature in beta on November 10, 2026. You can submit your website’s business information through Business Hub to claim or create a profile and provide details about your company.

I’m excited about this, and I’m sure other digital marketers, not just SEOs, are too and after submitting Search Engine Journal to ChatGPT, I decided to share my firsthand experience with SEJ’s readers.

If you choose the physical-location option to create a new business, you may see a waitlist. That happened in my test. The website option let me continue with Search Engine Journal.

Existing local listings have a different process. OpenAI’s setup guide says stores and restaurants can currently claim an existing U.S. listing.

For those listings, select Business phone if it is offered, then choose SMS or an automated call to receive a code. Make sure the listed phone number is reachable. If you choose a call, do it during business hours when someone can answer and an auto-responder won’t prevent the verification call from reaching you.

Unlike Google Business Profile, ChatGPT Business Hub accepts eligible online-only businesses. Google lists “Brands, organizations, artists, and other online-only businesses” under “Ineligible businesses” in its eligibility guidelines. For SEJ, this meant I could submit the publication as a website business.

Whether submitting a profile brings more visibility is another question. OpenAI says claiming a business does not guarantee higher placement. Its business overview explains that ChatGPT still makes its own recommendations and comparisons.

In this guide, we will walk through the submission process step by step and look at how to read the insights in Business Hub.

Before you start, make sure you have access to your domain’s DNS settings and your business records. I had to verify the website domain and my identity, then provide the business details.

For SEJ, I entered Search Engine Journal as the business name and used its DBA details. DBA stands for “doing business as.” I selected Entity as the business type.

One field caused some confusion. It asked for a registration number, but the 12-1234567 placeholder looked like a U.S. Employer Identification Number, or EIN. A state registration number is different, so check which identifier the form requests for your country before entering it.

Sign in to ChatGPT, open Business Hub, and search for your business name. Select the matching business if it appears. If there is no match, look for the option to create a new profile.

In a separate physical-location test, creating a new business profile led to a waitlist. ChatGPT said it would send an email when I could continue.

For SEJ, I selected Through a website because readers access the publication online. This took me to domain verification.

ChatGPT asked me to verify SEJ’s domain by adding a TXT record to its DNS settings.

Copy ChatGPT’s TXT record type, name, and value into your domain’s DNS settings. After the update takes effect, return to ChatGPT to verify the domain. See OpenAI’s verification instructions.

This confirmed control of the domain. I still had to verify my identity and submit the business details later in the process.

Once I verified the domain, ChatGPT asked me to choose a category from a predefined list and add an optional description.

I selected Magazine publisher for SEJ. ChatGPT also suggested Online media company and News service.

The description field said ChatGPT could share these details in relevant conversations. For a publisher, this is a place to explain the topics it covers and who it writes for.

I would expect ChatGPT to use this context when matching your services with relevant questions. Write the description with SEO and GEO in mind, using the language your customers use to describe what they need.

Next, I could add a business email address and phone number. Both fields were optional.

ChatGPT then asked about delivery and returns. Those questions felt out of place for SEJ, especially after I had selected a publishing category.

The delivery choices covered no delivery, paid delivery, and free delivery. The following screen asked whether returns were unavailable, accepted with fees, or free.

These choices appear in the profile preview. Check how they describe your business before continuing, especially if the questions don’t fit what you do.

ChatGPT then saved SEJ’s profile as a draft and directed me to the business overview to continue verification or check its status.

The same screen also offered $500 in advertising credit and an upgrade to a ChatGPT Business plan. The credit has eligibility and redemption terms, so check those before planning to use it.

You can manage an approved profile without running ads or publishing a Business Agent, according to OpenAI’s setup FAQ.

As director of technology at Search Engine Journal, I submitted SEJ’s information.

OpenAI’s eligibility requirement allows either the owner or an authorized representative to manage a business profile.

I completed the personal identity check first, then entered SEJ’s business information.

Review these details before submitting, because approval automatically publishes new profiles created through Persona. Approved Persona verification also adds the blue badge, according to OpenAI’s Persona guidance.

For SEJ, verification was almost instant. I captured the review screen below before approval.

If your verification is pending, return to the business overview to check its status.

To see how often ChatGPT references your business, Business Hub provides mentions and website clicks. OpenAI’s launch announcement says these insights use aggregated data with reporting thresholds. Businesses do not receive people’s private conversations.

A mention has a specific meaning here. For an online business, ChatGPT must reference its website. Mentioning the brand name alone does not count, according to OpenAI’s insights definition.

SEJ’s mentions chart below spans October 14 to November 10, 2026. The view shows 353,356 mentions and 2,663 website clicks, with data through November 10 in UTC. Daily mentions increased by roughly 90% between the first and last day, based on the chart.

These figures become more useful alongside your website analytics. Compare the same date range to see whether changes in mentions came with changes in traffic or conversions.

Business Hub also lets you give teammates access to manage the profile. From the business overview, select Invite in the upper-right corner. The Invite business admins dialog lets you add their email address.

OpenAI’s access guidance says the admin who claimed the business can invite teammates and grant an agency access. Each person should use their own ChatGPT account. If the profile was claimed through a ChatGPT workspace, invitees may need to be members of that workspace.

This is similar to Google Business Profile’s owner and manager access. Both let several people help maintain a business profile using their own accounts. The person who submits the business does not have to handle every update.

Business Hub gives you control over the information you submit about your company. Whether that leads to more recommendations is something we still need to measure.

For a brand with inconsistent profiles, I would start by submitting the current company name and description in Business Hub. I would also check its name, address, and phone number (called NAP) across its website, business listings, and social profiles. Keep them accurate and consistent for the same business or location.

Our guide identifies local citations as a ranking factor for Google’s local search results. That consistency still matters for AI systems, which may use several sources to describe the same business. Updating Business Hub is one part of that work. I would also correct outdated details wherever they appear.

If a rebrand has left both the old and new names in circulation, explain that relationship on your site and in the business description where relevant. Keep the legal name in the verification fields that request it.

This is why I would treat Business Hub as one source of business information, rather than assume it overrides everything else ChatGPT finds. OpenAI’s profile policy says profiles can include information from other sources.

If your company owns several brands, each needs to be managed individually. Business Hub does not yet offer a central way to manage a parent company’s brands and locations. Claiming the main brand does not extend that claim to its locations, according to OpenAI’s guidance on managing brands and locations.

For brands going through a managed claim process, access to individual location profiles is restricted by default. Ask your OpenAI account contact which claiming process to follow for those locations.

Business Hub is still new, and there is more to explore beyond submitting a profile. If you sell products, the next step could be adding a feed with pricing and details using OpenAI’s file-upload guide. Agent builder is another feature to explore as it gradually rolls out to more regions. I expect these features to improve over time and OpenAI to eventually launch a Search Console-style tool for ChatGPT.

Accurate business information and websites that AI systems can access matter, and Business Hub now helps us understand how AI platforms describe our brands and send us traffic.

#7383. Meta pulls down controversial terms of service

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“We’re pausing the changes as we continue to engage with stakeholders and take our time to get this right.”

Meta has indefinitely delayed its proposed commercial terms of service, which would have permitted the company to scrape the websites of Meta Business account users.

On Thursday, the company took down the preview link to the proposed terms and published an update to its Business Help Center that reads: “We recently shared that we were updating the Meta Commercial Terms, Product Catalog Terms, Self-Serve Ad Terms, and Ad Creative Generative AI Terms. These updates are now paused and will not take effect on November 20, 2026. We are continuing to consult with stakeholders and will provide a further update when we have more to share. In the meantime, our existing terms remain in effect.”

Meta — which owns Facebook, Messenger, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads — has repeatedly pushed back the terms’ effective date since Nieman Lab first reported on them on October 15. The terms were first set to take effect on October 30, sparking global outcry among news publishers, politicians, and trade organizations. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.” On October 23, Meta moved the effective date back by three weeks to November 20.

Last week, Meta pledged to consult news publishers and industry leaders ahead of putting new commercial terms into effect.

“We’ve heard publishers’ feedback on our proposed ads and business terms update. We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders,” vice president of communications Andy Stone tweeted on October 28. “We’re committed to getting this right, and as we expect this consultation could extend beyond November 20, in which case we’d delay implementation of the updated terms.”

Since then, courts in Norway and Brazil have issued preliminary injunctions to block the terms from taking effect as a result of lawsuits filed by media organizations in both countries. The United Kingdom’s Competition and Markets Authority has opened a formal investigation into Meta, examining whether the company was “abusing a dominant position by imposing exploitative terms on publishers and advertisers.”

In the United States, the proposed terms stoked bipartisan concern. At the end of October, Sens. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) and Marsha Blackburn (R-Tenn.) sent letters to Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg asking him to withdraw the terms.

#7379. The Traffic Pool Is Shrinking, And Which Channel Fills It Matters Less Than We Think via @sejournal, @DuaneForrester

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

AI referrals versus Google is a fair comparison of two streams feeding a smaller pool, and agents that complete the purchase are where the offset starts.

Late last month the industry spent a week debating a ratio, and seems to skip the number it sits on.

Three pieces of research landed within days of each other and carried an entire conversation. Marie Haynes looked at 12 months of GA4 data across 100 sites and more than 145 million sessions, and found that ChatGPT sent 0.62% of the sessions Google organic did, rising to 0.73% with every LLM included. The SEO Works benchmarked 95 sites and found organic search delivered 39 times the traffic of AI assistants. Kevin Indig ran 632 tests across 12 sites looking for AI visibility to show up later as branded search or direct traffic, and couldn’t find a reliable relationship. All three are careful pieces of work, generous with their data and candid about their limits, and I’d trust the numbers in each.

The conclusion the wider industry drew from them traveled faster than the work itself: AI sends almost nothing, so GEO is a distraction, so get back to Google. That reading answers a reasonable question: Which channel sends more visitors today, and the answer is plainly Google. What it leaves out is the size of the pool both channels draw from, and that pool is getting smaller.

Kevin, as it happens, published the clearest evidence of that a few days later. In his Similarweb analysis of 10,000 domains in each of the U.S. and France, organic search visits fell 13.2% in the U.S., and 24.4% in France between August 2024 and August 2026, and publishers lost roughly 41.5% of their organic traffic in the U.S. over the same two years. Paid search visits grew in the same data, and Alphabet’s third-quarter filing shows paid clicks still up 8% year over year, so the open web is losing visits from search while Google’s own paid inventory keeps growing, and no ratio between two referral channels will show you that. Kevin notes that paid growth replaced only about a third of the organic visits lost in the U.S., and that AI referrals, the fastest-growing channel in his sample, still account for about one visit in 200. Nothing on the search side of that picture refills the pool. His follow-up last week points to where some businesses are rebuilding it instead: email grew about 16% in his US sample, the steadiest gain of any channel, because it reaches an audience a business already owns.

Comparing AI referrals to Google organic tells you which of two inbound streams is smaller. It cannot tell you whether the total reaching your business is still enough to sustain it, and that second question is the one that decides budgets, headcount, and whether a traffic-dependent model survives the next three years.

The visits are not moving to a new channel so much as leaving the click altogether. A preprint from Bocconi University researchers Shi, Zhu and Gu, built on Comscore desktop clickstream data from more than 45,000 U.S. households, found that ChatGPT produced an outbound click in 5.2% of conversation sessions, compared with 31.1% of Google queries, and that wider access to ChatGPT Search reduced traditional search use by 9.4%. It’s a desktop-only preprint, but the direction matches everything else in hand: activity is moving to interfaces that answer in place and send fewer people anywhere.

Google’s own results page shows the same pull. Similarweb panel data for U.S. searches in early 2026 put the clicks reaching the open web at roughly 232 for every 1,000 searches. Last week I looked at Alphabet’s third quarter, where Google reported query growth at a five-year high, by a count it hasn’t defined, alongside its slowest search revenue growth of the year. More searching and fewer visits can both be true at once, and that pairing is the shrinking pool seen from Google’s side. Google’s head of Search said last year that total organic click volume to websites was relatively stable, but that claim came without numbers, and the Q3 call offered no figure on clicks sent to websites at all.

The decline is uneven, and that is the practical point. Kevin’s data shows the typical small site roughly flat while large sites and publishers absorbed most of the loss. The number to watch is your own pool: total inbound from every source, month over month, set against impressions for your category queries, so you can see whether demand is still there while the visits fall away. Nothing about tracking the pool needs a new vendor, though, and you can chart it this afternoon from your own analytics..

I argued recently that being indexed now earns a place in an answer rather than a visit. The shrinking pool is what that shift looks like in your reporting. Past a certain point, no mix of sources refills it, and the useful question changes from where your visits come from to whether the visit was ever the thing you needed. For most businesses it wasn’t. The visit was a step on the way to a booking, a purchase, or a signed form, and if that step is disappearing, the work moves to whatever completes the transaction without it.

Marie’s piece points exactly here. Alongside her data, she encouraged readers to spend their time preparing for the agentic web, naming UCP, Universal Cart, WebMCP, and intelligent eyewear, and I agree with her. Google is pointed the same way: on its Q3 earnings call, Sundar Pichai said agents will operate as a unifying layer across Google’s products, booking reservations, filling in forms, scheduling appointments, and making purchases. Agents acting on a searcher’s behalf are the most credible offset to a shrinking pool, not because they send traffic, but because they finish the job the traffic used to exist for. That changes the unit you are working toward, from the visit to the completed action, meaning the booking, order, or request an agent finalizes on someone’s behalf.

The clearest evidence of how agents choose comes from a McGill study by Davood Wadi and Yu Ma. They randomized the order of 100 hotel listings across 5,000 agent sessions on four large language models and compared the results with human data from Expedia. The agents searched more deeply than people and never once declined to book. Position still affected which listings they opened, but weakly and in an unexpected shape: the middle of the page was inspected least, not the bottom. Rank carried through to the final booking for only two of the four models. And every model converged on the same listing, the one nothing else beat on review score at its price, which took 78.2% of all bookings. The authors’ own conclusion is that for agentic search, the attributes displayed on a results page matter more than placement within it.

The same researchers found a second effect worth knowing before you assume agents are neutral shoppers. In a companion study, agents told they worked for a booking platform chose sponsored listings at roughly nine times the rate of agents told they worked for the traveler, 25% against 2.8%, with every other attribute matched. Whose agent it is shapes what it buys, and on the same Q3 call Google’s chief business officer said he’s excited about the advertising opportunities in an agentic world. A strong offer remains the price of entry everywhere, but on agents a platform deploys, sponsorship can still outrank it, so it pays to know which agents your buyers are actually using.

An AI agent does not need to find your business first. It needs to find nothing that beats your business on the attributes it can read, because agents converge on the undominated offer regardless of where that offer sits in a list.

That is also why the plumbing alone won’t carry anyone. A checkout protocol makes it easier to buy from you once an agent has decided to, and does nothing to make it decide. OpenAI ran a version of this experiment in public: It launched Instant Checkout inside ChatGPT in September 2025 and pulled back from it in March 2026, moving purchases back to merchants’ own sites and apps. The infrastructure matters, and it comes second.

Here is where that infrastructure stands today. Google’s Universal Cart launched in the U.S. on Oct. 15, with checkout running on the Universal Commerce Protocol, and on the Q3 call Google said UCP now enables direct checkout for hundreds of thousands of brands. That count almost certainly includes merchants enrolled through commerce platforms such as Shopify, because a public scan in May found 26 sites publishing a UCP profile of their own across more than 3 million. Both numbers can be true, and the gap is telling: If you sell through a platform, you may already be eligible without building anything, and if you don’t, very few competitors have built it themselves either. WebMCP, which lets a page expose actions such as booking or checking availability as tools an agent can call directly, is still an origin trial in Chrome, and Marie herself notes it may be early for most businesses to invest heavily there. Meanwhile, Cloudflare’s new defaults block agent traffic on ad-supported pages for new domains and for Free-tier sites that never changed their settings, so plenty of businesses are turning agents away without knowing it.

Earlier this fall, I declined to publish a checklist for measuring what AI does to your numbers, because there was no ground truth to score it against. This list is a different kind of thing. Every item is checkable, since an agent can either read your offer, trust it, and complete an action with you, or it can’t. The order runs offer first and plumbing last, with an honest maturity label on each.

None of this brings back the old volume, and it isn’t meant to. The businesses that come through a shrinking traffic pool will be the ones an agent can read, trust, and complete a transaction with, and that work starts with the offer rather than the protocol.

The debate over whether AI sends enough traffic has a fair answer: It doesn’t, and it probably won’t. The better use of a week of argument is to ask how much of your business still depends on the visit at all.

Salon, one of the first digital-native news outlets, closed this week after 31 years. Which one of these journalists did not work at Salon at some point?

“Salon was never just a website. It was an idea about what journalism could be, born at a moment when the internet was still a largely unexplored frontier,” the site wrote anonymously at its passing. Its last owner describes its core business as “Leading Performance Marketing in Crypto.”

The AI lab Anthropic — the makers of Claude — recently signed its first licensing deal with a news publisher. Which publisher?

The deal was for real-time election results for Tuesday’s U.S. midterm elections.

Harassment by the Kremlin has forced Russia’s last major independent newspaper to close. Its editor won the Nobel Peace Prize in 2021. What is the newspaper’s name?

“On November 9, we will ​turn off the lights in the home where we have lived for 30 years, shut down our servers, and leave,” the paper said. “The newspaper no longer ​exists. The Russian authorities and the Russian security services have forced our editorial team to cease its operations.”

The New York Times brought back one of its most famous data visualizations, which debuted in 2016. What’s its name?

The Needle scarred a generation of politics junkies with its constant jittering — and by giving Hillary Clinton an 82% chance of victory well into 2016’s election night.

If all goes according to plan, which important American vegetable crop could soon be reflected in the name of a college football game?

The satirical outlet The Onion has its eye on a bowl sponsorship deal — or at least on using it as a clever way to push subscriptions to its monthly print edition.

A major TV network had disappointing ratings on Election Night, averaging only 2.8 million viewers in prime time. Which one?

The Bari Weiss-led network news operation finished well behind competitors NBC (6.7 million), ABC (5.2 million), Fox News (4.3 million), CNN (4.1 million), and MS NOW (3.7 million).

Which of these words does Wikipedia warn editors to watch out for, because its presence suggests an article might have been written by AI?

“Tapestry” was especially popular with GPT-4o. More recent AI models are more likely to use “quietly,” “leverage,” “challenge,” and “multifaceted.”

People Inc. — the media conglomerate that includes magazines like People, Food & Wine, and Entertainment Weekly — has lost what percentage of its Google search traffic over the past two years?

“We have already lost 70% of our referrals, and AI is on 90% of searches,” CEO Neil Vogel said on an earnings call.

What do owners of electric vehicles consume about 21% less of than the average American?

EV drivers spend roughly twice as much time with podcasts as other drivers, as well as more time with satellite radio and streaming music services.

Fox News’ ratings have declined in recent months. What two-word phrase did CEO Lachlan Murdoch offer as a reason for the drop?

Murdoch argued on an earnings call that Fox News viewers “are not going to any other news channel. They’re not switching to MS NOW or to CNN. They’re just, you know, fatigued and watching…slightly less television overall.”

#7376. FIRY: Head of Product - Beamable

Beamable is the backend and LiveOps infrastructure platform for game developers, the system that lets studios build, operate, and scale live games without constructing the underlying architecture themselves. Backend services, identity, analytics, meta-game systems, virtual economies, and AI-native tooling: all integrated into a single platform that eliminates the point-solution stitching that has defined game development for too long. Beamable also powers Wingman, an agentic LiveOps product that gives game teams a unified operating system to understand, optimize, and act on live game performance in real time. This is the future of how games are operated.

Beamable is part of FIRY, a founder-led global technology portfolio operating at the intersection of gaming, AI-powered performance marketing, and developer infrastructure. Acquired in January 2026, and together with Skillz, our competition platform, and RZR, our AI-driven ad network, Beamable sits at the foundation of a compounding ecosystem that no standalone competitor can replicate. If you want to build the infrastructure that games run on at a company with the vision and resources to back it — Beamable is the right place to do it.

Beamable is seeking a Head of Product to shape its platform vision and lead its next phase of growth in the game backend market. This is an opportunity to build the product function from the ground up, guide the platform from early access through public release, and advance AI integration to deliver meaningful value for game studios.

As the company’s senior product leader and sole product practitioner, you will own product strategy, roadmap, launch milestones, and success metrics, combining strategic direction with hands-on execution. You will work directly with studios and design partners, represent Beamable’s product vision at industry events, and translate customer insights into clear priorities. Success in this role means delivering a successful public launch, developing early-access studios into reference customers, and establishing a product foundation that drives adoption and sustained revenue growth.

Total Starting Compensation including Base + Bonus + Equity: $212,625 + Bonus + Equity

Beamable embraces diversity and is proud to be an equal opportunity employer. As part of our commitment to diversifying our workforce, we do not discriminate on the basis of age, race, sex, gender, gender identity, color, religion, national origin, sexual orientation, marital status, citizenship, veteran status, or disability status, and we operate in compliance with applicable fair chance ordinances.

#7375. Product Designer at Clerk

Clerk is on a mission to solve user management once and for all. We are a globally distributed team dedicated to providing best-in-class developer experience, with obsessive attention to every detail. Today, we provide developers with full-stack React components and hooks like , , , useUser, and useOrganization. These APIs allow developers to build hard-to-get-right user and organization management flows. We believe that a component is worth a thousand APIs.

Our Dashboard is a primary tool for configuring our components, as well as a centralized place for managing your app’s users.

You’ll join Visual Designers, UI Engineers, and fellow Product Designers on the Clerk Design team to build our core product, the Clerk Dashboard. You’ll always keep developer experience first as you gain a deeper understanding of the business needs of user management. You’ll optimize for customer delight, flexibility, and ease-of-use within a complicated, technical domain.

Collaborate with teammates to design new features. You’ll work closely with Product, Design, and Engineering across project lifecycles from ideation to implementation.

Gather requirements. You’ll research the technology and customer impetus behind the features we release. You’ll attain a deep understanding of the problems we solve.

Structure and prototype. You’ll create flows and interactions that make sense of complex information, whether it’s a static component or a multi-page view.

Not settle for less than pixel-perfection. You’ll work with a world-class UI Engineering team to bring your Figma frames to life. You set the bar for quality.

Elevate our design culture. You’ll support and expand the Clerk Design visual language, component library, accessibility practices, design system, and design processes.

You’re a Senior(+) level Product Designer with experience working in authentication, user management, or developer tools. You love your craft. You push Engineers to build experiences they’re unsure if they’re capable of (and you expect them to do the same for you).

You’re in the Americas. The Clerk Design team is spread across the globe; Product Design currently spans UTC+1 to UTC+5:30. You live in a location that is ±3 hours within U.S. Mountain Time (MDT, UTC−06:00).

You have technical fluency. You have a deep understanding of designing interactive software on the web. You don’t need to author production-ready front-end code, but it’s a big plus if you can.

You work iteratively. Your process embodies a “ship and iterate” mentality. You value the quick pace of a growing startup like Clerk.

You own your time. You’re a self-starter who would rather find a tough problem to solve instead of waiting for a new task. To be successful at Clerk, you need to make the most of the autonomy you’re given.

💰 Competitive Salary – We want you to know that we value the skills and experience you bring to the table. We go out of our way to make sure that you feel fairly compensated.

📈 Equity Ownership – At Clerk, we believe in shared success. That's why we offer a stock option plan so that everyone can benefit from the growth and prosperity of the company.

⚕️ Health Coverage – We care about your well-being. That's why we offer top-tier health insurance to ensure that your health needs are fully met.

🎧 Work Gear - Set up your ideal home office with the gear of your choice. At Clerk, we want to ensure that you have everything you need to perform at your best.

🏖️ Flexible Vacation Policy – We believe in work-life balance and trust you to take the time you need. Although we recommend 25 days per year, our vacation policy is unlimited. This is in addition to observing national holidays specific to your country of residence.

🌍 Diverse and Inclusive Team – Join our exceptional, diverse, and globally distributed team at Clerk. We are committed to fostering an inclusive environment where everyone can contribute their best in building impactful products and tools for the modern web.

#7374. Microsoft Advertising Updates UI Following Advertiser Feedback via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.
