Microsoft Advertising announces UI improvements addressing advertiser feedback, including easier navigation, expanded reporting, sign-in troubleshooting, and account access management.

Microsoft Advertising is updating its platform interface following advertiser feedback about navigation, reporting, and account access issues.

In its November 9 product newsletter, Microsoft acknowledged several problems advertisers have faced, including trouble finding reports and resolving sign-in issues.

The updates include easier navigation, expanded campaign reporting, clearer account permissions, and better sign-in troubleshooting.

Microsoft says more improvements are planned as part of its ongoing interface updates.

Microsoft is making changes to how advertisers move through the platform and review campaign performance.

The updated navigation menu groups features by common tasks and makes more options available by default. New breadcrumbs show where users are within their account and campaign settings.

Advertisers can also use navigation search and customize menus to reach frequently used features faster.

Microsoft acknowledged that its previous reporting tools made it difficult to find, compare, and export certain data.

The bidding updates could help advertisers understand performance changes, especially when automated bidding targets have changed over time.

Microsoft is also responding to feedback about account access problems, including issues that have prevented some advertisers from signing in.

The newsletter specifically mentions blank pages, unexpected redirects, and repeated sign-in attempts.

To help resolve these issues, Microsoft is adding clearer error messages, browser troubleshooting tips, and more specific recovery steps.

Microsoft is also working to make sign-ins more persistent, although it hasn’t shared details about how login behavior will change.

Account permissions and user management are getting updates as well.

Advertisers will see clearer information about user roles, permissions, and who can grant access to an account.

Invitation pages will provide more details about required permissions, expiration dates, and next steps. Administrators will also have better visibility into pending and expired invitations.

These changes could be helpful for agencies and in-house teams managing access across several accounts, especially when adding new users.

Microsoft also acknowledged feedback that some account recommendations have been too generic.

It’s working to improve when recommendations appear and explain why a specific change is being suggested for an account.

Some of these updates address common frustrations with Microsoft Advertising, while others provide more detail for reviewing campaign performance.

The bidding reports are one of the more useful additions, especially for advertisers using Target CPA or Target ROAS.

Seeing past targets alongside actual performance and conversion-delay data could help explain changes that aren’t obvious in standard campaign reports.

The search term reporting improvements should also provide more insight into how AI Max campaigns match queries with landing pages.

On the account management side, clearer sign-in instructions and permission settings could reduce the time spent troubleshooting access issues.

Microsoft reiterated that some of these features continue to rollout, so it’s possible not every advertiser has seen the updates in their account UI.

For advertisers who have struggled with reporting or account access, these changes address some familiar problems. How well the new experience works will become clearer as more advertisers gain access to the updates.

Brooke serves as the Director of Growth Marketing at Smith Micro Software, with over 10 years of paid media experience. ...

#7373. Student journalists are reporting more local news for real audiences. Their professors are exhausted.

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

A new study finds that journalism school faculty — charged with putting more student work in front of non-student readers — often serve as a hidden infrastructure for local news. Burnout is a risk.

If there’s one big takeaway from the past two decades of local news struggle — the often perilous, occasionally brilliant transition from dominant newspapers to online media — perhaps it’s this: Passion alone isn’t enough.

A newspaper newsroom filled with committed editors and brilliant reporters still stares into the same headwinds as its more middling peers. The devotion of those leading a nonprofit outlet can mean little if revenue can’t keep the doors open. And even when the money is less important — when journalism is somewhat protected from the demands of the market — passion is still a finite resource, and it’s not always renewable.

That truism came to mind when I was reading a recent article by Sima Bhowmik of the University of Vermont and Andrea Lorenz of Kent State. It looks at one specific corner of the contemporary news ecosystem: journalism schools that use their students to fill voids in local coverage.

J-schools, after all, have faculties full of experience and students who need it. A few decades ago, an early-career journalist might have spent a few years covering city council meetings and school board budgets before moving up the chain to a bigger outlet. That path barely exists these days, and journalism programs know their students need the chance to do real work. What if these reporters-in-training could handle some of the local stories that the city’s daily no longer can?

RELATED ARTICLEA “win-win” partnership brings a surge of reporting firepower to hyperlocal news outlets around BostonSarah ScireNovember 13, 2025RELATED ARTICLELen Downie: The teaching hospital model works, but it will require drastic changeLeonard Downie Jr.September 12, 2012If you happen to live near a college with a journalism program, you’ve probably seen this idea — often called the “teaching hospital” model — in action. Here in the Boston suburbs, many of the stories I read about my town are written by Boston University students. According to UVM’s Center for Community News, where Bhowmik is an assistant research professor, there were 282 such efforts as of 2025, producing local news inside 205 colleges and universities. Those numbers were up from 227 programs in 172 colleges the year before — a boom fueled by a surge in philanthropic funding for these partnerships.

Bhowmik and Lorentz’s paper — published in Journalism & Mass Communication Educator — is the result of interviews with 118 faculty and staff at 101 of those colleges, and their major takeaway is one that will be familiar to many in local news: They’re exhausted.

Participants repeatedly used phrases such as “at capacity,” “don’t have the bandwidth,” “not enough hours of the day,” and “it’s just a lot” to describe the workload. Several described long workdays and exhaustion, with one participant saying the amount of editing “just about kills me every semester.” At many institutions, faculty workload was intensified by staffing shortages, retirements, hiring freezes, and budget cuts. This left faculty with little time to provide the one-on-one coaching necessary to elevate student stories to publishable quality… “What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.” (See above: Passion alone isn’t enough.) Here’s how one faculty member described their situation: Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. Just to save a few dozen proud Mizzou alums from emailing me: Yes, the “teaching hospital” model is not a new idea. The idea of giving journalism students real-world experience in public-facing outlets has been central to the Missouri Method for more than a century, and other major journalism schools have long done versions of this work. But Bhowmik notes that many of these new efforts are in smaller colleges with smaller journalism programs — places without the in-house infrastructure of a Columbia Missourian or KOMU, where media partnerships involve more ongoing negotiation. That can leave more of the pressure of sustainability on one or two overworked faculty members. As Bhowmik and Lorenz put it: …faculty labor is not incidental to the model; it is the hidden infrastructure that allows student work to move from classroom assignment to public-facing journalism. We argue that passion may help launch these programs, but passion alone is not a sustainable staffing model. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. In the decade-plus since, funders have gotten smarter about building institutional capacity — knowing that passion can only fuel so much. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

“What’s happening in the academic ecosystem is so phenomenal, thanks to these really passionate founders on the faculty,” Bhowmik told me. “But the extra work they’re putting it isn’t always getting acknowledged within the system. A professor might be editing 10 or 20 stories a week, by students without much experience, on top of a full teaching load. There’s a lot of overwork and some dissatisfaction, even though they know how important the work is.”

Here’s how one faculty member described their situation:

Think about it: Like, how many reporters does the average assigning editor at a mid-sized newspaper supervise? Half a dozen? So try, like, 20 of them, and each of them is writing for a different outlet maybe, which has its own set of deadlines and editor. And they’re all at varying skill levels, and broadly, they’re all pretty new. Their copy requires a lot of work, their reporting often requires a lot of handholding. Another talked about the responsibility of getting student work up to the level a news outlet expects. You can’t just give a meh student story a C- and be done with it if a local editor is expecting publishable copy. It takes a lot of work to get student work up to the quality of professionally published work. And a lot of students start out with really good ideas, do some good reporting by the time they’re done in the semester, it needs work, and that tends to fall on the faculty person. And that’s a lot of work outside of uncompensated work…But bridging that gap between what a student produces at the end of a semester and what can actually be published is still a big gap that needs to be addressed. And aside from the editing work, faculty described the administrative burden of being the connective tissue joining two distinct worlds. Negotiating pitches, coordinating deadlines, dealing with revisions, shepherding copy flows — all of it with students who are juggling other courses, work-study jobs, and the life of college student — can be “difficult to sustain without administrative support, dedicated staff, or funding for editors,” they write. If universities treat these initiatives as ordinary courses while expecting faculty to function simultaneously as instructors, editors, partnership managers, and newsroom supervisors, the model remains vulnerable to burnout and institutional instability. None of this, of course, is to say that these academic-publisher partnerships aren’t performing valuable roles in hundreds of communities. These programs have generated thousands of stories that otherwise might never have reached the public. But the sort of exhaustion Bhowmik and Lorenz describe reminds me a lot of what we saw in the first wave of local nonprofit news outlets in the late 2000s and early 2010s. Many of those were founded by idealistic journalists — often refugees from the local daily — who saw the opportunity to do something new and valuable for their communities. They put in long hours editing stories, selling ads, pitching foundations, and building audiences. And within a few years, a lot of them were burnt out, and their outlets suffered for it. In the decade-plus since, funders have gotten smarter about building institutional capacity — knowing that passion can only fuel so much. The world Bhowmik and Lorenz describe inside these journalism programs makes me think that department chairs and deans would be wise to consider a similar pivot to make these academic-publisher partnerships sustainable for the long term.

#7369. AI is becoming an information gateway. Audiences still don’t want it making their news.

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

In my Nieman Lab prediction this time last year, I wrote that “in 2026, the use of AI as an information gateway will only rise further.”

As it turns out, I was right. In new research that I published with my colleagues Rasmus Kleis Nielsen and Richard Fletcher, one of our many findings is that generative AI systems are becoming ever more significant information intermediaries, both when intentionally used and when incidentally encountered.

For our new report, “Generative AI and News Report 2026: From Standalone Tools to Information Infrastructure,”  we surveyed audiences across nine countries, including the United States, on their use of AI in their everyday lives, what they think its impact will be on different sectors of society, and what they think about its use in news and journalism specifically. I obviously think you should read the whole report, but here are some of the major takeaways.

The use of standalone conversational AI systems such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini continues to grow rapidly, with 51% of respondents across the six countries where we have past data saying they now use AI weekly (49% in the U.S.). That’s a faster spread, at least in the U.S., than smartphones or the internet saw.

Many people also encounter AI through “incidental” AI exposure in the AI-infused services and platforms they’re already using. Search engines such as Google and Bing are key drivers: 57% of respondents across countries said they saw or engaged with AI-generated answers or summaries in the last week. News media, too, drives AI exposure. An average of 24% of respondents across nine countries said they’d seen a label noting that content was made with AI in the last week, and 20% had seen a bullet-point AI news summary.

Despite all the talk about AI agents, we find that, so far, they haven’t broken into the mainstream. The vast majority of respondents across all nine countries (67%) said they had not used an AI agent in the last week. Fourteen percent used one for a real task and 13% tried one just to experiment.

Our data provides solid evidence that a broader transformation of the information environment is afoot. Conversational AI systems have arrived as information intermediaries. Trust is not the main reason people use them. Instead, people see conversational AI systems as friction-reducing machines that get them information more quickly.

“Getting information” continues to be the most common use case. Thirty-eight percent of respondents across countries (35% in the U.S.) said they had used generative AI for getting information in the last week, up from 24% in 2025 and 11% in 2024.

We looked more closely at what people do when they say they use AI for getting information. Across the six countries we’ve surveyed since 2024, we primarily see a mixture of search-equivalent behaviors like “researching a topic” (24%) or “answering factual questions” (18%). People are also increasingly turning to AI for advice (20%).

Using AI directly for news, meanwhile, remains a niche activity, as we demonstrated in the Digital News Report earlier this year. But that use is increasing: 9% of respondents across our six core countries say they use AI for news, up from 6% in 2025 and 3% in 2024.

Many people don’t hold clear or strong views about whether AI output is trustworthy. But people who do rely on it for information trust it. Across the nine countries, 75% of those who used AI to answer factual questions trusted the answers, as did 76% of those who used it for advice and 72% of those who used it for news. Only small minorities expressed distrust.

At the same time, trust and trustworthiness do not, at this stage, seem to be central to why people rely on AI systems. Instead, people tend to see these systems as friction-reducing machines. The convenience of getting a faster and easier answer was chosen by 64% as the main reason they use AI, followed by the interactivity afforded by natural language interfaces (56%). Only a minority (21%) said they use AI because they get more reliable information from it than from other sources.

As AI systems become widely used, their growing popularity is likely to affect the use of legacy content offerings and digital platforms and services. As my coauthor Rasmus Kleis Nielsen put it on LinkedIn, rapid growth in AI use isn’t just eating into what news sites have to offer. It’s eating into search, too — with people who use AI for information saying they use not just news, but search engines less, too, and by a wider margin. Challenging times may be ahead not just for news publishers, but also for the incumbent digital giants previously seen as unassailable.

As in previous years, we wanted to know what people think of AI’s use in journalism and news. We find that people’s attitudes here are remarkably entrenched and negative — though they also haven’t worsened significantly.

People across countries remain uncomfortable with the use of AI in the newsroom, especially with the idea of news being made entirely or primarily with AI. On average across nine countries, just 12% of respondents say they are very or somewhat comfortable with news made entirely by AI. That figure rises to 19% if there is some human oversight. This has hardly changed since 2024, and the gap between human- and AI-made news appears in every country we surveyed.

This also remains true regardless of whether it is breaking news or reporting. People are even more uncomfortable with AI being used for commentary or opinion — formats where the expectation that a human should share their view is strongest.

Our findings raise several important questions for journalists and publishers navigating the rise of generative AI.

First, generative AI is becoming an increasingly significant information intermediaries, with a number of possible knock-on effects on news. People are using AI to reduce friction by getting “good enough” outputs for some information needs — and skipping legacy platforms, established publishers, and the time and effort it takes to find the best available information. Discovery, brand attribution, data collection, and monetization all grow more challenging for publishers in this context.

Second, even though use continues to grow, the public remains skeptical of AI in news. This isn’t just a challenge for newsrooms’ internal adoption. It also shows that publishers using AI need to communicate to audiences what’s in it for them.

Third, beyond news, our findings speak to wider debates about AI’s societal impact. As in other studies on this topic, we find that uptake of AI is uneven. Weekly use is more common among people in professional occupations than those in entry-level or service occupations. Most people rely on free — usually less advanced — AI models.

The differences in how — not just whether — people use AI may become a new dividing line. As AI researcher and OpenAI cofounder Andrej Karpathy recently argued, there is a widening “gap in shared understanding of LLM capability,” with most people not seeing what has left those inside AI firms both excited and worried.

Expectations of what AI will do differ far more between daily users and people who have never used it than between any other groups in our survey. When two groups form very different views of the same technology because they have such different starting points, there will likely be consequences.

This doesn’t matter just for people’s individual choices around whether and how to make use of AI in their own lives. It also matters where AI is adopted and governed through collective decisions — in public services, for example — that apply to those who do not use it.

Felix Simon is the research fellow in AI, information, and news at the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism and a research associate at the Oxford Internet Institute (OII) at the University of Oxford.

Felix Simon is the research fellow in AI, information, and news at the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism and a research associate at the Oxford Internet Institute (OII) at the University of Oxford.

#7366. Visits To The Top Million Websites Fell 12%, Data Shows via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Data shows visits to the world's top million websites declined from an earlier peak, visits going to sites outside the top 100 barely changed.

Visits to the world’s top million websites were lower in the third quarter of 2026 than at their early 2022 peak (a decline of 12.2%), based on the data from Similarweb shared by SparkToro. The proportion of visits that go to websites that are not among the top 100 stayed relatively consistent over this time. That means while the total number of visits was down, that proportion still added up to less traffic for non-top-100 sites.

Rand Fishkin, SparkToro’s co-founder and CEO, wrote that the project began with his theory that the biggest sites, largely Big Tech properties, were taking more of the web’s traffic.

The post also reports a 29.2% drop in visits per internet user. This calculation uses user estimates that were revised partway through the period.

Fishkin asked Similarweb for total visits to the top million sites from its worldwide desktop and mobile web panel, along with a breakdown by site rank.

In the post’s figures, total visits peaked in Q1 2022 at 2.243 trillion. At the low point in Q1 2026, the total was 1.914 trillion, down 14.7% from the peak. The latest quarter, Q3 2026, came in at 1.970 trillion, 12.2% below the peak.

The totals on the post’s chart stay roughly level through 2023, then dip in early 2024 and fall again in the first half of 2025.

Similarweb categorized the top million websites from the number 1-10 to the 100,001st to 1 millionth website by five levels of ranking.

The share chart shows roughly a combined 33% and 36% of visits for the #1-10 (top) and #11-100 (next 90) sites during Q4 2021 and Q3 2026 respectively. The percentages for each band below the top 100 were a little lower at the end of the period compared to the beginning.

Fishkin described growth in the share for the top 100 as small, writing that “stagnation is a more accurate description of the trend.”

SparkToro’s per-user number is found by dividing Similarweb’s total visits by DataReportal’s estimated quarterly internet users globally. The table included in the blog post reports 4.95 billion internet users in Q4 2021 and 6.15 billion for Q3 2026. This represents an increase of around 24% during that time. The blog post reports the decline in visits per user at 29.2%.

The user series jumped from 5.65 billion to 6.04 billion in one quarter (Q3) of 2025. According to DataReportal in their October 2025 report, this jump in the number of users online was “largely attributable to significant revisions” in the rate of internet use that were officially reported by India and China. They also warned the reader to avoid interpreting this change as if hundreds of millions of new people came online in 90 days.

The SparkToro post did not include information regarding the change in reporting. Their estimate of 24% user growth compared data prior to and subsequent to the reporting change.

Similarweb’s panel tracks browser visits on desktop and mobile, but not app use. Fishkin wrote that consolidation by Big Tech is likely much more common in apps.

The data is also worldwide, and he wrote that it’s plausible traffic to U.S., U.K. and EU sites declined while regions with growing internet access, such as Asia and Africa, grew. The post doesn’t break out regions or state the panel’s size.

He did not provide reasons for the decline in visits. Fishkin’s list of possible factors includes app-only use, reliance on search engine results, AI tool utilization, social media platforms and the user behavior changes after the COVID-19 pandemic.

After initial publication SparkToro updated their article. On Nov. 10, Fishkin said on X that the prior version of his post “had an incorrect graph/data.” That version’s headline put the decline at 24%, which the URL still shows.

SparkToro sells audience research software, and Fishkin co-authored the book “Zero Click Marketing.”

SparkToro has provided an international benchmark for when a multi-year traffic decline shows up in your reports. The percentage of visits based upon the ranking of the website held close to steady in Similarweb’s browser-based metrics, although the actual number of visits decreased. The headline statistic in the report without reliance on changes in user estimates is the 12.2 percent decline in overall visits.

This report measures the number of browser visits to the top million websites regardless of where they came from, whereas the zero-click data I reported on back in June measured clicks per U.S. Google search.

Until a regional breakdown is available, SparkToro’s figures are best viewed as global context for traffic conversations, rather than as a benchmark for any single market. Fishkin also wrote that he plans to compare the findings with other sources and revisit the numbers every year or two.

#7363. Some local news outlets are losing physical newsrooms

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

When the Covid-19 pandemic normalized remote work, a second trend caught on: shutting down physical newsrooms.

A report from Northwestern University’s Local News Initiative, published Tuesday, looks at the cultural losses that news outlets face when their staff stops working together under one roof. Here’s LNI contributor and Columbia College Chicago journalism professor Jackie Spinner:

As pandemic-era safeguards that necessitated working from home waned, some have championed the technological advances that allow many journalists to report, edit and publish from basically anywhere with stable Internet access. But the cost savings from a smaller or nonexistent physical presence isn’t the only consideration. Less clear-cut is the impact on the elements of journalism that never appear on budgets, such as the training absorbed from looking over someone’s shoulder or the institutional knowledge gained and passed by working in proximity to others over time.

During the pandemic, several news outlets downsized their offices — from city high-rises to co-working spaces — due to financial constraints and new hybrid work schedules.

On Tuesday, I spent time looking for the local news outlets that have foregone or reduced office space and opted for primarily remote work in recent years. Here are some:

Are there any I’m missing? Let me know. And read the full piece here.

The most important stories about journalism and the future of news, delivered to your inbox every afternoon.

A new study finds that journalism school faculty — charged with putting more student work in front of non-student readers — often serve as a hidden infrastructure for local news. Burnout is a risk.

#7360. “A government that ‘gets it’”: Canada recommits to federal funding for news

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

The new budget would make a local reporting program permanent and extend a 35% tax credit on newsroom salaries. The support makes “a massive, massive difference.”

A Canadian program to place local reporters in underserved communities has lived from renewal to renewal. Publishers who relied on the Local Journalism Initiative couldn’t be sure whether the money would be there again.

That changed last week when Canada proposed a federal budget that would make the Local Journalism Initiative permanent. The 2026 budget would increase LJI funding to $134 million over four years with $41 million in annual ongoing funding after that. (Figures are, naturally, in Canadian dollars.) For the budget to be finalized, it must pass Parliament. With Prime Minister Mark Carney’s party in the majority, it’s expected to.

Expanding the LJI is not even the biggest piece of federal help for newsrooms, according to Paul Deegan, president and CEO of the news industry advocacy group News Media Canada. The same budget extends a 35% tax credit on newsroom salaries through 2028 and opens it to radio and television newsrooms for the first time. The tax credit will cost $501 million over the next five years, according to the budget. Thanks to the Online News Act, Canadian newsrooms also get funding from Google. And then there’s Canadian investment in public media; the 2026 budget added $300 million over two years to CBC/Radio-Canada “to strengthen its capacity to meet the evolving needs of Canadians across the country.”

I asked Deegan: How did Canadian publishers secure so much federal funding for news?

“We have a government that ‘gets it,’” Deegan said. “They see what Big Tech monopolists have done to local news. They know the labor tax credit is the leading driver of maintaining and growing newsroom headcount.”

RELATED ARTICLECalifornia’s big local news bill becomes lawLaura Hazard OwenOctober 1, 2026There have been similar proposals and experiments in the United States, including a new tax credit in California. But Canada is much further down the road. The country can offer lessons on what it looks like when a federal government commits to supporting journalism.

The labor tax credit refunds 35% of a newsroom employee’s salary, up to $29,750 per journalist per year. The percentage had been scheduled to fall to 25% at the end of 2026.

“In terms of employing the [largest] number of journalists and keeping newsroom employment stability, the labor tax credit is number one,” Deegan said.

There’s also the Online News Act which has brought in $100 million of Google money annually. Of that amount, $63 million is set aside for news publishers. (Meta blocked news on Facebook and Instagram in Canada rather than pay under the Online News Act.) Publishers receive around $17,000 per journalist per year.

“That’s about $47,000 per journalist per year from those two measures,” Deegan said. “That just makes a massive, massive difference.”

In the U.S., some ideas for funding local journalism have been criticized as helping large chain newspapers — including chains that have hollowed out local newsrooms for years. Deegan — whose news industry group counts Postmedia and Torstar as members — acknowledged that the largest share of tax credit dollars goes to the largest employers.

But News Media Canada also represents small and digital-only publications, and Deegan noted the smallest Canadian outlets face another problem. To qualify for the tax credit, an organization must produce original news content and regularly employ at least two journalists “at arm’s length” from its owners. An advisory board found the second rule excludes small “mom-and-pop” newsrooms.

The Local Journalism Initiative is a more targeted program than the tax credit. Launched in 2019, it was designed to support local and community news in underserved areas. The LJI program supports about 470 reporting positions in roughly 1,000 communities across Canada.

Even generous government subsidies have not stopped losses in local news completely. But early results showed the LJI increased reporting capacity and local news in “news poverty” communities with limited local news offerings. The same evaluation identified reaching “news deserts” — communities with no existing local news outlets — and Indigenous communities as obstacles.

“Since LJI is demand driven, funding allocation across the country varies and gaps remain in some areas, such as news deserts,” the report reads. Later, the report notes that allowing news startups “could better support attraction and retainment of journalists” in the neediest communities.

Many of the newsrooms receiving the LJI funding are small. Deegan described family-owned weekly newspapers with circulations as low as 800. Funding provides a local journalist to report on “cops, courts, city hall, and the school board.”

“Without those LJI journalists in some of those communities, those things simply wouldn’t be covered,” he said.

Some underserved communities, particularly in northern Canada, struggle to hire reporters given high living costs and remote locations. “Even with the grant, it’s just hard to recruit and find people,” Deegan noted.

Deegan points to two factors that have made Canada’s federal funding for news work. The first is distance. Publishers get LJI money from one of three administrator organizations: News Media Canada, Community Radio Fund of Canada, or the Consortium of Official Language Minority Community Media. These organizations — not the government itself — administer the funds and an independent board advises on who qualifies for the tax credit.

“To avoid perceptions of conflict of interest, the government ensured that third parties adjudicate who receives local journalism initiative reporters and who is eligible for the labor tax credit,” Deegan said.

The second factor applies to the Online News Act and, more generally, to Canadian politics.

“When it comes to holding those platforms to account, one of the reasons we have been successful is that we have a ban [on] corporate political financing at the federal level in Canada,” Deegan said. “These companies sprinkle goodies around to friendly academics, think tanks, and other useful idiots to do some of their bidding, but our government sees through that nonsense.”

#7358. Does The Affiliate Model Survive Checkout Inside AI Mode And Gemini? – Ask An SEO via @sejournal, @rollerblader

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Spend less time on publishing and reports, and more on helpful content. November 18, 2 PM ET.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Affiliate links still help protect your commissions as AI checkout grows, while stronger tracking and better program choices help preserve referral credit.

This week’s Ask an SEO is about affiliate marketing, which is actually one of my favorite topics to cover.

“With Google’s UCP and checkout happening inside Gemini and AI Mode, the shopper never clicks my link. Does the affiliate model survive this, and what should I be doing now?”

Great question, and yes, affiliate links still matter, so make sure you keep using them. It is similar to Shopify having the Google Direct Checkout UCP. That doesn’t mean you don’t need to keep your Shopify store, but you also want to make sure you are protected with multiple ways to monetize and that you work with brands that focus on more than just links.

This is why there is a question. If the person doesn’t make it to the link, do links matter? Yes, and this goes back to the history of links.

I was on a call once where we decided that if there was an affiliate parameter and if it was on the browser, the website would automatically prompt the person to log in. This was high-tech back then.

The message displayed was a benefit to them, to let them know that if they logged in, they could add to their cart and check out later when they have more time vs. needing to do everything right now. When they logged in, we could sync up the sale with the original click, and the affiliate would get credit. This led to fingerprinting and many more advanced techniques the networks (like Buy.at at the time) began working on.

There are also affiliate companies that show ads via streaming TV shows and track the IPs connected to the same connection to track affiliate sales back. They count the IPs against consumer data sent back to the network and get credit for the sale.

The reason I mention this is because, clicks don’t always matter, but they do add levels of tracking security for you. There is IP hashing, companies like TV Scientific and AudioHook are using alternative tracking methods, and vanity codes. Just make sure your vanity code doesn’t get leaked to coupon websites, browser extensions, and that AI doesn’t start showing them to traffic you didn’t refer.

See also: When Attribution Looks More Precise Than The Data Behind It

Being afraid of Google taking the order and cutting your link out is a valid concern. But think about TikTok Shop, or retailer apps inside ChatGPT like Walmart’s, which keep the purchase on the platform. These cut your link out completely. The person may read your content then go to TikTok for a review and purchase there.

You have big brands that allow third parties to sell on their website (i.e., marketplaces), and with some you don’t get commissions for those sales as they’re third party vendors. Your links matter and clicks tracked, but the conversion is not commissionable. In the case of systems like TikTok, use links on your website and upload content to their platform to make money with your vendors that are synced there. You control the situation, don’t stress over a single link on the website.

For programs that allow third-party sales and don’t pay, select other programs. That is a choice you are knowingly making and you are knowingly sending your traffic there.

See also: Getting Your Product Into ChatGPT Isn’t The Hard Part, Getting It Through Checkout Is

Another reason to not stop using affiliate links is that consumers do not trust AI. Keynote speakers at college graduations keep getting booed when they talk about it; younger generations are avoiding brands that use it for creating images; people are starting to engage less with AI ads on social media and convert less when the content and images are clearly AI on a website (blog posts, product pages, etc.).

When AI is used, there’s no way to know if the store and ad are legit, or if they’re a third-party counterfeit produced overseas. Reviews are also being gamed. Consumers may not trust the AI Overview as much if quality doesn’t improve, and if they don’t like AI, they will instead scroll down to your content, where they can be exposed to your link.

There is another layer to affiliate links mattering as well.

AI Overviews cannot demonstrate a product or service. Yes, they can play a video, but Google search has done this for years. People, especially those who do not trust AI, will keep looking for human-created content before making the purchase. This is especially true for larger-ticket items like bikes, software, or computer systems, and demonstrations of video games before buying them. Think of parents who want to make sure it is age-appropriate, or someone who wants to see what the graphics and games are like from an actual gamer.

This makes your affiliate links directly relevant, and in your advertising disclosure you can let them know that by clicking your link they are helping to support your business and not AI. This will resonate strongly with the anti-AI generations and shoppers. It is also required by the FTC and some international groups that you use advertising disclosures, so you might as well.

See also: Only 28% Of Americans Trust AI Search – And That Gap Is Your SEO Opening

There is affordable technology from third parties like ThoughtMetrics (I have zero relationship with them, just a happy user) that shows all touchpoints in a customer journey (and repeat purchases for LTV). If you didn’t use your affiliate link, you won’t be in the running for credit or a commission. In another program, we reconcile at the end of the month and manually add anything that didn’t track. If that click didn’t happen, you would not be making as much money.

Link clicks are always going to be an issue, but they are also still working and a first line of defense in your ability to get credit for your referrals. So yes, affiliate links and clicks still matter; there are just additional layers of protection available with more advanced tracking techniques.

#7356. With event costs up and funding down, journalism organizations cancel conferences and cut staff

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

ONA, OpenNews, and others see troubled times ahead, so it’s goodbye to “rubber chicken dinners.”

RELATED ARTICLEWe know coronavirus has wrecked the events business for media in 2020. But will it come back post-virus?Joshua BentonMarch 25, 2020When the pandemic forced conferences to go virtual, the question was whether people would come back in person post-Covid.

For a while, they did. For a couple years after the pandemic, people were excited to be in person together again. U.S. journalism organizations saw their revenue climb, as sponsors and funders spent generously.

At the end of 2026, though, the situation is less rosy. Combine surging jet fuel prices, inflation, media companies cutting costs, and declining sponsor and funder dollars, and the future appears grim not just for journalism conferences but, in some cases, for the organizations behind them.

Revenue is the total revenue line on each organization’s IRS Form 990. The 990s are from ProPublica’s Nonprofit Explorer. ONA’s 990 for 2025 was not available as of November 12, 2026.

The Online News Association said on Tuesday that it is “pausing” its 26-year-old conference and laying off its five staffers.

“Before the pandemic, the annual conference was ONA’s biggest source of revenue, and together with grants, it sustained the organization. This is no longer true,” Niketa Patel wrote. “The costs of large convenings have climbed sharply while professional development and sponsorship budgets have shrunk.”

LION Publishers said in September that it’s getting out of the large conference business for now. “Philanthropy is famously fickle, and funding priorities have shifted, making this kind of conference a very tough lift,” executive director Chris Krewson added.

LION barely broke even on the conferences it held in 2025 and 2026, Krewson told me. The organization may still hold smaller events next year, it’s not cutting staff or programming, and it’s expanding its Audit program. But it “won’t be taking on the responsibility for an independent event at a hotel with a food and beverage minimum and hotel room block for 400-plus people.”

“Every dollar I spend on rubber chicken dinners and microphones/WiFi for three days is money I can’t spend on programs that make publishers’ businesses stronger,” Krewson said.

OpenNews said in October that it’s switching into “emergency mode”1 and cutting back on “work that distills journalism craft: no more community calls or Source articles for at least the rest of 2026 and maybe longer.”

The future of OpenNews’s annual conference, SRCCON, is uncertain. “We usually make our decision about SRCCON at about this time every year, but this year is different as we consider the news from both LION and ONA and the funding pressures that we know all too well,” Jessica Morrison, co–executive director of OpenNews, told me in an email. “We are opening a community conversation today — emails going out very soon — to gather input about SRCCON, our other programs, and where we go next with OpenNews.”

The three-year-old Organization for Accessible Journalism said Thursday that it is shutting down. “Financial support never reached a level that allowed us to build beyond an all volunteer effort — including my full-time effort,” founder Stacy Kess wrote.

Journalism conferences in the United States are certainly not gone. IRE and NICAR will hold their annual data journalism conference in Denver in March. The Institute for Nonprofit News is “talking about what INN Days will look like in 2027” and will know more by early next year. And the International Symposium on Journalism is on for 2027 — its 28th year, founder (and Nieman Foundation board member) Rosental Alves told me. But things have gotten harder, he said. “It’s not only the difficulty of finding sponsors, but also the rising costs of venues, hotels, airfares, etc., compounded by shrinking travel budgets in newsrooms and even the problems international attendees face in coming to the U.S.,” he told me in an email. ISOJ cut costs, in part by moving to a different venue, and it benefits from being relatively small. “It’s very sad that foundations and corporations that funded journalism conferences in the past have pulled back their support,” Alves added. “The need for these gatherings is as great as ever.” a play on Emergency Mode for News, its initiative to support journalists working during disasters [↩]

Journalism conferences in the United States are certainly not gone.

IRE and NICAR will hold their annual data journalism conference in Denver in March.

The Institute for Nonprofit News is “talking about what INN Days will look like in 2027” and will know more by early next year.

And the International Symposium on Journalism is on for 2027 — its 28th year, founder (and Nieman Foundation board member) Rosental Alves told me.

But things have gotten harder, he said. “It’s not only the difficulty of finding sponsors, but also the rising costs of venues, hotels, airfares, etc., compounded by shrinking travel budgets in newsrooms and even the problems international attendees face in coming to the U.S.,” he told me in an email. ISOJ cut costs, in part by moving to a different venue, and it benefits from being relatively small.

“It’s very sad that foundations and corporations that funded journalism conferences in the past have pulled back their support,” Alves added. “The need for these gatherings is as great as ever.”

#7355. 🇫🇷 Head of Product - AI x EdTech - French fluent at Paradox

It’s the chance to define a new category : The Operating System (OS) for Human Greatness Augmented with AI.

Science-backed. Design-led. AI-powered. Global.

A chance to build an AI-native Product Department

You’ll be working with the most complex data in the world : the human mind.

#7352. Indonesia’s new copyright bill would force Google and Meta to pay to aggregate news

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

On Wednesday, Meta, TikTok, and Google testified in the Indonesian parliament in opposition to a new copyright bill. The proposed law would require digital platforms to pay news publishers in Indonesia for aggregating and previewing their content, according to a new report from Reuters.

RELATED ARTICLEThis citizen-run organization is teaching thousands of Indonesians to fact-checkHanaa' TameezApril 12, 2023The bill does not distinguish between aggregation and republication. Tech platforms would have to pay even when they surface news link previews or story snippets in their products, including AI search products. The law would also force platforms to compensate publishers when their journalism is used to train AI models.

Any compensation would be paid through a state-run management organization, which would then distribute the funds to publishers. Platforms that don’t comply could face sanctions, including the loss of their local business permits.

If the bill does pass, Indonesia would become the first country in Southeast Asia to formally fold AI into its copyright law.

Social media and search engine companies have come out in force against the bill, with Google leading the campaign.

Indonesia is a massive market for global digital platforms with roughly 230 million internet users. In 2023, Indonesia had the fifth-most Google users of any country in the world, with traffic reaching 4.42 billion searches.

RELATED ARTICLEMeta will remove legit news from Facebook and Instagram in Canada — but may leave the bad stuff upLaura Hazard OwenAugust 2, 2023As of October 2026, 92% of internet users in Indonesia reported using WhatsApp, with Instagram and Facebook falling close behind at 84% and 79% respectively, according to We Are Social’s Digital 2027 report.

On Wednesday, a Google official argued that the bill would limit access to news, according to Reuters.

Berni Moestafa, Meta’s head of public policy in Indonesia, testified that if the bill is passed “publishers could lose readership traffic as well as potential advertising and subscription revenue.”

Back in 2023, Canada passed the Online News Act, its own, albeit narrower law which required platforms like Google and Meta to pay publishers for sharing or linking to their content. Meta responded by blocking news links on Facebook and Instagram in Canada altogether.

Google made Gemini 4 Hydrogen the default model in AI Mode for all users and uses it for the hardest AI Overview questions, two days into a core update.

Google made Gemini 4 Hydrogen the default model in AI Mode for all users worldwide, starting today. Sites that track how often AI Mode cites them now have a new model to account for.

In an X thread announcing the change, Robby Stein, VP of Product for Google Search, said Hydrogen is also “being used for your hardest questions in AI Overviews.” Google’s Search post says the model is available globally in Search for all users.

The switch came two days into the November core update, which was still rolling out as of 2:05 p.m. ET today.

The Search post says Hydrogen is able to handle multi-part inquiries, inquiries that contain typos, highly specific inquiries, and follow commands more thoroughly than previous versions of Search.

The post from Search says that Hydrogen also allows for better query fan-out. Query fan-out refers to how Search simultaneously executes multiple queries across each of the subtopics of an inquiry. According to the post, the result is what Google calls “connecting you to a broader range of helpful links than before,” and this includes websites which users may have never stumbled upon themselves. Stein’s thread says that the fan-out improvements result in showing more links to the web. Neither the post nor Stein’s thread provide counts for links or sites.

The post references more visual aids in answers through use of images, structured responses, comparison tables and additional customized tools available to users; additionally, the answers provided will be more nuanced in all languages supported by AI Mode.

The last default change I covered was at I/O back in May when Gemini 3.5 Flash became the global default for AI Mode for everyone. Neither the Search post nor Stein’s post said what Hydrogen replaced.

Stein’s thread has Hydrogen handling the hardest AI Overview questions but doesn’t say which questions count as hardest or what answers the rest. The Search post doesn’t mention AI Overviews.

Google made Gemini 3 the default model for AI Overviews globally in January. At the time, I noted that Google had described plans to send complex questions to Gemini 3 and simpler ones to faster models, and hadn’t said whether that would apply to AI Overviews.

Citation data you pull for AI Mode or AI Overviews this month now spans a model change on Nov. 12. The change applies to AI Mode’s default answers and to the hardest AI Overview questions.

Nov. 12 falls inside the core update window, which began Nov. 10. Logging both dates lets you compare your tracking data before and after each one, rather than reading this month as a single change.

Google’s claim that there are more links doesn’t include figures. You can check how it applies to your site by looking at the citation and referral numbers in your own reports.

Although the Search Status Dashboard shows that the November core update is rolling out, its completion date will be logged as the second date, after Nov. 12.

You should watch for an announcement from Google on which AI Overview questions are classified as hardest so you can see how much of your AI Overview tracking will reflect the change.

Follow on YouTube. Matt G. Southern is the Senior News Writer at Search Engine Journal, where he’s covered Google, SEO, ...

#7344. Google Ads Rolls Out New Invalid Traffic Overview Card via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Google Ads introduces an Invalid Traffic Overview Card showing estimated savings, post-billing credits, and campaign-level reports. Here's what advertisers need to know.

Google Ads is rolling out a new Invalid Traffic Overview Card that shows advertisers how much invalid activity its systems filter before billing, along with credits issued after billing.

Until now, advertisers had to look across campaign reports and monthly billing statements to find that information. The new card brings those figures into one view, with additional reporting available by day and campaign.

Ginny Marvin, Google Ads Liaison, announced the rollout on LinkedIn and shared more details in the latest Ads Decoded newsletter.

The global rollout began this week, and the feature is also available in Search Ads 360 for Google Ads accounts.

Advertisers can find the new card on the Google Ads Overview page, under the Campaigns tab.

Google has long used automated systems and manual reviews to filter invalid ad interactions, including bot activity, accidental clicks, and attempts to artificially inflate traffic.

Most of that activity is caught before billing, so advertisers aren’t charged for those interactions.

The new card includes three reporting areas:

Clicking “View details” opens a daily trend chart and campaign table. Advertisers can also access the monthly invoices where credits were applied.

The estimated savings figure is the main addition to this information. Advertisers could already review invalid click metrics, but those reports didn’t show the estimated dollar amount Google had prevented from being charged.

According to Marvin, Google’s systems catch the majority of invalid traffic before it reaches an advertiser’s invoice.

Google Ads locks standard campaign metrics at month-end to match billing records. If Google detects more invalid traffic after that, any credits issued won’t change the Cost or CPA figures in the main Campaigns table.

Advertisers who want to see those adjustments reflected in their performance metrics can use the Invalid Activity Credit: Search & PMax report.

Available in Report editor, it includes 10 columns, such as Adjusted cost and Adjusted cost / conv. Credits are attributed to the dates the ads originally ran, even when Google issued them later.

This gives advertisers a way to review Search and Performance Max costs with post-billing invalid activity credits factored in, without relying solely on the standard Campaigns table.

The campaign-level breakdowns should make it easier to see where Google is filtering invalid activity, although the estimated savings figures will need some context.

A campaign with significantly more spend could show higher estimated savings simply because it’s generating more traffic. Comparing those dollar amounts alone won’t necessarily tell advertisers which campaigns have greater traffic quality concerns.

There’s also a limit to what the numbers can tell us. The card reflects activity Google’s systems have identified as invalid, but it doesn’t establish how much fraudulent traffic may have gone undetected.

For lead generation accounts, invalid traffic reporting won’t necessarily explain why fake or low-quality leads are continuing to come in.

Marvin addressed this in the Ads Decoded newsletter: Google’s systems can filter invalid ad interactions, but bad leads can still come from legitimate clicks or submissions that never originated from an ad.

An account could have relatively little invalid traffic reported in Google Ads and still struggle with lead quality. Advertisers dealing with those issues will need to compare the reporting against their CRM data rather than assuming Google’s invalid traffic figures tell the whole story.

Continuously build expertise in Coalesce's technical features, use cases, and integration possibilities.

Experience with MCP and governing AI agents' in building and managing data infrastructure. Strong technical understanding of agentic AI and how it is used in data engineering.

Familiarity with data governance and data observability concepts and their relationship with data engineering

Excellent communication skills, with the ability to convey complex technical concepts to both technical and non-technical audiences.

Self-motivated, proactive, and results-driven mindset with a focus on driving customer success.

Experience with version control systems (e.g., Git) and collaborative development practices.

#7341. Reddit tests AI-compiled product reviews

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The new feature is designed to help users get recommendations and information by converting platform comments into overviews.

Reddit is testing a new Reddit Reviews option, which will help users find relevant product recommendations and info in the app by converting relevant Reddit comments into dedicated overviews.

As shown in these example screens, Reddit Reviews will use artificial intelligence to collate information on products, sourcing relevant references from comments and mentions across the app. The tool will then present comments and upvotes in a centralized review format, which should make it easier for users to gather context about a prospective purchase.

As explained by Reddit: “Lived experience shows up across Reddit communities: in r/skincareaddiction, people share what they’ve tried for dry skin, and in r/espresso, baristas and home brewers compare their setups. These exchanges show how a product can work for different people and what tradeoffs they consider. Reddit Reviews uses AI to both organize these conversations where people describe their product experiences, and to surface the nuance, context, and connection to the original conversation.”

Reddit said these reviews may also include images, prices and purchase links when they’re supplied by brand and retailer catalogs.

“To go deeper, people can browse all comments about a product, using topic filters derived from Reddit conversations to focus on their questions,” Reddit said. “Clicking on a comment lets them read the original discussion, ask a follow-up question, or join the community for more advice. For people considering alternatives, the review also highlights products people commonly compare.”

To find a review, Reddit users can search for a product or category in the app. Products with a review page will be highlighted in the predictive answers to the query.

Reddit said it will initially test the option “with a set of frequently discussed products and categories.”

Reddit added that these reviews will only include information gleaned from organic conversations within Reddit communities, and that these recommendations will not include paid placement of any kind.

Reddit is working to find more ways to get people coming directly to its app for insights because its referral traffic from search engines has been rapidly declining.

Amid rising use of AI chatbots for discovery, Reddit had become a critical source of information due to its user-generated, community-voted listings, which allowed chatbots and search engines to offer direct insights into consumer experiences.

But Google has been putting more emphasis on AI overviews, which provide an AI-generated summary of these notes within search results, while ChatGPT has also reduced its reliance on Reddit data. As a result, Reddit has suffered a big loss in terms of search referrals.

In the company’s Q3 performance update published earlier this month, Reddit CEO Steve Huffman said that the company’s search traffic dropped by more than 1 million referrals on average per week in October.

Given this, Reddit is working to build more valuable tools to drive direct traffic. The company is also experimenting with locking down more of its information, making it only available to logged-in users.

Reddit Reviews is another step in this direction as the platform looks for ways to maintain the value of its app.

#7337. TikTok tests shopping subscriptions

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company invited select users in the U.S. to join its TikTok Shop Plus program, which will cost about $10 a month after a free trial period.

TikTok is trying out another way to get more people shopping in its app with the launch of a new TikTok Shop Plus subscription option that offers discounts and deals for repeat buyers.

As reported by Modern Retail, TikTok began inviting selected U.S. users over the weekend to join its new TikTok Shop Plus program. Additional reporting from GeekSeller suggested that TikTok has been gradually rolling out the option over the last month.

As per Modern Retail: “TikTok Shop Plus costs $9.99 a month after a 30-day free trial, according to TikTok Shop. It includes perks such as expanded free shipping, exclusive discounts, coupon bundles and instant refunds.”

The offering is similar to Amazon Prime and other subscription shopping options, which are designed to increase customer loyalty through add-on benefits.

It’s TikTok’s latest effort to get more people to buy products on the platform as it works to build a bigger revenue stream through in-app shopping.

TikTok owner ByteDance saw significant success with in-stream shopping via Douyin, the version of TikTok available in China. With Douyin, ByteDance was able to successfully transition short-form video engagement into livestream shopping, which has since generated billions of dollars for the company.

According to KrAsia, Douyin generated nearly $500 billion in gross merchandise volume in 2025, with livestream commerce in particular driving a significant portion of that activity.

That’s likely why Douyin has been working to make in-stream shopping a bigger element of the TikTok experience as well. However, TikTok users have so far been less open to shopping in the app.

That may be changing over time, though. According to Marketing Maze, TikTok generated $26.2 billion GMV in the first half of 2025.

That’s still a long way from Douyin’s result, the numbers make it clear why ByteDance is so enamored with the opportunity, and why the company keeps looking for ways to get more people searching for products and making in-stream purchases.

A loyalty program could help on this front by encouraging the most active shoppers to keep buying from TikTok merchants, in the hopes that this will lead to further endorsements and promotions of TikTok’s shopping experience.

Patrick Nommensen, TikTok Shop’s president of strategic initiatives, told Modern Retail that the program is currently being tested “with an expanding group of eligible U.S. users.”

Places’ mission is to help discerning global travelers find their favorite places wherever they go through expert curation and community trust.

While the product itself has existed since 2023 the culture is very much like a new start-up with leadership being heavily invested in this product space and this role being the first dedicated Data Scientist to this space and supported by a dedicated Data Engineer.

As shown in this example image, Threads users will now be able to filter their search results by:

#7331. Ad Tech Briefing: Amazon’s auction trial puts agentic advertising’s trust problem in focus

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Ad Tech Briefing covers the latest in ad tech and platforms for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Tuesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

Amazon, along with its Big Tech cohorts, wants advertisers to trust its AI more, but fresh allegations from federal regulators underline concerns over such platforms.

Last week, the Federal Trade Commission published newly unredacted details from its lawsuit accusing Amazon of secretly inflating advertising prices, with similar charges against Google still fresh in the public mindset.

According to the FTC, Amazon imposed surcharges of up to 50% on ordinary shopping days and 250% during major retail events, generating an additional $87 million during the 2022 holiday season. Amazon disputes the allegations, and the legal proceedings are ongoing.

Amazon recently reported quarterly advertising revenue approaching $20 billion, with  Amazon Ads Agent – an AI-powered platform that’s likely to attract accusations of “black box” from detractors – a key part of its push to automate campaign planning, buying and optimization.

And with Amazon, the third pillar of adland’s triopoly, the key question for advertisers is whether they should hand over more control to a platform when they cannot independently verify how its existing auctions determine prices.

The FTC and 22 state attorneys general accuse Amazon of misleading advertisers about its Sponsored Products auctions.

Amazon presented these as generalized second-price auctions, where advertisers generally pay prices determined by competing bids rather than their maximum bid.

However, regulators allege Amazon introduced undisclosed reserve-price mechanisms that increased charges without corresponding competition, effectively pushing prices closer to first-price auctions.

According to the government body’s allegations, during the 2021 holiday season, Amazon increased average cost per click by 20%, with some advertisers seeing increases exceeding 200%. The complaint also alleges executives considered using reserve prices to address a $40 million revenue shortfall.

The allegations extend to how Amazon explained rising prices, i.e., whether Amazon misrepresented how its auctions worked and what advertisers were charged.

However, Amazon maintains that reserve prices reflect the value of its advertising placements, disputes the FTC’s interpretation, and argues that reserve pricing is not inherently unlawful.

For advertisers, the concern is straightforward: they can see what they paid, but cannot necessarily establish how that price was calculated.

The allegations come shortly after Amazon introduced new products designed to automate more advertising decisions.

At October’s unBoxed conference, Amazon unveiled Ads Agent and Full-Funnel Campaigns, allowing advertisers to set objectives, products, creative and budgets while its technology handles targeting, allocation and optimization.

Amazon says beta testing delivered up to 67% higher return on long-term ad spend and 29% lower costs per new-to-brand customer compared with manually configured campaigns. Those figures are company-reported.

The proposition is that advertisers can reduce manual campaign management and improve performance. However, greater automation also means relying more heavily on Amazon to explain how budgets are spent and why its systems make particular decisions.

While the FTC’s allegations concern Sponsored Products auctions, not Amazon’s new AI products, they still add weight to concerns raised about advertisers’ ability to inspect automated decisions, verify results, and understand fees.

Google’s ad tech antitrust case provides a precedent, although the conduct and markets differ.

A federal court found Google illegally monopolized publisher ad-serving and ad exchange markets – in a separate case, its search practices were also found to be monopolistic. The subsequent remedies stopped short of divestiture but imposed measures intended to improve competition and interoperability.

Both cases concern platforms whose control over advertising infrastructure limits customers’ ability to scrutinize how transactions work.

The difference is that Amazon is now asking advertisers to give its technology greater control over buying decisions, even as regulators challenge the transparency of its existing auction practices. These developments involve different businesses and issues, but they point to a broader accountability problem in digital advertising.

“We’re having to look much more closely at what happens after the click and whether those clicks are actually producing qualified leads and revenue.”

— Lindsay Marty, founder of Above the Bar Marketing, speaking to Digiday about advertisers reassessing their Google Search budgets.

ChatGPT’s ad offering may be on the rise, but the nature of its value is still hard to determine, according to numbers analyzed in recent studies.

⁠Amazon’s Ads nears $20 billion in Q3 as it bets on agentic AI to fuel further growth

Amazon’s new automated buying tools promise simplified execution while raising questions about transparency and advertiser control.

Alphabet’s latest earnings reveal how AI is complicating the economics of search advertising even as its cloud business expands.

⁠Taboola faces Yahoo mediation over its 30-year native ad deal

A dispute involving one of Taboola’s most important commercial relationships emerges alongside disappointing quarterly revenue.

AI-driven tech layoffs suck, but might lead to certification

Former Google ads leader, Jonathan Bellack, on his Platformocracy outlet, examines how AI coding threatens junior engineering career paths and argues that professional certification could become increasingly important.

⁠Agencies find AI overuse is leading to brief bloat

Agency executives warn that AI-generated briefs are creating more work rather than improving productivity.

Media Briefing: Publishers’ revenue growth defies traffic declines, but tougher comparisons loom

Major publishers continue to grow revenue despite deteriorating referral traffic and mounting AI disruption.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.

#7327. Can web users trust ad tech’s server-side shift?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

“Trust” is one of those words that gets thrown around a lot in ad tech circles. But it’s going to become even more important if ad tech completes its server-side shift.

In response to Apple’s identity resolution crackdown, the online ad industry has rallied around server-side ways to sidestep browser-based controls. IAB Tech Lab’s Trusted Server is the biggest example of this. Not only does the open-source software promise publishers faster page-load speeds, it enables publishers to better control their ad tech stacks and minimize their exposure to browser-based blockades.

Taking ad calls out of the browser, however, has the effect of taking them out of users’ sights. A browser can see – and block – the calls a web page asks it to make. Once the calls run on a publisher’s server, though, users have to trust the publisher to a greater degree.

And this is where the trust in Trusted Server really matters.

Lam also noted that Trusted Server was designed from the start to support integration with consent management platforms. That would give websites a way to share a user’s consent status across the programmatic supply chain. But of course, that requires the user blindly trusting the site to do so.

Here are a few highlights from the conversation, which have been edited for length and clarity.

“There is a fear of just broadly server-side because it’s not automatically observable to the consumer’s browser and you can’t automatically inspect it from that point. But what we’ve done for Trusted Server in particular is we’ve made sure all of this is considered. What’s included in Trusted Server is pretty robust logging and auditing. Anyone who is looking to deploy Trusted Server gets that out of the box.”

Unsealed FTC allegations underline pre-existing questions over black boxes.

The Trade Desk’s CEO says the company is looking harder at M&A than ever before.

Forrester’s Wave study assessed 12 agency groups for their current offerings and strategies, and found diminishing differentiation among the largest of them.
