#7325. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7323. X adds video clipping

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The option allows users to share shorter snippets of creator content while still giving credit and allowing both posters to earn monetary rewards.

X has added another way for users to share content and ensure that original creators get credit, with the launch of its new clipping option for videos.

As shown in this example screen, now, when viewing a video on X that is 2 minutes or longer, users will be able to select “clip video” from the repost options.

With this, users can choose a specific segment of the original video to share, from 5 to 60 seconds in length (limited to 30% of the original clip). They can then re-post and add their own commentary.

The re-shared clip will include an overlay that displays the creator’s handle, while viewers of the re-shared clip will also be able to easily tap through to the full video to view the full surrounding context.

As per X: “The timestamp shows where the clip sits in the original video, such as 1:14/25:00.”

What’s more, both the creator and the sharing account will be credited with any impressions generated as a result of this process, ensuring that everyone gets an opportunity to earn from X’s Original Creator Rewards program.

This could be a valuable way to drive more video engagement and ensure that creators get more credit and exposure from re-shares of their content.

However, there are also some limitations.

At present, creators don’t have the option to turn off clipping for their videos. The only option to avoid unwanted clips is to delete the original video post, which will result in any clips generated from it also being removed from the app.

X also said that live broadcasts cannot be clipped yet.

However, the clipping tool is another means for X to build on video engagement as the platform looks to double down on what’s driving usage.

In 2023, X reported that video consumption was rapidly rising in the app, and said that it would try to capitalize on this shift with new video features.

It’s taken a couple of years, but in recent months, X has launched an updated livestream studio process, added new video overlay options and rolled out an updated video composer.

X also launched a Video Kickstart Bonus program last month, providing another means for video creators to get paid for their content.

Clipping adds yet another element, which could see more video content and related discussion flowing through X’s network.

X said that clipping is now available in both the iOS and Android versions of the app, with web access coming soon.

#7322. Reddit dangles seasonal ad credits but advertisers still want better proof it works all year round

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Reddit is offering advertisers extra incentives to spend in Q4 — the stretch of the year when budgets are biggest and competition for them is fiercest.

The social news website is offering new advertisers credits on new campaigns that go live before November 20: in the U.K., they get £500 for spending £1,000, or £2,000 for spending £2,000, according to an ad exec who recently spoke with their Reddit rep and shared an email confirming the incentives and deadline. In the U.S., the platform is also offering new advertisers $1,500 in ad credits when they spend $1,500 on the platform, per ads seen by Digiday.

After reviewing the incentives that Digiday had seen, Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha said that incentives like this “do work to reignite dormant clients’ interest to retry the platform.” However, following the decline in Google Search referrals, the general sentiment among his team’s clients is that Reddit isn’t as attractive without ChatGPT and Google sending it all that traffic.

The dip Divecha is referring to is the one Reddit disclosed on its earnings call last month when CEO Steve Huffman said October search referrals were down by more than one million a week on average, in part because of Google’s AI search features. To be clear, the credits aren’t tied to the slump. Q4 incentives are a fixture of the platform ad calendar and Reddit’s deadline for them is timed to get new campaigns running before Black Friday. That said, the extra spend could still help offset some of the hit from search, at least for a quarter.

Because incentives only go so far. Keeping advertisers around once the credits run out takes an ads platform that does what marketers need it to do, from measurement to targeting to self-serve tools. Reddit is still building toward all of that.

“Reddit is making big strides to compete with their competitor set within social with new capabilities, features across targeting, bidding and measurement but ultimately still comes in last place,” said independent ad tech consultant Jonathan D’Souza Rauto. “Advertisers do like to test the waters with it but it doesn’t necessarily translate in always-on budgets.”

The incentives come at the end of a year in which the rate of Reddit’s ad revenue growth has slowed down over three consecutive quarters. In Q1 2026, Reddit recorded $625 million in ad revenue, up 74% year over year, per the company’s earnings reports. By Q2, ad revenue had increased to $762 million, the rate of growth had slipped to 64% on the same period a year ago. By Q3 2026, the platform announced $847 million in ad revenue, with growth falling again to 54% versus the same period a year ago, per its latest earnings.

“The slowdown in ad revenue growth and referral traffic may be occurring at the same time, but that doesn’t necessarily mean one is driving the other,” said Jessica Dervyn, senior principal analyst in Gartner’s Marketing Practice.

Reddit, however, points to its continued revenue growth and advertiser demand as evidence that the platform remains an attractive proposition for marketers.

“The numbers speak for themselves: we’ve grown ad revenue by more than 50% year over year for nine straight quarters, well ahead of most peers over the same timeframe,” said Natalie Maguire, director of global B2B communications at Reddit in an emailed statement to Digiday. “We continue to see strong demand from advertisers as they increasingly turn to Reddit to drive results across the funnel. Our ad incentive program has stayed consistent over the last year and we have not increased our incentives amount. It’s an ongoing part of our business and a standard practice across the industry.”

It’s a thought not lost on some advertisers.

Take James Mortimer at Roast, for example. He said that his team finds the platform “really valuable” for advertisers because the users are so engaged.

“There’s quite a lot of people on there [Reddit] you can’t reach on Meta,” he said. Mortimer acknowledged that Reddit isn’t for every advertiser, but said it was “definitely valuable for the right advertisers that can work on there.”

However, audience engagement alone isn’t necessarily enough to convince advertisers to shift more of their budgets to Reddit. Measurement remains an important part of the equation.

“That’s something where Meta is really good now, so it’s harder to make the case for a client who is spending on Meta already to then take budget and put it into another channel [like Reddit], which might not be as effective,” said Mortimer.

New Gen’s strategy director Mark Bellamy had a similar take, saying that search referrals are part of the story but not the main thing holding back ad spend on Reddit. The bigger picture, he said, is the ad product.

“Reddit is still a test-and-learn platform for us, not somewhere we’d confidently put full-funnel budget,” he said. “You can get good CPMs and decent awareness. What we haven’t seen enough of is proof that you can build an ecosystem there that drives quality traffic and nurtures it. Until we do, we’ll be cautious about recommending significant media spend.”

That puts the onus on Reddit to demonstrate that advertisers can get meaningful returns from the platform beyond the initial incentive. While promotional offers can bring new advertisers through the door, converting those trials into long-term investment is a different challenge entirely.

Long gone are the days when LinkedIn was just a job-searching site.

#7319. Meta publishes explainer on Your Algorithm controls

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The feature enables users to opt in or out of certain topics and customize their feeds based on personal preferences.

Meta has shared some new insights into how its Your Algorithm control option on Facebook works, which enables users to provide direct input into what content they want to see more or less of in the app.

The tool enables users to add or remove specific topics from an automatically generated list of interests, which Facebook's system has built based on each user's in-app actions. In addition to removing and adding topics, users can also now tell the algorithm specifically what they want to see more of, via a recently added chatbot prompt.

Conceptually, this gives users more ways to refine their Facebook experience.

On the Meta engineering blog, the company has shared notes on how the process works, and explained more about how exactly updating user algorithm preferences can impact in-app experiences.

In addition to these topic-related notes, Meta said the system also used demotion rules to enact more general parameters that don’t relate to a specific topic.

“Quality-related requests, such as reducing AI-generated or sexually suggestive content, map to dedicated demotion signals rather than topic matching,” Meta said.

These demotion rules work differently than topic ranking, with the system using these as indicators to reduce these elements.

“During ranking, the same embedding-based comparison used in the relevance check removes candidates too close to an interest in Not Interested In, and demotion rules push down near matches,” Meta said.

In other words, this feature can reduce the presence of artificial intelligence-generated content in a user’s Facebook feed, though it won’t get rid of that entirely, and ranking will still be influenced by future engagement.

When a user requests less content from a specific creator, however, “that creator’s content is filtered out directly,” Meta said.

These notes provide more insight into how Meta plans to facilitate algorithm customization and give users more control over their in-app experiences.

Meta’s hope is that through tools like this, users will feel more empowered in the app. In turn, that may quell ongoing calls for the platform to return to a reverse chronological feed and remove ranking elements entirely.

Given that algorithmic amplification has continued to drive up Facebook and Instagram usage, Meta is very keen to keep that in place.

Though the company may not have a choice. Last month, the Australian government began preliminary investigations into potential new laws that would force social media platforms to provide users with an option to switch off algorithmic recommendations and set non-algorithmic feeds as the default.

A growing body of research suggests that algorithmic amplification can have harmful impacts. If this new push in Australia goes ahead and it ends up showing that the negative impacts of algorithms are reduced, that could lead to more regions exploring similar rules.

As noted, Meta is very keen to avoid this outcome.

Last year on a Q2 earnings call with analysts, Meta CEO Mark Zukerberga said AI recommendations had led to a 5% increase in time spent on Facebook, and a 6% boost on Instagram for the quarter.

On its Q2 earnings call a year later, Zuckerberg said that global time spent on Instagram had grown in the quarter by double digits year over year, and Facebook video time spent had increased by 9% globally, "largely driven by improvements to our feed and Reels recommendations."

It makes sense, then, that Meta is keen to avoid any external push to switch off its algorithmic ranking.

As such, Meta's hoping that the introduction of this control option will ease user concerns. Combine this with the fact that, based on past history, users rarely update their settings, and the company's likely hoping that it can benefit from the perception of control afforded by the tool, while also seeing a minor impact on its business.

#7317. The case for and against AI token capping

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

When one of the world’s most valuable companies starts to get serious about AI waste, it’s time to pay attention. Apple is placing a hard cap of $200 on its engineers’ daily AI token use, and may reduce non-engineers’ token allowance to $25, Bloomberg reported last week (Nov. 4).

A handful of ad agencies have already embraced token capping this year — using AI software, or a bespoke tool, to prevent users from consuming more than a set amount of AI compute as the costs of AI transformation become clearer. Apple’s policy change indicates that more companies could follow that path, but there are tradeoffs involved in this strict approach to costs.

Agencies considering capping token usage must weigh up the risk they could douse their employees’ creativity by limiting their access to the tech.

AI costs — from tokens to licensing and subscriptions — are likely as cheap as they’ll ever be. While manageable today, they’re kept low because of the enormous capital expenditures of companies like Meta, OpenAI and Anthropic. Eventually, those firms will want a return on those investments, meaning today’s prices might not be stable for long.

With that in mind, marketing agencies are building the infrastructure of AI accountability today, rather than wait for a crisis. Performance agency Roast, for example, has capped the amount of AI tokens its staffers can use since March.

“It’s up to the individual to use those tokens responsibly, and I think the benefit of that is then it’s making people think about what use cases they use tokens for,” John Campbell, head of innovation and AI at Roast, told Digiday.

The company allots a set amount of tokens to each employee within its Claude subscription, Campbell said. Higher individual caps can be provided at particularly busy times. He noted that the limit had stopped some users from relying on the tech for repetitive tasks that might be better delegated to non-AI software tools.

Video and image production generally represents the most token-intensive activity for creative agencies; media agencies experimenting with agentic tools for planning and buying must also grapple with runaway operating costs.

U.S. media agency PMG places a $50 daily limit on staff AI usage through its Alli4U portal. Staff that hit the cap more than once can be assigned a larger token budget, or receive advice on using different AI models, Dillon Larberg, consulting and strategy director, told Digiday in September. “That provides us a human in the loop moment to meet with teams or meet with individuals,” he said.

While agencies might still want to allow staff the flexibility to experiment and play with AI tools they can use tiered capping systems to provide teams with enough leeway to pilot something new, without burning up the budget.

“If you say you have a limit of $30, $25, $10 a day, you’ll find people become very creative in how they get this stuff done based on that constraint,” said Jonathan Whiteside, global executive vp at Dept, which caps team AI usage rather than individual costs.

“Constraints are sometimes useful for us to find new ways of working,” Whiteside added. “People start to think much more carefully about, ‘Well, do I actually need to use AI to do this?’”

AI costs, including the price of tokens, won’t stay low forever, so agencies should make hay while the sun’s shining. Intentionally cutting back before compute costs rise would be like abstaining from MoviePass or WeWork in the glorious days of zero interest rates. Agencies can chew through tokens while they develop the best uses for AI tech now, while it’s cheap to do so.

Some agencies use soft AI caps, rather than a hard limit. “When someone maxes out their spend for that tier, they show their work and their output to their department head,” explained Daniel Gilbert, CEO of Brainlabs, in September. He described the company’s approach as one that embraced “checkpoints” rather than caps. “The department head goes through the work with them, and if it’s good work, then they push them into the next tier.”

Such an approach preserves staff autonomy while keeping a lid on AI spending for agencies that want to to let a hundred flowers, or AI agents, bloom.

There are also means of nudging staff into cost-conscious AI decisions, without placing concrete limits on their usage. Gateway systems, or “gating,” refers to a tool which reroutes a user from a token-intensive AI model to a less powerful (and cheaper) model, if it’s better suited to a user’s project. If they’re using ChatGPT to draft their LinkedIn posts, they probably don’t need Astra, its latest and most powerful model.

Whiteside said that model selection is “a big factor” in keeping costs down, but that gating systems or staff training can provide a more appropriate guardrail than an individual token allowance.

As the real costs of AI-enabled marketing no services become clearer, expect more agencies and brands to consider whether hard caps or flexibility are most important.

Long gone are the days when LinkedIn was just a job-searching site.

#7316. X experiments with expanded AI transparency labels

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company is testing an automated way to detect and tag posts that may have been generated using artificial intelligence.

X is developing new AI transparency labels for posts, which will not only indicate whether AI was used to generate an update, but also how it was applied in each instance.

Michael O’Herlihy, X’s head of brand safety, shared some examples of these new labels during a presentation at the Brand & AI Safety Summit in New York on Tuesday.

As shown on these example screens, posted by Adweek senior tech reporter Kendra Barnett on X, X’s new AI content labels will include more detailed information on why a post was flagged for AI content. This will better explain the rationale X’s automated enforcement system is using, while also giving creators more opportunities to refute these labels by offering them a more detailed understanding of any potential concerns.

The hope is that these expanded notes will help X refine its AI detection process by prompting more creators to provide feedback on the specifics. This information can then be used to train the system.

At the same time, the tags will add more transparency for users, who may be less likely to re-share a post that’s been flagged for AI content.

O’Herlihy added that X increased its reliance on automated tagging of AI violations because it’s much faster. The automation should also ensure that more harmful content is refuted in-stream.

It could be a good way to enhance transparency in the AI era. However, false positives could also be problematic for creators, who could get annoyed if their content is unfairly tagged.

#7312. OpenAI’s new business agents give brands a free way into the conversation it’s also selling as an ad

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The sponsored link had a good run. Its successor might talk back.

OpenAI certainly thinks so. Businesses can now claim a profile in ChatGPT through a new Business Hub, get verified and feed in product and company details. In the U.S. (for now), e-commerce and online services businesses can go a step further and publish a business agent – a separately labelled chat that speaks for the brand once a user wants to dig into a product. OpenAI’s vp of monetization Benji Shomair declined to share a timeline for further rollout.

He did, however, clarify that the business agent is the free, organic way into a format Digiday reported on in September, when OpenAI started testing it as a paid ad.

“Think of the business agent as the organic experience,” Shomair said during a press briefing. “The sponsored agent is the paid experience, but the agent that sits behind it should be the same.”

So, OpenAI has built two routes into the same conversation for brands, and only one of them costs money. One through ChatGPT’s organic recommendations, and the other via paid placements.

Shomair said early testing of sponsored agents had shown “promising results,” particularly in the depth of conversations between consumers and businesses.

“When a consumer chooses to engage more deeply with our business, there’s a high level of intent there,” he said.

Shomair described typical consumer use cases as working through product choices, weighing up size, colour and price before exploring related purchases. For businesses, that could result in turning an initial product enquiry into a bigger basket, and ultimately a deeper customer relationship.

When the shopper is ready to pay, the agent waves them off to the brand’s own site, because “it’s up to the business on how they want to close the transaction,” said Shomair.

Whatever got said before that point stays with OpenAI. Brands hoping to read back their agents’ transcripts get themes instead.

“The only insight provided today are the ones we sort of shared there initially, which are the themes of the conversations where you’re appearing,” Shomair said.

OpenAI keeps hold of the agent itself too. It’s built from the instructions and product feed a business supplies with no room for an in-house version. “We aren’t allowing folks to bring their own agents,” said Shomair.

The rest of the Business Hub sits under Insights, the the analytics dashboard-type product that Digiday reported on this week — the beta shows mentions, views and website clicks, plus discovery topics.

“Those are common themes in conversations where ChatGPT cites the business’s site,” an OpenAI spokesperson confirmed to Digiday. “Businesses don’t see individual prompts or chats. Share of voice isn’t part of this beta.”

How mentions are tracked depends on the type of business. For local businesses, a mention counts when ChatGPT shows a result for that location. For an online business, it counts when ChatGPT references the business’s website.

OpenAI declined to say how long mentions are tracked. An ad exec with access to Insights within the Business Hub did share that the chart within the feature shows everything within the past seven or 28 days.

Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha said that his team has been reliant on tools like Peec,.AI, Otterly.AI or Semrush to guesstimate brand visibility on ChatGPT as well as other AI platforms. But he noted that they are all using approximations from Google search data, third-party traffic estimators and their own data modeling tools, and none have access to original data from the source.

“Now that OpenAI is making their own mentions data available, we’re very interested to see how far off these tools were from the actual data from ChatGPT, and whether these tools will be able to adapt and incorporate the OpenAI data,” Divecha said. “If their estimation data was wildly erroneous, that will make us rethink paying for those tools.”

The implications extend beyond the accuracy of individual tools. OpenAI’s decision to share its own data could also shift the balance of power in the growing AI visibility industry. As Sonata Insights’ founder and chief analyst Debra Aho Williamson pointed out, the entire AI visibility (AEO/GEO) industry is built on third-party research (and some guesswork) about what helps a brand show up in an answer.

“The fact that OpenAI will now share insights itself will take some of the power away from the third-party companies,” she said. “But OpenAI isn’t going to share everything it knows, and ChatGPT is only one of several AI platforms, so demand for what the AI visibility companies offer will remain strong.”

Business Hub and agents are free for now but Shomair didn’t rule out a paid model in the future.

“In terms of where they develop in the future, I think it’ll depend on the directions that they go,” he said.

What none of it buys is a leg-up in ChatGPT’s recommendations. The core product needs to“maintain objectivity,” said Shomair, claiming a business “should not have an impact on how frequently it appears.”

That doesn’t mean the information is irrelevant to visibility though. Shomair said it could help ChatGPT surface a business in more relevant conversations since the platform would have a clearer picture of what that business offers.

Long gone are the days when LinkedIn was just a job-searching site.

Brands go from booking creators to green-lighting shows.

Mars readies for AI grocery agents by chasing LLM visibility and immersive stores as impulse buys shift. See what’s at stake.

#7310. Elon Musk’s Grok AI faces legal challenges over nudification

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Multiple plaintiffs allege that the chatbot is directly responsible for creating naked images based on photos taken of them as children, per Bloomberg.

Elon Musk’s xAI is facing questions regarding who is ultimately responsible for the generation of harmful content via generative artificial intelligence tools. At the heart of these issues is whether the fault lies with the person who tells the system to create this material, or the platform that facilitates this functionality.

These problems lie at the core of multiple legal cases involving xAI’s Grok chatbot, with plaintiffs in three U.S. states seeking legal recourse against xAI over the creation of CSAM material.

As reported by Bloomberg, xAI has come under investigation in three high-profile cases.

The company has denied responsibility in these cases, and said these depictions were generated by users, not by xAI itself.

Musk, xAI’s owner, has also made similar public statements.

In January, amid reports that Grok was being used to generate thousands of nudified images every day, Musk posted: “Anyone using Grok to make illegal content will suffer the same consequences as if they upload illegal content.”

However, shortly after making that announcement, and facing a potential ban of X apps from app stores, xAI changed its policies and usage restrictions on Grok to ensure that it could no longer be used to generate nudified images at scale.

The change in approach showed that xAI had the capacity to restrict this functionality whenever it wanted. However, the company instead initially refused to restrict Grok usage on free speech grounds, and settled on user autonomy as its legal defense.

Indeed, xAI has even challenged new laws designed to outlaw the generation of non-consensual naked images.

As reported by Reuters last month, xAI succeeded in blocking the implementation of Minnesota’s proposed ban on AI-generated naked images by arguing that the law was unconstitutional and infringed on free speech rights. The company was the only provider actively opposing this law.

At the same time, xAI has repeatedly touted Grok’s systematic capacity to generate what is called “spicy” content or offensive material in its “unhinged mode.” The company has also launched companion chatbots that can engage in NSFW chats.

As such, this does seem like more of a feature than a bug, yet xAI is hoping it can hang onto the fact that it’s not liable for usage that goes beyond certain stated parameters.

Establishing legal responsibility could set a new precedent in AI image generation, and it will be interesting to see whether any of these cases results in xAI being held responsible.

#7307. Future of TV Briefing: Inside creator Grace Wells’s 100-hour branded video production process

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at creator Grace Wells’s full production process, from initial brief to final delivery.

Picture the stereotypical sponsored video produced by a creator. You may be imagining someone holding up a product to their camera, hand behind the product to prevent their camera’s autofocus from blurring out said product, reciting a script about the product’s role in their everyday life and thanking the brand for partnering with them on this video.

Grace Wells (@gracewellsphoto) disproves that stereotype.

A creator who has attracted millions of followers across TikTok and Instagram, Wells represents how creator-produced branded videos have leveled up in production value. She spends, on average, 100 hours producing videos for brands and knocks out two to three branded videos per month. During Digiday’s recent Creator Strategies virtual event, she broke down her full production process, from initial brief to final delivery.

“It’s a very time-intensive process. I’m very big on pre-production, and that’s a question that I get a lot: is how much planning actually goes into these. There’s a lot of hours before I even get into the studio to film,” Wells said.

The first step in that process is joining a briefing call with the brand. Other creators may be satisfied with receiving a one-sheet telling them what the client is looking for. But Wells wants to have a conversation to understand the brand’s objective, its target audience and whether the video she is producing is meant to be tied to a larger marketing effort in case there are pre-existing brand assets or visual elements to incorporate.

“Because ultimately, I’m trying to create an asset that feels equally like me and like the brand, and that can be kind of a delicate balance to strike sometimes. Depending on how fleshed out the brief is and how much other content is being sourced for this particular campaign, I decide how many of those pre-existing elements I want to pull into my storyboarding and my ideation process,” said Wells.

Post-briefing call, Wells moves into her pre-production process. This stage can take roughly 30 hours. A pillar of Wells’s pre-production process is storyboarding. Every. Single. Shot.

“The perspective of the shot. How long that shot is going to last. Is there going to be voiceover or sound effects or text callouts that are happening?” said Wells.

She has adopted this extensive storyboarding process for two main reasons. First, it allows the brand to literally see what she intends to film and how she plans to edit it all together. Second, it allows Wells to head into filming on solid ground with minimal risk of the brand being dissatisfied when she hands in the final video and asking for reshoots.

“That can really mess me up and kind of create a domino effect in my workflow,” said Wells.

That domino effect is important to avoid because here’s the most crucial part of Wells’s production process to be aware of: She does everything by herself. That includes making custom newspapers in Photoshop (as she did for this recent video for Bayer), building sets, setting up lighting, prepping and styling food products and, of course, filming. Actually she effectively films two videos because the videos she produces for brands include the product showcase video as well as the behind the scenes of Wells making the product showcase video.

“The bulk of my time is spent on set filming, just because I am one person with two hands,” said Wells. She added, “every time I’m setting up a shot, I’m actually setting up two shots because I have the shot that I’m filming and then I have the behind the scenes that’s happening.”

A perk of filming solo is that it affords Wells the flexibility to film for as long as she wants, to take her time to experiment with shots and even to edit the video as she films it.

“I can film a shot then I can throw it into Premiere and edit it and that might inform decisions that I make about the following shot that I’m going to film,” Wells said.

Unsurprisingly then, post-production is the shortest stage of her process, typically taking around 20 hours to complete. She has her storyboards that effectively serve as a paper edit, so editing the video is a bit like coloring by numbers.

“Music is the very first thing that I choose, even before I’m storyboarding. So even decisions like that have been made at this point a very long time ago. And so it really is just putting the finishing touches on things,” she said.

This pre-production-heavy process helps Wells to avoid what can often be the bane of any creative client work: revisions.

“If there are any revisions, it’ll be simple things like, ‘Oh, actually let’s change the wording of this text,’ which is a very simple change, or ‘Let’s punch in on this so we can see details a little bit more.’ So I would say generally it’s like maybe three to four rounds of very small changes.”

“Exposure as a form of payment and with the intention of cutting into YouTube’s market share and cutting into the amount of views people are getting on YouTube — it’s just gross.”

359%: Percentage increase in daily AI retrieval of YouTube videos that have updated their descriptions.

495 million: Number of daily active users that Snap had at the end of Q3 2026.

21%: Percentage share of Gen Z survey respondents who think influencers rarely or never disclose paid deals.

#7304. LinkedIn tests frequent viewer feature

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Premium users already see who views their profile, but a new report from Wired said the platform may also soon show how often someone stops by.

LinkedIn users may soon be able to derive more value from its “Who viewed your profile” list, with the platform working on a new feature that would not only show who visited their profile, but also how often each specific user revisited their page within a given period.

That would add more context to LinkedIn’s profile viewer data, although for most users, this is fairly useless information. However, some marketers have highlighted this as a lead-generation opportunity, providing a list of potential warm contacts, based on the fact that they’ve checked out a user’s profile.

A frequency indicator on the same display would enhance that capacity, by showing people who have returned to a user’s profile in the app again and again, which could be an indicator of even stronger interest.

So long as that interest is in a professional capacity.

A visit count for each profile listed could also show users who could be interested in them for other reasons, which could make this a somewhat unsettling display.

LinkedIn users can lock down their profile to connections only, so there would be ways to address this concern, and of course users are under no obligation to contact people who’ve visited their profile.

Wired said that LinkedIn confirmed the feature is currently in testing, but it had no further info to share at this stage.

#7299. Thought leadership remains highly valuable for B2B buyers

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A new survey report from Edelman and LinkedIn found that brands value professional insight and experience from other professionals.

Edelman has published a new report which examines the impact of thought leadership content on LinkedIn, and how professionals are utilizing thought leader content within their decision-making process.

Based on insights from “more than 1,100 management-level professionals,” Edelman’s 2026 B2B Thought Leadership Impact Report provides notes on the shifting B2B landscape. In particular, it looks at the role professional expertise plays in the artificial intelligence era.

According to the report, that role is still significant.

Edelman’s data showed that 82% of buyers believe that thought leadership is becoming more valuable, with original research, insight and experience now being more of a differentiator.

The data also showed that 89% of buyers view high-quality thought leadership content “makes a company feel like a long-term partner worth choosing.”

What’s more, a brand that doesn’t share thought leadership content can risk being eliminated from consideration for some potential partners.

And while AI tools are now playing a bigger role in the discovery process, the data also showed that most buyers still validate AI recommendations by referring to a brand’s content.

Also worth noting: Buyers are more likely to share thought leadership content in private channels.

There are also some interesting notes on the value of external influencers when it comes to endorsing a business, and the importance of brands continuing to produce thought leadership content to reinforce existing partnerships.

In summary, while product research is changing, and more business buyers are using AI tools to find relevant products, original brand content still plays a key role in winning deals and assuring buyers that they’re making the right decision.

The key differentiation, then, is expertise and unique insight that nobody else can provide. Even in the age of AI, where digital systems can summarize information,  AI can’t provide real, hands-on experience and insights. It also can’t summarize information that doesn’t already exist.

Hence the need for more thought leadership-type content, and for brands to differentiate themselves through expanded sharing of their professional expertise.

#7298. TikTok Shop launches a paid premium tier for 1M of its top U.S. shoppers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The story was first exclusively published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

TikTok Shop has officially launched a paid membership pilot in the U.S. called TikTok Shop Plus in time for the competitive holiday season.

Over the weekend, TikTok Shop began offering the service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers. TikTok Shop Plus costs $9.99 a month after a 30-day free trial, according to TikTok Shop. It includes perks such as expanded free shipping, exclusive discounts, coupon bundles and instant refunds.

The program is TikTok Shop’s latest effort to grow retail sales in the U.S. as live commerce takes off outside of Asia. TikTok Shop launched in the U.S. in 2023 and now represents about 20% of the social-commerce market in the country, per Capital One Shopping. With TikTok Shop Plus, the platform is offering extra perks similar to other paid membership programs like Amazon Prime ($14.99 a month) and Walmart+ ($12.95 a month). Business Insider first reported that TikTok Shop was testing a version of the program in July.

“TikTok Shop Plus is currently being piloted with an expanding group of eligible U.S. users,” Patrick Nommensen, president of strategic initiatives at TikTok Shop, said in a statement to Modern Retail. “As we gradually scale access to the pilot, we’ll continue to gather feedback and refine the experience, with the goal of delivering more value and convenience to shoppers.”

TikTok Shop Plus members have access to multiple perks. First, they can get free shipping on eligible items marked with a “Plus” badge on the product page. Plus members also receive an extra 10% off select products. In addition, each month, Plus members will get three coupons for a range of products. The deals are: $5 off with no minimum spend, $10 off orders of $59 or more, and $30 off orders of $119 or more.

What’s more, for qualified products, members can get an instant refund (up to two times per month), issued as store credit to their TikTok Shop balance before they drop off the return. For TikTok Shop’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday period — which kicks off Wednesday — TikTok Shop Plus members will receive additional exclusive coupons and deeper discounts on select items. The app will display specific offers.

TikTok Shop has high hopes for the holidays this year. Notably, 2026 will be the first holiday season when big names like Best Buy, Ulta Beauty and Sephora will be selling products on TikTok Shop, meaning that shoppers will have more major retail players to choose from than in any previous year. And, this year, shoppers can browse and buy holiday gifts with the help of TikTok Shop’s new AI assistant. Shoppers can also now buy directly in TikTok’s For You feed, without leaving the app.

Nearly a third of U.S. TikTok users expect to buy a gift this Black Friday, per TikTok Shop data. Like previous years, live shopping and limited-edition products will be front and center in TikTok Shop’s holiday strategy. During the 2025 four-day Black Friday and Cyber Monday weekend, TikTok Shop’s sales surpassed $500 million, and sellers hosting livestreams saw 84% year-over-year sales growth across more than 760,000 sessions, per a press release.

Americans spend about $43 million per day shopping on TikTok, according to Capital One Shopping. Many of these purchases are health and beauty products, but the platform continues to expand into new categories every year, including big-ticket items like comforters and above-ground pools. Today, TikTok Shop is becoming so crucial to some brands’ businesses that many, including Harry’s and Stila Cosmetics, are creating dedicated positions for heads of TikTok Shop.

TikTok Shop U.S. is now home to retail companies of various sizes, including Vera Bradley, True Religion, Beachwaver and Mellow Sleep. Last month, PF Global’s TikTok’s Economic Impact Report found that economic activity on TikTok helped generate $81 billion in GDP for U.S. businesses in 2025, and that 54 million Americans purchased a product after watching a TikTok video last year. Earlier this month, TechCrunch reported news of a standalone TikTok Shop app in select markets like Singapore, Malaysia and Thailand.

“TikTok Shop is no longer experimental,” Nommensen said at a summit in October. “It’s now a key component to these brands’ e-commerce strategy, and we’re really seeing that shift accelerate as time goes by.”

#7293. Product Owner - Innovation Team (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is the maker of Imunify360 — a suite of security products protecting millions of websites across the web hosting industry — and of CloudLinux OS, the operating system behind a large share of the world's shared hosting. To learn more about our company and products, visit our websites: cloudlinux.com and imunify360.com. Alongside those established product lines we run a small innovation team whose job is to find and prove the next ones.

We are looking for a Product Owner to lead that team: a small, senior, fully remote squad of engineers that takes new product concepts from idea to a working MVP in customers' hands, then hands proven products to a product line and moves on to the next concept. You will be accountable for the pipeline of ideas, the speed and rigour with which they are tested, and the evidence behind every go / no-go recommendation.

This is a hands-on lead role, not pure people management: roughly 50% discovery and leadership (customer conversations, scoping, prioritisation, coaching, reporting to the CPO) and 50% building (prototypes, spikes, design and code review, demo stands). You will not be the team's only builder, but you must be able to build a credible prototype yourself and hold a technical opinion about every part of it.

You don't need to arrive as an expert in web hosting or security. If you have taken a product from nothing to its first paying customers before — as a founder, an early engineer, or the lead of an incubation team — and you get deep into unfamiliar technical territory fast, we will teach you the market.

The innovation team sits across CloudLinux and Imunify and owns the product concepts that do not yet have a home in an established product line. Ideas come from customer conversations, industry events, telemetry across more than 80,000 servers and the CPO’s strategy; the team’s job is to turn them into something a customer will pay for within a quarter, using AI coding agents throughout.

We evaluate engineers partly on how effectively they work with coding agents, and in the innovation team this is the main lever on speed. As the lead you model and coach it: prompt specificity, review quality on agent-generated code, context provision, iteration and error recovery, and end-to-end task completion. Expect to use agents to build prototypes, generate test corpora and draft customer-facing material.

#7292. Mars wants to future proof impulse buys as AI is poised to take over more grocery runs

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

When AI does the grocery shopping, what happens to the impulse snack purchase at checkout? That’s the question Mars is grappling with.

The conglomerate, which owns snack brands like Dove chocolate, M&Ms and Pop-Tarts, is hedging its bets against an agentic future. Neil Reynolds, global chief customer officer at Mars, doesn’t expect AI to kill the in-store checkout lane, but he expects that agentic shopping assistants will take over more of the weekly grocery run. At least that’s the case based on Mars’ recent future of shopping and retail report penned in partnership with Kantar and Synthesis.

Digiday caught up with Reynolds to discuss how Mars is trying to sell products to AI, the upshot of AI visibility and the future of impulse purchases in an agentic landscape.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity.

For us, the road map sits against those two parallel parts… how can we build the right capabilities internally to make sure our categories and our brand really shine in agentic commerce. That means, how do we make sure our brands are discoverable by the LLMs and how do we make sure that once those brands are discovered, that they convert through to purchase? The second milestone plan, or the second road map, is around, how do we start to step into building these experiential, immersive retail destinations for our stores? How do we partner with our retailers to think about, in effect, creating different cultural environments, and building a different experience when you step into a store?

The first thing we’re trying to do is sort of get some real data around what’s the start point for how our categories and how our brands show up. What that looks like for us is trying to get data through partnerships with emerging tech companies to get a start point for how we can show up in the LLM. What’s the data that, in effect, is showing up and where’s that data being sourced from?

Part of this genuinely is about connectivity between the teams. We’re setting up what we call One Demand performance squads — which are, again, how do you get the brand manager next to the insights person next to the key account manager next to the category manager, and the agency partner?

Winning this consumer journey through effortless, personalized and experiential, will be within the Publicis functionality or contract as well now. Our historical relationships are more been around media and creative, and what we’ve now got with Publicis is an end-to-end agency partner who can also support us with commerce. We can unlock some of the data that they have. They talk about connected ID being part of their offer, and then we can ideate to build those effortless, personalized experiential shopper experiences.

Back in the day, we would have a salesperson from Mars going to see a commercial buyer from a retailer. But we’ve quite quickly worked out that if we want to be strategic partners, we need to bring more people to that conversation. For example, how can we have a tech-to-tech conversation. Why is our data team not talking to the data team at Walmart?

We have ongoing conversations with many of the key leading retailers around, call it “store of the future initiatives” — and how we can ideate around continuing to create even more immersive and engaging shopping experiences for snacking in their stores.

#7290. Why Rao’s heated up brand building with a request to slow down meals

The Campbell Company sauce brand reimagines the concept of a limited-time offer by keying in on how many meals people have left with loved ones.

Rao’s Homemade today revealed a four-minute short film that reimagines the concept of a limited-time offer by reminding consumers that the most limited time is the time people spend together, per details shared with Marketing Dive. “Limited-Time Only” was directed by Aqsa Altaf and John X. Carey, the duo known as Aqsa + John X. who were behind Dove’s “Real Beauty Sketches” campaign.

“Limited-Time Only” shares the story of three individuals as they are interviewed about how often they have dinner with family. The heart-tugging video culminates with each being presented a jar of Rao’s sauce labeled with an estimate of how many meals they have left with certain family members before being surprised with a dinner with their loved ones.

The short film is part of a larger “Make Time” campaign created with Goodby Silverstein & Partners that spans premium video, social, influencer, public relations, retail media and owned channels.

In a survey of more than 1,000 individuals and in-depth interviews with 200 people, the brand and agency learned that 84% of Americans wish they shared more meals with loved ones, yet only one-third of families actually sit down together. The campaign unites those findings with product truths about the time-intensive way Rao’s sauce is made, from hand-picked tomatoes and hand-peeled onions and garlic that are simmered for hours.

“It's not just about the food; It's about the experience that creating that food enables, and it's an opportunity to really slow down and savor the food and to savor those that you're sitting around the table with, and to make meal time less of a transaction and more of an experience and an opportunity to connect with others,” said Chris Tutor, vice president of advertising and design at Rao’s parent The Campbell Company.

That experience was captured in a series of ads that feature groups of people enjoying a meal and include taglines like “the best part of the day is sharing it” and “keep long stories long.” The spots are set to live orchestral music and end with a scarpetta, the Italian tradition of using bread to mop up the last of the sauce. The creation of those spots led the team to investigate the larger narrative around meals with loved ones that informed “Limited-Time Only.”

“The long-form [video] allowed us to tell more of that story from the impetus for it to the aha moment to then the celebratory moment, and it really allowed us to blow out that visceral moment around the table into the full story,” Tutor said.

While “Limited-Time Only” does not have a traditional ad length, the creative is in line with a trend around longer-form content that marketers have adopted to tell more engaging stories to consumers. Depending on the platform, the brand will either run the long-form asset or teasers that point to the full video.

“There's been a lot of media attention around this idea, and so we’re leveraging media partners to help tell the story,” Tutor said. “Setting up the context before watching the film will also, we think, drive a lot of eyeballs to it. We're trying to do the math and science of making sure the right piece of content shows up on the right channel at the right time.”

The Campbell Company acquired Rao’s parent Sovos Brands for $2.7 billion deal in March 2024. Rao’s is a billion-dollar brand for Campbell’s and continues to grow, with consumption up 11.3% in fiscal year 2026. To fuel this growth, Rao’s increased media spending and will further increase it in fiscal 2027, executives said on a recent earnings call.

While Rao’s has grown household penetration, it still lags behind sister brand Prego. The “Make Time” campaign is key to the brand’s marketing efforts and represents both a brand building story and an awareness play.

“As people become aware of the brand or if they're already aware of the brand, what does the brand stand for beyond just that exceptional food? It's bringing this notion of slow living to every meal, reminding people that life tastes better when it's savored, and establishing what the brand means on an emotional level, versus just a rational level,” Tutor said.

#7288. How a sports betting creator drove 200 million short-form views of his podcast

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series examining successful creator business strategies. More from the series →

After some trial and error, Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has determined how to toe the content line. The sports betting analyst and commentator has nearly 2 million Instagram followers, and a Draft Kings-sponsored podcast, Double Coverage, that boasts millions of views across YouTube and TikTok.

But not long ago, one of Hirsch’s biggest videos (both in terms of views and in terms of guests, as it was an interview with fellow creator-turned-boxer Jake Paul) was taken off YouTube, reportedly because it was sponsored by an offshore betting company Hirsch wouldn’t name, rather than one of the more mainstream platforms like Draft Kings, which is vetted by Google and available state-by-state depending on local gambling laws. The offshore sportsbooks exist in a sort of legal gray area — they’re unlicensed in the U.S., but federal law targets the operators and payment processors rather than the bettors themselves. Still, the takedown threw his content approach into relief and forced him to take a step back.

“That was really scary,” said Hirsch. He said the company wasn’t an approved Google vendor, which seemingly prompted the strike, but didn’t provide details.

This spring, Hirsch found that partner in Draft Kings, which he calls an “anchor sponsor.” There’s exclusivity, but only in the category of sportsbooks and prediction markets, so he’s free to get other partnerships, and the increased social acceptance of legal betting brands has made it fairly easy for him to find new partners, including direct-to-consumer telehealth and online pharmacy brand Hims. He did not say how much his partnerships are worth — or how long they will last.

“Sports betting hasn’t been a notable flag for brands when they vet creators,” said Mat Micheli, co-CEO and co-founder of Viral Nation. “Betting is now deeply ingrained in the culture of the brands that deem it important.”

However, Micheli stressed the importance of partnering with licensed, regulated vendors to avoid what happened to Hirsch last year.

“There are regulated multinational operators in other parts of the world that are legitimate and licensed, just not in the United States. For American creators speaking to an American audience, the standard has to be simple: you only promote something that is lawful to use in your audience’s own country. That’s the line that separates a brand-safe partnership from a risky one,” he said.

Hirsch’s creator career is deeply entwined with the controversial world of sports betting. He signed a contract with BetOnline as soon as he turned 18, and spent nearly five years with them, co-hosting a sports prediction podcast with former NFL player Adam “Pacman” Jones. At the time, however, he was just a paid host, and not involved in the show’s day-to-day production or marketing efforts.

Hirsch decided he wanted to take a more hands-on approach for his next podcast endeavor.

“I saw a lot of soft spots and inconsistencies that could be improved, especially as a Gen Zer,” he said. The biggest soft spot for him was the lack of reliance on other social media platforms to drive discovery. “Eighty-five percent of Gen Z sports podcast listeners are introduced through social media, which is clips,” Hirsch said, referencing a 2024 Edison Research report.

He leveraged his existing connections in the professional sports and entertainment worlds, which he built with a different podcast, Kiss and Myst, co-hosted by rap legend Jadakiss (Hirsh said the podcast was sponsored by World Star Hiphop for seven figures). These connections helped ensure Double Coverage would have an impressive slate of guests, from WWE wrestler Cody Rhodes to boxing legend Mike Tyson, as well as a regular slate of NFL players and UFC fighters, and leaned hard into circulating clips on TikTok and YouTube.

“We’ve had over 200 million views on short-form content since the NFL season began,” Hirsh said. Since July, the podcast has seen steady, impressive growth, with 151 million cumulative views on TikTok, and 43.7 million on TikTok, per the platform’s statistics shared with Digiday.

“I’ve gone all in on short-form content and identifying what resonates most,” Hirsh said. That includes shaving off a half-second or word to make the clip more punchy and hiring a professional designer for thumbnails. In its second season, Hirsch pivoted to make the podcast more fast-paced so it can easily be cut up into clips.

“There’s no room to breathe; it’s like you’re watching one continuous TikTok clip, which is what resonates with our audience,” he said.

For Hirsh, short-form video is not just a promotional channel; it shapes how the show is made. He says the team edits clips tightly, invests in thumbnails, and has made the second season faster-paced so moments can be cut for social platforms.

Double Coverage is also looking beyond the screen, with plans for more experiential programming and in-person events. Hirsh said his ability to “set up anywhere, travel anywhere, fit into any crowd” could help make that possible. He doesn’t have an official NFL partnership, but he says he is, “in talks” and plans on having a “massive media day presence” for the second year in a row.

“I’d love to get 100 million views a week on short-form content and get over a million views a podcast episode,” he said. “I’d like to have four anchor sponsors that believe in me and are committed long term that I can build around.”

#7287. Priceline’s AI agent Penny lands in Snapchat ads amid holiday rush

The brand is the first in the travel category to use AI Sponsored Snaps, which allow advertisers to bring their own chatbots into Snapchat.

Priceline is trying out Snapchat’s AI Sponsored Snaps in the ramp up to a holiday season when travel is expected to be more expensive due to higher fuel costs and stubborn inflation. Average domestic round-trip airfare for Thanksgiving was up 20% year over year on Priceline as of late October, Marketing Dive’s sister publication Hotel Dive recently reported. As consumers try to stretch their dollars further, more are turning to AI tools, including personal agents, to map out their trips and find deals.

Priceline has been shoring up a brand positioning around combatting soaring travel costs. In May, it brought back its Priceline Negotiator character in ads that showed William Shatner, who played the role in memorable TV commercials from the 2000s, passing the baton to Randall Park.

The Snapchat campaign is a way for Priceline to build awareness and adoption for Penny, a conversational AI tool that the brand revamped in June. Penny at the time added capabilities from Anthropic’s Claude models as part of a transition to becoming more “fully agentic,” including the ability to handle more complex user queries and surface real-time pricing comparisons.

Snapchatters who open Priceline’s promoted Snap message and engage with Penny will be able to seek out inspiration for travel destinations, develop an itinerary and identify places to stay. Those details will be preserved for consumers who then jump to Priceline to complete their travel booking. Penny converts users at approximately twice the rate of non-Penny users on Priceline, according to the company.

Snapchat introduced AI Sponsored Snaps in April, allowing advertisers to bring their own chatbots into a high engagement chat inbox interface on the ephemeral messaging app. The format is an iteration of Sponsored Snaps, a popular ad product that drives about 22% more conversions and 20% lower cost per action compared to other inventory on the platform.

Snapchat sees a bigger opportunity in the travel category, as two-thirds of surveyed users have added a destination to their wishlist after viewing it on Snapchat. Eighty-seven percent of Snapchat users make at least one trip for leisure annually, higher than non-users of the service, per press materials.

Snapchat for the past few years has been reworking its ad suite to be more centered on performance marketing solutions that can produce business results. The app has recently seen an exodus of advertising executives while its rate of ad revenue growth has fallen short of expectations, including due to declines in upper-funnel ad products. Snapchat’s revenue derived from ads increased 4% in Q3 while total revenue was up 14% for the period.

“While advertising revenue growth remains below our ambitions, advertisers are seeing the benefits of our performance improvements,” Snap CEO Evan Spiegel said in prepared remarks ahead of the company’s Q3 earnings earlier this month. “Our focus now is to turn these improvements into broader adoption, more meaningful share of advertiser budgets and more durable growth.”

#7286. True Talent Solutions Partners: Strategic Project Lead, Legal (J.D.)

Lead end-to-end legal and law-related projects for customers at an AI company. You will translate customer needs into actionable specifications and coordinate Operations and legal experts to build and deliver high-quality data workflows.

Own projects from initial scoping and pipeline design through execution and delivery.

Translate customer requirements into clear specifications for legal workflows.

Design and improve domain-specific pipelines for usability, accuracy, quality, and scalability.

Coordinate Operations teams and legal domain experts to support rapid, high-quality delivery.

Set milestones, establish execution cadence, and track progress against delivery goals.

Manage client communications and provide regular internal updates on progress, blockers, and next steps.

Lead legal experts and project leads to maintain project quality and delivery standards.

A J.D. or equivalent law degree, with at least 2 years of experience in legal practice, legal research, regulatory or compliance work, or a related field.

At least 2 years leading complex projects, operational workflows, or legal initiatives.

Experience translating customer needs into project specifications, designing specialized workflows, and managing projects from scoping through delivery.

Strong communication and stakeholder management skills, especially with attorneys and senior legal experts.

Experience managing legal experts or project leads and coordinating client communications, milestones, and execution.

Familiarity with legal research, contract analysis, litigation, regulatory or compliance workflows, or other complex legal work.

Experience with AI or machine learning companies, data annotation or evaluation, data pipelines, AI training workflows, or human-in-the-loop operations is valuable.

Strong ownership, attention to detail, and an execution-focused approach in a fast-paced environment.

Base salary is $180,000 to $230,000 USD annually. The role includes equity eligibility and may include performance-based bonuses. Visa sponsorship is not available.

Remote position. The role is open to candidates nationally.

#7283. TwitchCon is at a crossroads as creators question its value

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

TwitchCon remains a draw for brands and streaming companies, but some creators say the cost, safety concerns and uncertain payoff have made the annual convention harder to justify.

Ahead of TwitchCon 2026’s kick off this weekend in San Diego, allegations spread on X that Twitch was giving away tickets, and that ticket counts for Twitch Partners, the top-performing, monetized creators, were the lowest they’d been in years. Though Twitch wouldn’t outright say attendance was down, a spokesperson told Digiday that “events have changed a lot in the past few years,” after the post-COVID “crazy high demand.” They confirmed the company was giving away tickets through their Ambassador program, which it has done in previous years.

Digiday spoke with six creators and two brands showing up at the convention about what this might signal for the streaming platform’s biggest annual event and the state of the streaming creator economy.

“I don’t feel like we have a clear enough understanding of what TwitchCon is today and how I could add value to its programming by being there,” said Justin Miclat, chief growth officer at talent and brand management company Click Media. Miclat also founded the Kinetic Group talent agency, which has worked with live streamers. Last year, the group spent $50,000 producing a TwitchCon event promoting and showcasing women in the industry.

TwitchCon, the IRL event for the livestream platform Twitch, which accrued 900 million hours of watch time last year, has historically been a place where creators connect with fans, sponsors and their peers. Past and present sponsors include State Farm, e.l.f. Cosmetics, Streamlabs, Southwest Airlines, Honda, and the platform’s parent company, Amazon Prime. Creators often spend the weekend seeking out new partnerships opportunities.

Recently, however, TwitchCon (which is typically held in California every year along with a European counterpart) has faced scrutiny from attendees around security issues, rising costs, less opportunities and a lack of clarity around what the platform considers its core community. Top creator Emily-Beth “Emiru” Schunk was assaulted during a meet-and-greet at TwitchCon 2025, prompting backlash and calls for tighter security (and a statement from Twitch). Others pointed to a rash of IRL streamers’ bad behavior while livestreaming at or around the San Diego Convention Center.

Mike Murphy O’Reilly, chief revenue officer at Dexerto, a gaming publisher that is also a full-service creative and marketing agency, said TwitchCon is still a “core event” for the company. “It’s one of the few events across the year where you have a mix of both endemic and non-endemic clients,” he said. Still, he noted that this year’s event “does feel quieter.”

For companies like Streamlabs, which offers a streaming platform regularly used by Twitch creators, the convention is still a must-attend and a must-sponsor. The company sets up content creation stations in the convention center, and creates TwitchCon-themed overlays for livestreams.

“At TwitchCon the percentage of attendees that are actually streamers is generally around 50 percent,” said Ashray Urs, head of Streamlabs. “For us, making broadcast software, that’s a great place to be.” Urs said the convention’s value lies in having so many streamers in one place to get consumer feedback and build new partnerships, as well as meeting key existing brand partners like NVIDIA.

“It’s a perfect endemic fit for us,” Urs said.

But is TwitchCon the perfect fit for creators?

Streamer Tevin “YourBudTevin” Lolar, who has over 50,000 followers on Twitch, told Digiday that creators attending the convention usually fall into one of three categories: low-level streamers looking to learn more and make connections, mid-level streamers hoping to connect with brands, and top streamers, who host events and establish “big money partnerships and deliverables.”

TwitchCon began as an IRL event focused on leveraging its tentpole creators, But as major stars like Kai Cenat and Darren Jason “IShowSpeed” Watkins Jr. have moved beyond the need for the convention, Twitch may have to redefine how it serves creators and fans.

“Twitch needs to hone in on the community-driven streamers and bring them up in order to maintain the health of the convention,” Lolar said. The Twitch spokesperson said the platform wants TwitchCon to reflect the growing community of streamers, while ensuring “gaming is still the heart” of the event.

For mid-tier creators like Veronika “Nikatine” Ripley, a Twitch Ambassador (an initiative that recognizes community leaders on the platform) with over 30,000 followers, TwitchCon isn’t a viable expense anymore. She attended the convention from 2018 through 2023, and was even featured in 2022 marketing materials.

“Do I stand to make more money attending a convention through business deals or meeting potential product vendors or catching up with sponsors? Or do I make more money just streaming instead? In the case of TwitchCon, unfortunately the latter has been true for some time,” she said.

Amira “Xmiramira” Virgil, feels similarly. She was in the first class of Twitch Ambassadors and attended TwitchCon in 2018 and 2019, but the ROI isn’t worth it for her anymore. “The brands I want to work with can be found at conventions and events that don’t cost me upwards of $3,000, and even if they do, there’s still a better ROI,” she said. “I’ve also heard some critique about what brands are attending as well.”

Frogan, a streamer with nearly 70,000 followers who prefers to go by her social media handle, said the event is “way too expensive” and that there are very few incentives for partnered creators.

Quin “CtrlAltQuin” Martin, who has over 10,000 followers on the platform, said stressed the cost. “Traveling to the other side of the country to create free content for Twitch just doesn’t make sense to me at this moment – though I love the platform,” they said.

Nearly everyone Digiday spoke with raised safety concerns. Urs said past TwitchCons felt like there were cameras on you at all times, noting the high volume of IRL creators streaming on the convention floor (this year, Twitch introduced lanyards that signal attendees’ comfort level with being on camera). O’Reilly mentioned the Emiru incident as a detractor for potential attendees.

“Accessibility can sometimes be an afterthought for events inside and outside the ‘con,” said Martin, who uses a mobility device. “Pair that with minimal security and folks who don’t understand boundaries, it can be a hot mess.”

Frogan attended her first TwitchCon in 2022, and said she requested security for 2023’s event after allegedly receiving death threats for her vocal support of Palestine. In 2024, she and several other streamers were temporarily banned from the platform after a Twitch-approved panel held on-stage during TwitchCon drew online backlash and accusations of antisemitism, which she vehemently denies.

“I don’t feel safe going to the event from both physical interactions and Twitch as a company in general,” Frogan said. “I feel like they have failed me as a creator multiple times so it’s not worth it for my peace of mind.”

“We’ve taken the concerns raised following last year’s TwitchCon very seriously,” said the Twitch spokesperson, adding ahead of this weekend’s event, the company has increased investment in security, added more trained safety, and strengthened escalation procedures for on-site teams.

“Our goal is to create an environment where everyone attending TwitchCon feels safe, heard and excited to be there, and we’ll continue listening to our community and making improvements.”

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has figured out how to maximize engagement around sports-betting content while staying brand safe.
