#7282. Netflix embraces 35mm film in international campaign

“Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” consists of three new spots shot in different aspect ratios and a social series.

As U.S. consumers continue to cut back on subscriptions, including for streaming services, Netflix’s latest campaign emphasizes the quality of its vast content library in order to help it stand out in a crowded field. “Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” comes as company executives continue to put an emphasis on value and engagement following price hikes and lower projected ad revenue.

Created by Netflix’s in-house shop the Brand Creative Studio and agency Mother, the campaign features three spots shot on 35mm film using different aspect ratios. In “Axolotl,” which is shot for widescreen, an axolotl leverages its quadrupedalism to escape its tank and turn the TV back on after its owners leave in the middle of a “Bridgerton” marathon. “Dawn” follows a vampire who braves the rapidly rising sun to find a working TV in order to watch “Love Is Blind.” In “Hypersleep,” an astronaut who is the only one awake while his fellow shipmates are in cryosleep, wakes someone up in order to show them the “Soda Pop” scene from “KPop Demon Hunters.”

The spots were directed by Vania Heymann and Gal Muggia of the production company Iconoclast. The nature of the ads allows for the featured program to be swapped out depending on the market. In Brazil, the show featured in “Dawn” is crime drama “Os Donos Do Jogo,” or “Rulers of Fortune” in English.

Over the course of four episodes, the social series “Typecast” will feature four creatives and showcase their somewhat unexpected Netflix fixations. The series kicks off with fashion designer Willy Chavarria and his show of choice “Love on the Spectrum.” Other guests include musician Teezo Touchdown, makeup artist Isamaya Ffrench and Randy Savvy of The Compton Cowboys. The videos were filmed in 16mm to fit the feel of the overall campaign.

The campaign’s use of analog film is part of a recent trend that has seen advertisers looking to run counter to the rise of artificial intelligence. Stop-motion animation has been of particular interest, with brands such as Levi’s and Prada embracing the artform. Netflix’s spots, which feature custom-built sets, follow a similar vein of appealing to the desire for human-made advertising.

Netflix grew revenue 12% year over year to $12.9 billion in Q3, per an earnings release. The company lowered its expected ad revenue for the year from $3 billion to $2.7 billion, a drop it attributed to a slower-than-expected rollout of some advertising capabilities.

#7278. Why brands like Anthropologie are commissioning creator-created shows instead of posts

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Pooja Tripathi created Brooklyn Coffee Shop, the comedy web series built around a barista with an opinion about every customer who walks through the door. The show won a Webby this year and its fan club in marketing circles keeps growing.

SharkNinja paid to work its Auto Barista machine into an episode, Anthropologie went a step further and hired Tripathi to write and lead its first micro comedy series — a holiday party caper she took from script to shoot. Even instagram has noticed. Tessa Lyons, its vp of product singled out the show as one her favorites while talking up the app’s renewed push into longer-form content.

All of which has turned Tripathi into a social showrunner of sorts, operating in a corner of the creator economy where more brands are starting to shop.

Take Anthropologie – the brand’s marketers were already fans of Tripathi’s work before they got in touch earlier this year.

“We engaged her because we’re fans of her work,” said Barbra Sainsurin, global CEO at Anthropologie Group. The admiration turned out to be mutual and Tripathi “leaped at the chance to create a series for us,” she said. Her comic voice was the draw for the brand, which wanted to try its hand at comedy even though “humour and entertainment haven’t been voices or characters that we’ve incorporated in our story telling traditionally,” said Sainsurin.

The result was the brand’s first micro comedy series “The Holiday Party,” a twist on the seasonal misadventure trope that sits inside its “On My Way” seasonal campaign, which went live last month. Each episode is 90 seconds and will air on the brand’s social channels between November 11–19 alongside behind the scenes footage.

Tripathi developed the characters and the direction of the show while Anthropologie’s team took charge of the styling, from what each character wears to how the party itself was decorated. The goal was a setting that felt true to Tripathi’s audience and also looked like “a party that an Anthro person would throw” said Sainsurin.

The brand took a few more pages from the entertainment playbook for the rollout. Anthropologie is “treating it like a movie launch,” with trailers, teasers and hooks at the end of each episode to pull viewers into the next. Instagram and TikTok are getting the series as are YouTube, Pinterest and Reddit, which means it will be “distributed more broadly than it has ever,” said Sainsurin.

Whether it goes further depends on the numbers. Sainsurin’s team is watching earned reach, how many non-followers the series lands in front of and “how far is this content travelling in the algorithm”. Saves, shares, comments, site visits, product page views and sales will come into the picture once the full spread of holiday content has run. That’s a big shift for a team that doesn’t even do much with creators now. They make up a smaller share of the media mix than at other brands, said Sainsurin.

Putting a price on that kind of work is its own puzzle. Hyphen, the Reign Maker Group-owned talent management firm that brokers series deals, splits the bill into the creator’s fee, production, usage rights, exclusivity and the number of episodes. On its own roster, a single sponsored post from a mid-tier lifestyle creator starts at around $8,000 to $10,000. A multi episode series lands “well into five figures,” and paid usage plus category exclusivity can push the total to six, said Victoria Bachan, CEO of Hyphen. Her team tells brands to weigh that against “ what it would cost to build that audience habit themselves,” she continued.

More brands are starting to have that conversation. They’re hiring social show runners like Tripathi, sometimes in place of part of their influencer marketing spend and sometimes alongside it. In doing so they’ve reopened one of the industry’s longest-running arguments: whether influencer marketing and creator marketing are different things at all.

Both pay someone with an audience to put a product in front of it but they arguably split from there. Influencer marketing leans on the person, whose recommendation and follower count do the heavy lifting on a post that lives and dies on their own account. Creator marketing, on the other hand, heads off toward the content itself. Think characters that viewers get attached to and a format they come back to week after week. All of that sits with the show itself so it holds value well after the influencer post has scrolled out of view.

Take the show We Are MICE, a mockumentary set at a fictional live events agency whose name stands for meetings, incentives, conferences and exhibitions. Beauty creator Genevieve Turley, in her first acting role, plays Maggie, who manages the agency’s team. She’s a 40-something parent who is nearly divorced and recently back at work, trying to pull off flawless events with a team that’s some way short of flawless itself. The clients are real as in they’re the brands that sponsor the series and the plot follows Maggie’s team as it tries to deliver an event for them. Laura Geller Beauty is the first, commissioning six episodes between 60 and 90 seconds that started rolling out across Instagram and TikTok last month (October 26).

The format comes from Rooscreen, the Royal Television Society-nominated production company behind comedy short film Mourning Glory, and was developed with brand partnerships strategists DASH Talent. Like plenty of sitcoms before it, MICE started life in a writer’s room, built around an idea Rooscreen founder Becky Rooney had been carrying around for a while. The characters and their world came first, in other words, and the brands were invited in after.

“Yes, it’s an advert and yes it’s a commercial but rather than a typical product placement or doing a standard influencer post we’ve tried to get into the storyline with the brand,” said Rooney.

“I wanted it to be funny and edgy but also brand safe,” she added.

The plan is for each new series to bring in a different brand and a different event while Maggie and her colleagues, who are also creators, carry their storylines from one to the next. Which leaves room for a different member of the cast to step forward each time, depending on whose audience best fits the brand. A sportswear deal, for instance, would hand the spotlight to Austyn Farrell, a creator and keen marathon runner who plays Mo on the show. “These creators understand their audiences so who better to guide how we advertise certain brands than the people who actually really understand that product,” said Rooney.

Early numbers appear to back that up, or at least as far as views and interactions can tell. As of earlier this week, the episodes had collectively hit 1.3 million views on Instagram with 11,000 interactions.

“Going viral is one thing but if that means reaching two million people who don’t want to buy the product then does that have as much value as reaching 20,000 people who actually do?,” said Rooney.

None of which means brands are turning their backs on creator scale. Anthropologie, for instance, has around 50 creators working on the same campaign. Some are returning partners. Others came through an agency to reach audiences the brand isn’t getting to yet. Similarly SharkNinja works with Tripathi as well as Alex Berry, John Connor Hammon and Ari Caga — the three creators of The ick vertical digital web series — while continuing to work with large groups of creators.

Long gone are the days when LinkedIn was just a job-searching site.

Mars readies for AI grocery agents by chasing LLM visibility and immersive stores as impulse buys shift. See what’s at stake.

TikTok Shop is offering the shopping service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers.

#7276. Carnival gamifies cruise experience via interactive Twitch livestream

Streamer iJustine will take on a series of challenges voted on by viewers as she explores a digital Carnival Celebration ship.

Carnival is turning to Twitch to give consumers a unique look at what a trip aboard one of its vessels would entail at a time when the demographics of the average cruise passenger are in flux. The activation comes as the company reported record-high bookings for 2027 and a net income of $1.9 billion in the third quarter, an all-time high for the company. However, gross margin yields fell 1.3%  due to high fueling costs, per an earnings release.

Cruising has become more popular than ever, with a record 22.4 million Americans expected to take a cruise in 2027, up 3.6% year over year, per AAA research. The pre-pandemic total for American cruise travelers was 14.2 million. While the vacation choice is stereotypically associated with older demographics, the average age of an American cruiser is 45.8, and millennials and Gen Z travelers are increasingly drawn to the travel method due to lower costs and value-oriented packages, according to AAA research.

The livestream will feature a custom-built game in which iJustine, who has nearly 55,000 followers on Twitch at press time, will tackle challenges on the simulated Carnival Celebration. The ship’s spaces and activities, as well as destinations from the brand’s Paradise Destinations collection, will be part of the gameplay. Along with input from the audience, Weigand will help keep energy high by introducing challenges, passing on requests from viewers and offering community instructions. iJustine, who will be playing live onboard, will work to earn her cruise director badge and a Fun Meter will track energy levels.The game is over once the meter hits zero.

To encourage participation, audience members will have the chance to win a cruise vacation. The full livestream will be posted to Instagram and TikTok the day after the event so fans can catch up on what they missed. The game will not be made available to the public after the stream.

“We’re constantly looking for new ways to push the boundaries on how we connect with consumers, and Play Carnival takes that thinking to a whole new level,” said Michael Sotelo, vice president of brand, product and social media at Carnival Cruise Line, in press materials.

The effort falls under the “Find Your Fun Again” campaign, which encourages consumers to ditch the stress and monotony of everyday life and find joy aboard a Carnival ship. Actor Nick Offerman took center stage in a spot in which he goes on vacation after smashing his phone due to receiving an overload of notifications.

Amazon-owned platform Twitch has become a go-to for brands looking to experiment with connecting with young consumers. Last year, E.l.f. Cosmetics became the first brand to test Twitch’s livestream shopping ads.

#7272. Maybelline updates ‘Maybe’ tagline as beauty perceptions evolve

Miley Cyrus is leading a new global brand platform promoting changes meant to be more inclusive and empowering for women.

Maybelline is reworking one of its signature brand assets as it tries to adapt to an evolving landscape for beauty and cosmetics. The L'Oréal-owned marketer’s “Maybe she’s born with it” tagline became a fixture of its advertising beginning in 1991, but consumers in 2026 are applying makeup for different reasons.

The company has attempted to modernize “Maybe” in the past. It was at one point dropped for the largely forgotten “Make It Happen” but eventually brought back. Maybelline revamped the “Maybe” tagline for the TikTok era two years ago. The latest overhaul appears more substantial, changing the underlying meaning of the copy, and is described in the release as permanent.

Over half of surveyed global respondents today see beauty as a way to build confidence compared to 26% who see it as a means of looking more attractive to other people or appearing younger for longer, per McKinsey findings cited by Maybelline. The suggestion is that makeup can be something less superficial and more empowering and inclusive.

Tweaks to the marketer’s tagline try to recognize these trends by adjusting the phrasing to sound less like a question being asked about a single woman’s look. Instead, the new “Maybe” is positioned as a declaration of the possibilities all women can achieve by leveraging makeup as a form of self-expression.

“With 'Maybe.', we're building on our legacy and inviting women everywhere, at every stage of life, to explore the possibilities within them,” said Maybelline Global Brand President Sandrine Jolly in a statement.

Maybelline enlisted Cyrus, an ambassador who’s previously voiced the “Maybe” tagline, to lead its new “The Beauty of Infinite Possibility” platform. Cyrus was selected for the effort because her own career has entailed a constantly shifting identity, moving from child star to hard-partying club song purveyor and then back to her country roots.

Cyrus released her latest album, “Bass Persuades,” in September, which could draw additional interest to the Maybelline partnership. “Bass Persuades” sees Cyrus again dabbling in the dance pop and electronic music genres.

Products featured in the campaign include Maybelline’s Serum Lipstick, Lash Sensational Sky High Mascara and Instant Eraser Concealer. Maybelline will partner with additional women in the worlds of music and elsewhere to share their stories of using makeup as a form of expression and reinvention.

Consumer products, the division of L'Oréal that Maybelline sits under, grew 4.3% on a like-for-like basis in Q3, lagging other segments like dermatological beauty and professional products.

#7269. LinkedIn brings its B2B ad targeting to U.K. connected TVs

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

In its latest effort to capitalize on growing demand for video, LinkedIn is expanding its CTV ads to the U.K.

The launch follows its staged U.S. rollout, which began in April 2024, before becoming widely available across the U.S. and Canada in 2025. It brings LinkedIn’s professional audience targeting to CTV, allowing advertisers to reach U.K. decision-makers by job role, industry and seniority while they watch ad-supported streaming content. For example, a software company looking to reach senior IT decision-makers could use LinkedIn’s targeting to serve CTV ads to viewers in relevant job roles and industries, rather than relying solely on broader demographic or household-level targeting.

So far, the platform has partnered with LG and Titan OS for its U.K. CTV ads offering, and it’s available to all advertisers in the market.

“When you think about professional identities and how you want to reach and engage with them, we’re a platform where people tell us who they are,” said Jae Oh, senior director of product management at LinkedIn. “They get verified for the jobs that they have, and we’re able to support a higher fidelity of reach when it comes to how you reach those audiences at scale.”

LinkedIn is positioning CTV as more than just an awareness play, arguing that its professional audience targeting can help advertisers reach decision-makers earlier in the buying process and influence them further down the funnel.

“For so long CTV has been seen as like you naturally think it’s an awareness play, but actually where we’ve seen a real success is when clients use it as part of a full-funnel strategy,” said Milka Privodanova, vice president of LinkedIn Marketing Solutions, large customers — international at LinkedIn.

The company claims that people exposed to a brand’s CTV ad cost 30% less to convert into sales leads. Cisco, which has already tested the targeting update, saw 52% incremental reach beyond its LinkedIn in-feed activity, 141% higher lead form completion rates and 35% higher click-through rates among audiences exposed to its CTV ads, according to its LinkedIn case study.

But the proposition comes with questions over cost and targeting accuracy. Accuracast, for example, evaluated LinkedIn’s CTV offering when it was only available in the U.S. and found its pricing significantly higher than other direct CTV providers, alongside high minimum spend requirements.

“There’s definitely a business case for it — our B2B clients would love to get more reach and eyeballs on connected TVs — but if we’re going to pay LinkedIn’s usual premium pricing, we’d want more clarity on how LinkedIn plans to accurately target their users on these devices,” said the agency’s group CEO Farhad Divecha.

The idea is that advertisers can use CTV to introduce their brand to decision-makers, retarget and nurture those audiences through video and other LinkedIn ad formats to encourage them to take action. The platform is also looking to improve how advertisers measure CTV’s contribution to business outcomes beyond clicks and lead form completions. Oh said LinkedIn is also investing in revenue and pipeline attribution, alongside incrementality testing, to help advertisers understand CTV’s contribution to business outcomes.

“You will see deeper investment when it comes to incrementality testing or experimentation to prove that these different tactics you’re employing work for you,” Oh said.

The U.K. CTV expansion gives LinkedIn another way to tap into the demand for video, by  extending its video offering beyond the platform and onto the TV screen.

Video advertising on LinkedIn is growing 25% year-over-year, according to Privodanova, though she declined to share how much CTV contributes to that growth.

LinkedIn has been stitching together the pieces of its CTV business since 2024 when it lined up Roku and Samsung Ads as supply partners and launch LinkedIn Premier, a managed product built on NBCUniversal’s streaming inventory, A deal for premium Paramount inventory followed in mid-2025, along with integrations with Innovid and Sprinkly meant to make CTV campaigns easier to run.

“As a premium advertising platform, LinkedIn will need to demonstrate that its audience data can translate into meaningful reach and measurable impact,” said Andy Hunt, head of paid media at agency Brave Bison. “Targeting professionals through household devices presents its own challenges around signal quality, accuracy and attribution. Advertisers will want to understand how effectively CTV exposure can be connected to wider campaign performance, rather than simply adding another awareness metric.”

Brands go from booking creators to green-lighting shows.

Mars readies for AI grocery agents by chasing LLM visibility and immersive stores as impulse buys shift. See what’s at stake.

TikTok Shop is offering the shopping service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers.

#7268. Fetch trades views for rewards with brand-backed holiday microseries

“The Receipt” features brands like Dove, Breyer’s and Olipop across six episodes and offers consumers points for viewing that can be redeemed for gift cards.

Fetch is angling to become a more integral part of the holiday shopping experience with what it bills as the industry’s first rewarded romantic comedy. The effort leverages an in-app rewarded video format launched by Fetch in August alongside WPP Media, which helped bring the tool to market, and Unilever, which served as a launch partner. In early tests of the format, Fetch observed a 96% video completion rate, per details shared in the launch announcement.

Privately-owned Fetch operates by allowing consumers to upload receipts from everyday purchases to earn points that can be exchanged for gift cards. The company sees over 13 million receipts submitted each day and has awarded over $1 billion in points to date, per release details. Over the holidays, Fetch expects consumers to redeem over $100 million in rewards, said founder and CEO Wes Schroll in a press statement.

“The Receipt” follows the story of Chloe, played by Condor, after a meet-cute with Max, played by De La Torre — Condor’s real-life husband — results in a tote bag swap. To reconnect with Max, Chloe relies on shopping receipts found in his bag and the help of best friend Liam, played by Sedeño, a creator with nearly 1.5 million followers on TikTok. The series, created with director Michael Hoy, also features creators from Fetch’s Creator Club ambassador program.

New episodes of the series will launch every three days until Dec. 18 on Fetch’s Instagram, YouTube and TikTok channels. Each episode will feature a single brand partner, with participants including Olipop, Breyers, Dove, health food brand Kize and CPG manufacturing company Nehemiah, a close partner of Procter & Gamble. General Mills brands Betty Crocker and Blue Buffalo will be featured together in one episode. The featured brands will each be tied into a storyline in the series, pushing past traditional product placement in a move that could drive stronger brand recall among viewers.

Within the Fetch app, consumers can watch the series trailer, which launches Nov. 20, and episodes to earn points that can be redeemed for gift cards. Users can also earn points by shopping the episodes within the in-app Fetch Shop, or complete Fetch Play gaming challenges to unlock bonus content.

The global cashback apps market was valued at $6.12 billion last year and is expected to grow to nearly $12 billion by 2034 as consumers attempt to make their dollars stretch further. Average household spending over the 2026 holiday season is expected to reach $1,654, a 4% increase over the year-ago period, according to Deloitte.

The latest effort from Fetch taps into the growing trend around branded microdramas and episodic content. Others that have leveraged a similar strategy recently include L’Oréal’s Urban Decay, Marc Jacobs and JCPenney.

#7265. How Unilever’s social-first marketing is evolving amid reorganization

Leandro Barreto will be promoted to chief growth and marketing officer on Jan. 1, a move that’s in line with how other marketing leaders are adding responsibility for the P&L.

After seeing strong sales and volume growth last quarter, Unilever upgraded its outlook for the rest of 2026 and now expects at least 3% underlying volume growth for the full year. But the CPG giant is not standing still as it looks to build on that growth: At an investor event last week, the company announced plans to roll out a reorganized operating model in 2027 that will be centered around a “global strategy, local execution” principle.

Unilever’s operations will be split into six global units, with leadership responsible for, among other things, managing the growing convergence of content, media and commerce. In addition, Leandro Barreto, currently chief marketing officer of Unilever and its beauty and wellbeing business, will be appointed chief growth and marketing officer on Jan. 1, a move that’s in line with how other marketing leaders are adding responsibility for the P&L.

The reorganization arrives as Unilever continues to evolve into a pure-play home and personal care company following the separation of its ice cream business last year and the planned spinoff of its food business as part of a $44.8 billion deal with McCormick. The latter hit an antitrust snag in the U.K. this week that Unilever described as an “expected step” in the process.

Moving forward, Unilever plans to focus on four pillars when operating its brands: increasing the role of wellness, developing authority systems, executing flawlessly and remaining committed to a marketing philosophy it implemented last year called SASSY, which is an acronym for superior science, aesthetics, sensorials, shared by others and young spirited principles.

The “shared by others” part of SASSY includes the company’s work to shift half of digital ad spend to social-first and creator-driven marketing. Unilever reported that it now relies on 500,000 content creators, up from the previously reported 300,000 and significantly more than the 20,000 utilized before CEO Fernando Fernandez joined the company.

“We have embraced social-first marketing at a very significant speed, and this is showing in the way we are growing our key brands,” Fernandez said at Unilever’s investor event on Nov. 4. “Some of our brands have been really rejuvenated… Have we done it in all our portfolio? No. We have great examples, and we have brands in which we are starting to replicate this model.”

Currently, authority systems in a content-overloaded, artificial intelligence-powered world mediated by agentic shopping and LLMs represent a gap in Unilever’s strategy and an area where some competitors have outperformed the company.

“The algorithms have to follow a logic, and the logic they follow is geographical proximity, independent recommendation, clinical proof, patents published, ratings and review. This plays in favour of great brands with great science, with great budgets,” Fernandez explained.

AI is affecting Unilever’s business in a variety of ways, including shifting the innovation process from primarily being a physical discipline to a digital one. The company can now develop consumer signals into validated concepts in hours instead of months.

“We now can see very clearly how the signals are operating, and we can, with synthetic audiences, not only spot more than 60,000 consumer signals in a continuous way, but we also can sharpen those ideas before even real consumers see them,” Barreto said during the investor event. “The innovation also is leveraging way more data points, more than 25 million of them. That's why we can match the formulation with the concept, and we can have predictions on performance and consumer appeal.”

Unilever’s social-first marketing model involves four areas at scale: recommendation, content, influence and media. To solve for recommendation that is mediated by LLMs, Unilever said it is structuring its data to be machine-readable, working with partners like Google and optimizing by tracking more than one million prompts across more than 30 markets. In the last six months, Unilever has improved in more than 90% of the brand market sales positions that it tracks.

The company is using an AI-powered engine to meet the scale and speed of its content needs, and reports that it is producing twelve times more content than in 2024 at a 90% lower cost. In some cases, purchase intent and brand recall have increased, due in part to training AI on each brand’s DNA and core proposition. AI is also helping Unilever find, brief, vet and measure creators whose content yields ROI that is 1.5-times higher than brand-led content.

The proliferation of AI is also one of the factors reducing the lifespan of video content to four days. After that time, content on platforms like Meta, Google, TikTok, Amazon and Walmart has negative — not simply diminishing — levels of return, making marketers like Unilever rethink how it approaches content.

“The level of engagement that you provide to the platform fundamentally changes the price you pay. This is a fundamental change and makes development of content such an important capability in a company these days,” Fernandez said.

Unilever said it has dramatically reduced the cost per unit of content and improved its return on investment between 10% and 15% per year. However, that ROI is not expected to return to the company’s P&L or bottom line, but serve as a cost of doing business in a digital media environment.

“In the past, a company like Unilever could gain a significant competitive advantage by purchasing large amounts of media from TV Globo in Brazil. Today, however, media is being purchased through Meta, Google, Amazon, Walmart and TikTok, and that model no longer favors scale of purchase. Instead, it favors the quality of engagement you generate on the platform,” Fernandez said. “I believe improving our return on investment is fundamental to keeping our business model working, continuing to drive top-line growth and delivering modest margin expansion every year.”

#7263. Media Briefing: The IAB finds AI measurement standards elusive for publishers amid platform data gaps

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at how the IAB’s months-long effort to standardize AI attribution ran into a roadblock: the lack of visibility into what happens inside AI platforms.

The Interactive Advertising Bureau spent months trying to determine how the industry should attribute and credit conversions influenced by AI. But it ran into a problem: you can’t standardize what you can’t see.

Digiday first reported in August that the IAB was developing an AI attribution framework to standardize measuring and crediting AI’s role in conversions, and the signals to quantify AI’s influence on purchase decisions. The IAB’s working group included tech companies, publishers, agencies, measurement vendors and brands — including People Inc., Walmart, WPP Media, Emberos, Criteo, Bluefish, Microsoft, Brandlight, Tinuiti and Prudential Financial. The resulting “AI-Era Attribution Blueprint,” was published today.

The blueprint addresses some of the questions behind the effort, but it also underscores how much remains unmeasurable. Recommendations, consumer decisions and even transactions may happen inside AI platforms, but that data isn’t yet accessible to advertisers or measurement providers.

“Going in, I thought that we were going to help figure out how credit was assigned through the process. I realized actually what this document was starting to become was more of a call to action to the industry,” Caroline Giegerich, the IAB’s vp of AI said during a virtual IAB roundtable on Monday. “There are evidence layers that are missing. We’ve started to have conversations on that, but there’s still room to go. But without remarking on what’s missing, what we want to see, and AI’s influence in the purchase funnel, I don’t think we’re going to get there. So it kind of started in one place, and over the course of four months, ended in another.”

As Digiday previously reported, the measurement gap has prompted a growing number of startups to pursue solutions.

The IAB’s blueprint offers a framework for understanding AI’s role in driving business outcomes, while acknowledging that data gaps make its influence difficult to reliably measure and attribute. It also underscores a broader challenge for the industry: as publishers expand their GEO offerings to brands, measurement remains a major obstacle.

But publishers are experimenting with ways to address parts of the measurement gap. Time is working with Mobian to measure the performance of the ads it serves to AI agents, for example, and Bustle Digital Group is working with Profound to measure its GEO campaign performance for brands. Those efforts offer visibility into specific activity, but they don’t yet solve the broader challenges of tying AI’s influence to sales.

AI search makes it more difficult for brands to measure how LLMs influence consumer purchases, because impressions, clicks and referral data aren’t always available when an AI assistant guides consumers from product discovery to purchase decisions. And even when an AI recommendation does lead to a sale, those conversions can be incorrectly attributed to search, direct traffic, or other channels, according to the IAB’s report.

That attribution gap can distort marketing investment decisions, with spend going to channels that capture conversions rather than those that influenced them. Similarweb data cited in IAB’s report found consumers who received a brand recommendation from an AI assistant are 2.5 times more likely to visit that brand’s website within seven days, but only 8.8% of those visits come directly from AI referrals. Instead, 55.9% are attributed to search and 19.9% to direct traffic.

The IAB framework focuses on three areas. It classifies who or what created the intent, made the decision, and executed the transaction. It defines additional claims AI involvement creates, and the evidence each one requires. And it highlights the transparency and measurement gaps that make those claims difficult to verify. (More detail is available in the framework).

The clearest case of this gap is when publisher content is behind an AI recommendation, the IAB noted in its report. If a consumer asks an AI answer engine for the best option in a category, and the answer draws on a publisher’s review, the consumer may not click the citation (a link to the publisher’s review), and it gets reported as a search or direct sale by the retailer.

“The review shaped the sale, and the publisher is eligible for a contributory claim. The evidence differs depending on how publisher content informed the answer. Content used in model training is generally not traceable from outside the model. Grounding and citation are more observable: publishers may be able to see when their content is cited or reflected in an AI response, although citation alone does not establish how much that content influenced the answer. What remains largely missing is the connection between those sourcing signals and downstream conversions. Until that link exists, the contributory claim cannot be fully substantiated,” the IAB report reads.

The IAB’s recommendations span AI platforms, brands, agencies and publishers. Platforms should disclose whether recommendations are paid or organic, which sources inform them and label AI-originated traffic consistently.

Brands and agencies should apply the framework’s claim-and-evidence standards across platforms, while publishers should identify claims about their content’s influence and the evidence needed to support them, per the report.

“Publishers are feeding the answers but they don’t have the receipts,” said Jaime Schultheis, head of global data partnerships at Bombora, where she leads a network of B2B publishers, brand sites and data providers.

The IAB framework does accomplish defining the parts of the AI measurement journey, which can help publishers compare these between vendors and their different measurement structures, Jon Roberts, People Inc.’s chief innovation officer, said during the roundtable.

“Everyone’s pitching you on their custom proprietary measurement solution, and they’re all using the same terms, and the terms all mean something different because they’ve all designed these in a silo and are racing to get your business, and they’re all unique,” he said.

There are some signs that the measurement gap may narrow soon. The U.K.’s Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) is requiring Google to provide U.K. publishers with clickthrough data for its AI search features by Dec. 3. OpenAI is also testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers and how many clicks those appearances send to its site.

“We’re waiting [with] bated breath,” said Graham Howes, svp of distribution and AI intelligence at BDG, adding that this data from OpenAI and Google would be incredibly valuable for its GEO offering.

During the roundtable, Roberts noted that without this data, publishers are effectively “flying blind.” Sharing analytics benefits both publishers and AI platforms by helping them identify business opportunities, improve products and better serve their users, he said. Third-party measurement would still be important for independently verifying platforms’ own data, Roberts added.

Brands want this measurement gap solved just as much as publishers. Ross Bucholc, associate media director at Prudential, said in the roundtable that the lack of first-party data from AI platforms is holding back his ability to unlock more ad budgets going to LLMs.

Even with better visibility into AI activity, connecting that activity to sales remains difficult. Josh Blyska, head of insights at Profound, told Digiday that existing attribution tools struggle to connect AI search’s influence on consumer purchase decisions to conversions. Platforms like Profound can measure AI visibility, citations and how brands appear in AI answers, but can’t reliably track whether those interactions lead to purchases.

In a recent analysis of browser-session data, Profound found one in three consumers had a relevant AI conversation about their purchase within a month that went unattributed, Blyska said. (The dataset didn’t include Google’s AI Mode or AI Overviews.)

“It’s a really tricky problem right now,” he said.

Ultimately, the challenge is not just measuring a brand’s visibility within AI platforms but proving that visibility drives business results. John Sills, head of sales, North America at The Guardian, said the industry needs a more consistent way to understand how AI patterns surface and answer questions.

“We’re not measuring CTRs here. There’s no way of really understanding where the attribution is coming from,” he said. “These LLMs are in the ad business now… In the same way you can call your account manager at Facebook and try to optimize a social campaign or on YouTube, we need that level of account management to help guide the best practices.”

“We are capable, and AI companies are capable of reaching deals that give us fair compensation for content and referring to our site. Ninety-five percent of the AI referrals we get now come through OpenAI. So to me, that’s a win for them and a win for us if that’s how people want to access the news. So when people say deals like that aren’t possible or we don’t deserve compensation, I don’t really buy it.”

— Matt Murray, Washington Post executive editor, speaking about its deal with OpenAI, at Web Summit in Lisbon, Portugal.

$330 million: The reported price under consideration for The New York Times Company’s potential acquisition of Letterboxd, an online community for film enthusiasts.

2.7 billion: The number of times Ziff Davis’ content was cited in Google’s AI Overviews and AI Mode in Q3, nearly 10 times the clicks from Google Search those sites received.

37%: Ziff Davis’ YOY decline in Google referrals.

44%: BuzzFeed’s total YOY revenue decline in Q3 2026.

#7255. Yahoo becomes first licensee of ‘Spotify-like’ collective AI content licensing model

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

AI content licensing has largely comprised of one-off deals between publishers and tech companies. RSL, which is being renamed The Internet Licensing Collective (ILC), is pursuing a collective model instead, with Yahoo as its first licensee.

The deal means publishers of all sizes can license their content for use in Yahoo’s AI-powered search engine Scout and get paid based on how that content is used.

Digiday spoke with ILC co-founder Doug Leeds about how this model works and how publishers can earn revenue.

A licensee like Yahoo pays a blanket licensing fee for access to content in the collective. Leeds declined to say how much Yahoo was paying, but the fee is tied to the “demand-side revenue.”  Yahoo declined to be interviewed for this story.

As a licensee’s business scales, the amount available for publisher compensation can grow as well.

“It allows businesses that are making a lot of money off of the content to keep that content in place and sustainable. But it also allows for entrants,” Leeds said. “As the revenue scales, the percentage scales. So more money comes out. But that also means that entrants can come in, and there can be new use cases and new players and new products, and all of that’s important to the evolution of what we’re doing to the market.”

A new report out today from publishing analytics firm Mather Economics estimated that collective licensing for AI could generate publisher compensation equal to 15% to 35% of AI company revenue, a potential market that in three years could surpass the U.S. display advertising market. The analysis was based on the current annual revenue from the two biggest frontier models, OpenAI and Anthropic, and an economic model created by Mather Economics incorporating AI training-data scaling laws.

Leeds likened the licensing collective to a Spotify-like model.

“It’s kind of like Spotify, but also a song is a song is a song, and content is very different. And so we had to come up with these rules that made sense specifically for digital content,” Leeds said.

Leeds said ILC worked closely with its publisher collective to figure out those rules. The first step was to define a “segmented royalty pool.” This separates licensing revenue by AI company, rather than combining payments into a single fund shared by all participating publishers. That means publishers only receive a share of the revenue generated by AI products that actually use their content.

More AI and tech companies are testing pay-per-value licensing models. Digiday has previously reported that Google and Microsoft are testing and developing these models, which compensate publishers when their content meaningfully contributes to AI-generated responses, rather than paying based on how frequently their content is used. It’s a shift from flat-fee licensing deals to more dynamic pricing, based on the value publishers’ content delivers to AI companies.

When an AI company signs a licensing agreement with ILC, that money goes into its own royalty pool. Rather than combining all these payments into one enormous fund and distributing them across participating publishers, ILC keeps the royalty pools separate.

This protects specialized publishers from having their revenue diluted, Leeds explained. A general-purpose AI search engine like Yahoo Scout might use large quantities of content from hundreds or thousands of publishers, but a specialized healthcare AI product might use a much smaller amount of content from a handful of medical-focused publishers, and that specialized content could be considerably more valuable to the healthcare company, he said.

ILC wanted to ensure that a publisher’s compensation reflects the value of the AI product using its content, not simply how much content it contributes to the overall licensing ecosystem.

“An AI vertical in health might be very low volume, but much higher specific value. So if your content gets used in that, you should split that high value, not get diluted by all the content that got used by Yahoo,” Leeds said.

Over 3,000 publishers are now part of the ILC, he noted.

The payments are determined by what Leeds called “allocation rules.” These take into account how much content is used, like grounding, retrieval or displayed in an AI response, and whether the publisher is credited or cited as a source.

For example, an AI product that uses a publisher’s content to check a fact might receive a lower allocation than one that displays detailed election results and explicitly credits the Associated Press, Leeds said. He did not disclose the exact formula used to calculate each publisher’s share.

The allocation rules are intended to discourage AI companies from choosing content simply because it’s cheaper, and instead incentivize them to use what they consider the highest-quality sources, he said.

Publishers can also decide who they want to license to. Right now, their only option is Yahoo. But as more licensees sign up, they can go into their ILC portal and select which AI companies they do and don’t want to license their content to. For example, if a publisher signs a separate content licensing deal with an AI company, they can take their content out of the collective. In the portal, they can also see what their content is being used for, such as RAG or training.

RSL, which stood for the Really Simple Licensing (RSL), was two things: a group of publishers working on a collective licensing agreement, and the organization behind the RSL Protocol, a standard that publishers can use in robots.txt to describe in machine-readable language what their terms of service are. Publishers can ask AI companies for a pay-per-crawl or pay-per-inference fee through the RSL Protocol, for example. “Thousands” are currently using this standard, Leeds said.

But Leeds wanted a new name for the non-profit organization that reflected the publisher collective, to make it clear that this is a licensing model that any publisher can join, he said.

Leeds said ILC is currently in talks with “some” of the major AI companies interested in the licensing collective. He declined to name which ones.

“You can do a deal with a one-off, and that’s fantastic. But that doesn’t invest in the infrastructure of content in general,” he said.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

Ahead of TwitchCon 2026, brands and creators weigh in on if the platform’s big IRL event is worth the cost and potential safety issues.

#7254. Sociable: Meta’s AI ads included unapproved discounts

Recent reports found that the artificial intelligence-powered ad tools posted discount offers to customers even with promotions turned off.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

As artificial intelligence tools become more empowered, new parameters need to be established to limit their capacity when it comes to undertaking certain tasks.

There have already been reports of an AI tool giving out a user’s home address, generating hundreds of videos at a users’ expense and conducting questionable crypto trades.

Automated errors can also extend to buying digital ads. PPC Land reported last week that Meta’s AI-powered ad tools took discounts from a store’s website and displayed them on the business’s Facebook and Instagram ads, “even when the shop had turned promotions off on the ad it copied.”

The discovery was made by digital marketing advisor Bram Van der Hallen, who shared a LinkedIn post outlining the discovery.

As per Van der Hallen: “Meta may now automatically detect offers from your website and use them in your Meta ads. Even when you have turned off this option for your original ad. When the option to display offers detected from your website and ads is enabled in Commerce Manager, Meta may then automatically display these offers in your ads.”

Van der Hallen said the issue can be controlled by updating Commerce Manager settings to stop Meta from using detected offers in other campaigns.

But this adds another element to consider, and PPC Land noted that it could cause issues for advertisers: “This matters for any business advertising on Meta with more than one offer live, because a discount meant for one campaign or one audience could show up somewhere else.”

Meta recently outlined how its expanded processing capacity is helping generate more effective ad campaigns. The company said that improvements to its ads ranking system drove a more than 8% increase in ad clicks and a 15.7% uplift in conversions on Facebook in Q2 2026.

However, as more systems are guided by AI, there will be errors because AI systems seek out efficiencies and optimizations wherever they can, which could cause issues.

Advertisers need to be aware of these potential issues, which could have a big impact on campaign approaches.

#7252. Why user-first ad experiences build the strongest app businesses

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Today’s mobile users are searching, streaming, scrolling and shopping in bursts as they go about their day. According to RealityMine, the average smartphone user interacts with around 18 different apps per day, racking up to 200 app sessions daily.¹ In each of those moments, attention is won second by second — and lost just as quickly.

Capturing value in those seconds is remarkably difficult. When audience acquisition is expensive, and engagement is just as hard to earn, app studios cannot afford ad experiences that cause churn.

In-app ads are a core part of the overall app experience, yet monetization and user retention are still too often treated as competing priorities. The assumption is that earning more revenue requires heavier ad loads, which damages the user experience, while protecting that experience means leaving money on the table.

“Disruptive ads have a severe, compounding negative impact on both current engagement and future user acquisition,” said Scott Sheffer, vp of global partnerships on Google’s sell-side monetization team. “Disruptive ads not only drive current users away but also prompt negative reviews, discouraging future users from downloading the app. Seems intuitive, but now there is data to back that.”

According to a Deloitte and Google AdMob study, a single encounter with a disruptive ad increases a user’s likelihood to churn by 6% to 7%.² When those experiences repeat, 52% of gamers say they leave the app for good.³ On the flip side, high-quality, user-first ad experiences actively protect engagement: 72% of gamers keep playing when ads are high quality⁴ and 20% report playing more often.⁵ These metrics demonstrate how ad quality serves as a fundamental growth engine beyond a cosmetic choice.

“App economics are not an either-or,” Sheffer said. “With quality ad experiences, users can stay engaged longer and you can naturally unlock more opportunities for other revenue streams, like in-app purchases and subscriptions.”

Sustainable revenue growth doesn’t come from aggressive ad strategies that disrupt audiences. It comes from pairing global, premium demand with uncompromising quality controls. This foundation protects in-app experiences and grows long-term revenue opportunities.

To truly scale revenue, safety cannot be restricted to a single network. Google built its advanced ad quality protections to extend across the AdMob network and participating third-party bidders. This means studios can filter out unwanted categories, block problematic creatives and apply brand rules across their bidding partners from a single interface.

Toss, a financial app company from South Korea’s Viva Republica, chose AdMob as its mediation partner to grow revenue through quality ad experiences. By using AdMob’s real-time ad review and rigorous A/B testing capabilities, Toss grew ad revenue 891% year over year while driving a 17% uplift in next-day retention for one of its app’s features.⁶

Across the wider app ecosystem, SensorTower data shows that apps primarily using AdMob’s mediation platform have a 25% higher 60-day and 90-day user retention rate than apps primarily using competing platforms.⁷˒⁸

“Our focus on quality and long-term growth explains why, in recent research, Kantar found that app developers rank Google AdMob No. 1 in both providing high-quality ads and in maximizing revenue — ahead of other leading app monetization platforms,” said Sheffer.⁹˒¹⁰

As app development cycles continue to accelerate, monetization operations must keep pace.

Instead of losing critical hours to manual creative review, app developer teams can lean on Google’s intelligent brand safety systems to help. For example, improved AI models in the Ad Review Center can swiftly flag off-brand creatives for removal. In parallel, click-through-rate-based filtering helps to block deceptive ad behaviors like forced redirects, fake buttons or hidden close icons. This tactic helps developers eliminate these formats across all bidding sources before they trigger player frustration.

For ads that need a closer look, revamped search filters in the Ad Review Center help teams find creatives faster — such as sorting creatives by revenue to prioritize the reviews that impact the bottom line.

By protecting both developer velocity and creative safety, teams can more easily grow revenue and focus on the user experience. That balance of automation and control is why a Kantar analysis showed app developers rate Google AdMob 1.5x higher than the competitor average for making it easy to monetize apps.¹¹

Delivering on ad quality and making monetization simple is only the start. Google is building new tools to help teams catch performance anomalies, implement and validate ad placements and preserve player retention.

“We believe enterprise-grade AI and monetization requires secure, native integration across the tools you already use today,” Sheffer said. “That is why Google AdMob is continuously expanding what an advanced monetization platform can do. By shifting routine diagnostics, complex yield optimizations and brand safety into intelligent, automated workflows, we are bridging the gap between app development and ad operations — so you can build, test and ship ad setups with the same speed and safety as your app code.”

Partner insights from Google Publisher Connections

Publisher Connections brings together publishers, buyers and their audiences. Across a suite of trusted platforms — from Google Ad Manager, AdMob and AdSense, to their buyer solutions — they deliver continuous innovation and powerful results.

Yahoo is the first licensee of the Internet Licensing Collective, offering publishers a new way to get paid for content used in AI search.

The IAB’s new AI attribution framework promises to standardize measurement, but a lack of platform data leaves much of AI’s impact invisible.

#7249. Welch’s holiday microdrama embraces Y2K nostalgia as Gen Z become parents

The five-episode series features the actress who voiced Kim in “Kim Possible” and a member of The Cheetah Girls group.

Welch’s is jumping on the microdrama trend as consumers head into the holiday season. “Before the Toast” features many of the trappings typically associated with the format — like absurdly high stakes and plenty of drama — and a nostalgia-fueled cast to promote the brand’s line of sparkling juices during a time of year when demand is usually high.

Joining Romano in the series is her husband, Brendan Rooney, Sabrina Bryan of The Cheetah Girls and Heather Morris, from “Glee.” Storylines largely revolve around navigating holiday season chaos with young children and creating new memories.

The actors chosen for the campaign, in addition to its strategic placement around family-friend holiday programming, may help the brand reach families with younger children. The demand for sparkling juices has gone up as consumers look for alternatives to soda and alcoholic beverages. This season also sees the introduction of Welch’s Sparkling Low Sugar Grape, which is available at Walmart.

The cast may also be nostalgic for Gen Z parents who grew up on Disney Channel shows, many of whom are sharing the media with their own children.

As part of the campaign, the brand teamed up with Instacart to give away full-size bottles of its products in the lead-up to Thanksgiving and Christmas. Shoppers are able to claim the offer via the product card on the Instacart app.

Microdramas are being embraced by brands looking to reach consumers. The format has proven popular with Gen Z, who have grown used to watching short, vertical videos on their phones. The scale of efforts have varied, from just a few episodes to P&G’s 50-part series for Native. Welch’s isn’t the first beverage brand to jump on the microdrama bandwagon. In May, Dr Pepper promoted the return of its Creamy Coconut product with a four-episode series in which a young woman has a fleeting love affair with a can of the dirty soda-inspired flavor.

Welch’s, which operates as an agricultural cooperative and does not release earnings statements, replaced its CMO position with a chief brand and innovation officer in October of last year. Andrew Hartshorn, who previously held roles at PepsiCo and Danone, assumed the role. Shortly after he arrived, the brand introduced a new kid mascot, marking the first time in 25 years that Welch’s creative starred a child character.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

A strong foundation in A/B testing mechanics — comfortable formulating clear copy hypotheses, understanding sample size requirements, and knowing when a test result has achieved true statistical significance.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

#7238. SunnyD and Juicy Juice Hand Their Comeback to Goodby Silverstein & Partners

Everything that’s old is new again, and that includes SunnyD, the orange drink with the ‘90s tagline: “The good stuff kids go for.”

#7217. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

#7213. Talkspace Recruits Michael Phelps for Men’s Mental Health Awareness Month

Talkspace, a virtual behavioral healthcare provider, brought in former Olympic athlete Michael Phelps to discuss mental health tips for men.

The “Rewriting the Rules” campaign launches amid Men’s Mental Health Awareness Month.

Phelps, who is an ambassador for Talkspace, pulled from his own mental health journey to create a list of habits that can promote healthier mindsets. Some of his tips include making time for activities one enjoys, being vulnerable with close ones, and asking for help – regardless of a mental health crisis.

The aim of the campaign was to “rewrite” narratives surrounding masculinity that may prevent men from seeking mental health guidance through therapy. While men are often led to believe that stoicism and “dealing with it” are the ways of manliness, Phelps and Talkspace point out the strength it takes to be honest about feelings.

The platform offers therapy to all identity groups, but took this month as an opportunity to highlight barriers faced by men. The “Rewriting the Rules” campaign includes testimonies from other men who saw a change in their mental health after seeking therapy, and who experienced the toxic masculine stereotypes.

“We built the campaign around real member stories that showcase the success men have had using Talkspace. Rather than telling men why they should try therapy, we’re letting other men show them what it actually looks like and what it can do.” said Katelyn Watson, chief marketing officer, Talkspace. “When someone engages with the campaign, we want them to see people who look like them, who’ve faced the same kinds of everyday issues, and who’ve come out the other side feeling better. That sense of recognition makes the idea of getting support feel more relatable, more approachable, and ultimately more achievable.”

The “Rewriting the Rules” campaign is accessible on Talkspace’s site, on its “Men’s Mental Health Awareness” page.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

Agentic workflow experience — you've designed and deployed AI agents that act on GTM data in production.

Strong change management practices — you've shipped changes to live systems while protecting active deals and campaigns.

Experience influencing senior stakeholders — translating business strategy into system requirements and roadmaps.

A history of mentoring engineers, from early-career to senior.

Salesforce CPQ: approvals, order forms, discounting rules, and the closed-won-to-billing path.

SQL and data modeling fluency; familiarity with a warehouse-centric stack (BigQuery, Snowflake, dbt, reverse ETL).

Experience with billing platforms (Stripe, Zuora, Maxio) and the handoffs between GTM systems and billing.

Experience managing a GTM tooling budget or vendor portfolio.

Experience as a technical lead on a GTM or business systems team.

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

#7207. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it
